For MHD with CT this is incomplete, since eqs. 57 to 60 do not keep the divergence zero: the update of the interface states must change, and a step of CT must advance the field from $t^n$ to $t^{n+1}$.

Two modifications serve this purpose (§4.1). First, CT requires the fluxes at the centres of the faces to be integrated to the corners of cells (§4.1.1). With $f_x(B_y) = -\mathcal{E}_z$ and $f_y(B_x) = \mathcal{E}_z$, the face values $\mathcal{E}^*_{z,i,j+1/2} = \hat{\mathbf{e}}_{B_x}\cdot\mathbf{F}^*_{y,i,j+1/2}$ and $\mathcal{E}^*_{z,i+1/2,j} = -\hat{\mathbf{e}}_{B_y}\cdot\mathbf{F}^*_{x,i+1/2,j}$ and a corner value $\mathcal{E}^*_{z,i+1/2,j+1/2}$ give fluxes integrated to the corner from either side, such as $\mathbf{F}^{L*}_{y,i+1/2,j+1/2} = \mathbf{F}^*_{y,i,j+1/2} + (\mathcal{E}^*_{z,i+1/2,j+1/2} - \mathcal{E}^*_{z,i,j+1/2})\hat{\mathbf{e}}_{B_x}$ and $\mathbf{F}^{L*}_{x,i+1/2,j+1/2} = \mathbf{F}^*_{x,i+1/2,j} - (\mathcal{E}^*_{z,i+1/2,j+1/2} - \mathcal{E}^*_{z,i+1/2,j})\hat{\mathbf{e}}_{B_y}$, with $L$ and $R$ for the sides below and above, or left and right of, the corner (eqs. 65 to 68). In effect the normal components of the field on the faces advance to $t^{n+1/2}$ by an integral of CT, $B^{n+1/2}_{x,i\pm1/2,j} = B^n_{x,i\pm1/2,j} + \tfrac{1}{2}(\delta t/\delta y)(\mathcal{E}^*_{z,i\pm1/2,j-1/2} - \mathcal{E}^*_{z,i\pm1/2,j+1/2})$ and $B^{n+1/2}_{y,i,j\pm1/2} = B^n_{y,i,j\pm1/2} - \tfrac{1}{2}(\delta t/\delta x)(\mathcal{E}^*_{z,i-1/2,j\pm1/2} - \mathcal{E}^*_{z,i+1/2,j\pm1/2})$ (eqs. 69, 70). The fluxes $\mathbf{F}^*$ are those of the single-step scheme of 2.6.2, so that $\mathcal{E}^\alpha_z$, $\mathcal{E}^\circ_z$ or $\mathcal{E}^c_z$ can give the corner field, and the tests use $\mathcal{E}^c_z$. The field then satisfies $\nabla \cdot \mathbf{B} = 0$ at $t^{n+1/2}$. The interface states advance as $\mathbf{q}^L_{i+1/2,j} = \mathbf{q}^{L*}_{i+1/2,j} - \tfrac{1}{2}(\delta t/\delta y)(\mathbf{F}^{L*}_{y,i+1/2,j+1/2} - \mathbf{F}^{L*}_{y,i+1/2,j-1/2}) + \tfrac{1}{2}\delta t\,\mathbf{S}_{x,i,j}$, the right state with $\mathbf{F}^{R*}_y$ and $\mathbf{S}_{x,i+1,j}$, and the states on faces normal to $y$ with the fluxes along $x$ and $\mathbf{S}_{y,i,j}$ or $\mathbf{S}_{y,i,j+1}$ (eqs. 61 to 64).

Second, without source terms the updated states would not advance by $\delta t/2$ for MHD, because the interface states of 2.6.2 are computed in primitive variables, so that $\mathbf{q}^{L*}_{i+1/2,j}$ differs from $\mathbf{q}(x_i + \delta x/2, y_j) - (\delta t/2)\,\partial\mathbf{f}_x/\partial x$ (§4.1.2, eq. 71). The source $\mathbf{S}_{x,i,j}$ adds $\mathbf{B}\,\partial B_x/\partial x$ to the momentum, $v_z\,\partial B_x/\partial x$ to $B_z$ and $B_z v_z\,\partial B_x/\partial x$ to the energy, all at $t^n$ and with $\partial B_x/\partial x = (B^n_{x,i+1/2,j} - B^n_{x,i-1/2,j})/\delta x$, and $\mathbf{S}_{y,i,j}$ does the same with $\partial B_y/\partial y$ (eqs. 72, 73). Placing the term $v_z\,\partial B_x/\partial x$ for $B_z$, with its source of energy, here rather than in the interface states of 2.6.2 prevents a spurious growth of $B_z$. A field loop in the plane, advected with a uniform $\mathbf{v} = v_z\hat{\mathbf{z}}$ and $B_z = 0$ initially, has nonzero $\partial B_x/\partial x$ and $\partial B_y/\partial y$ almost everywhere, but $B_z$ must stay zero. With the term in the interface states, these would carry a coherent $B_z$, which the transverse update diminishes without cancelling, so that an unphysical, coherent $B_z$ would grow and eventually affect the dynamics in the plane. With the sources as chosen, $B_z$ stays at the level of round-off and without structure, since the terms in $\partial B_x/\partial x$ and $\partial B_y/\partial y$ remain balanced in both predictor and corrector.

To evolve the field by CT from $t^n$ to $t^{n+1}$, the algorithm for the corner fields must still reduce to the integrator for planar flows aligned with the grid, which requires the gradients of the electric field at the half step (§4.2). Eq. 46 becomes $(\partial\mathcal{E}_z/\partial x)^{n+1/2}_{i+1/4,j} = (2/\delta x)(\mathcal{E}^{n+1/2}_{z,i+1/2,j} - \mathcal{E}^{n+1/2}_{z,i,j})$ (eq. 74), with the reference field $\mathcal{E}^{n+1/2}_{z,i,j} = v^{n+1/2}_{y,i,j}B^{n+1/2}_{x,i,j} - v^{n+1/2}_{x,i,j}B^{n+1/2}_{y,i,j}$ advanced by $\delta t/2$ (eq. 75). Its cell-centred field is the mean of the normal fields of the interface states at the half step (eqs. 76, 77), and its density and momenta advance by $\delta t/2$ with the fluxes of the first step, $\mathbf{q}^{n+1/2}_{i,j} = \mathbf{q}^n_{i,j} + \tfrac{1}{2}(\delta t/\delta x)(\mathbf{F}^*_{x,i-1/2,j} - \mathbf{F}^*_{x,i+1/2,j}) + \tfrac{1}{2}(\delta t/\delta y)(\mathbf{F}^*_{y,i,j-1/2} - \mathbf{F}^*_{y,i,j+1/2})$ (eq. 78). In summary, CTU with CT computes the interface states by PPM with the multidimensional source terms of 2.6.2, their fluxes by a Riemann solver and the corner fields by $\mathcal{E}^c_z$ (§4.3). It advances the states by eqs. 61 to 64, solves the Riemann problems again, computes the corner fields at the half step, and advances the face fields from $t^n$ to $t^{n+1}$ by eqs. 14 and 15 and the volume averages by eq. 9. The algorithm is of second order, unsplit, and keeps the divergence zero throughout the step.

[Stone et al.](#stone-et-al-2008) (2008, §5.1) list the integrator of Athena in eleven steps (Figure 4). The states on both sides of all faces along $x$ and $y$ are computed and stored at once by a reconstruction of 2.4, and Riemann solvers give the fluxes $\mathbf{f}^*_{i-1/2,j}$ and $\mathbf{g}^*_{i,j-1/2}$, with the longitudinal field of each face as a parameter (eqs. 60, 61). The corner fields $\mathcal{E}^*_{z,i-1/2,j-1/2}$ follow as in 2.3.5, with the reference field $\mathcal{E}^{r,n}_{z,i,j} = -(v^n_{x,i,j}B^n_{y,i,j} - v^n_{y,i,j}B^n_{x,i,j})$ at $t^n$. Each state advances by $\delta t/2$ with the transverse flux difference: the density, momentum, energy and $B_z$ of the left state at $x_{i-1/2}$ as $\mathbf{q}^{n+1/2}_{L,i-1/2,j} = \mathbf{q}_{L,i-1/2,j} - (\delta t/2\delta y)(\mathbf{g}^*_{i-1,j+1/2} - \mathbf{g}^*_{i-1,j-1/2}) + (\delta t/2)\,\mathbf{s}_{x,i-1,j}$, and the right state with the fluxes and sources of cell $i$ (eqs. 62, 63). The components of the states must be matched to those of the transverse fluxes, which are ordered differently in the two directions. As these updates are split by direction and conservative, the source vector $\mathbf{s}_x$ adds $\mathbf{B}_{i,j}\,\delta B_x/\delta x$ to the momentum, $(B_z v_z)_{i,j}\,\delta B_x/\delta x$ to the energy and $v_{z,i,j}\,\delta B_x/\delta x$ to $B_z$, with $\delta B_x = B_{x,i+1/2,j} - B_{x,i-1/2,j}$ (eq. 64), and terms in $\delta B_y/\delta y$ serve on faces normal to $y$. The face fields advance to the half step by CT with these corner fields (eqs. 65, 66). A reference field at $t^{n+1/2}$ follows from the velocities of a conservative update of mass and momentum with $\mathbf{f}^*$ and $\mathbf{g}^*$ and from the averages of the face fields at the half step. New fluxes $\mathbf{f}^{n+1/2}$ and $\mathbf{g}^{n+1/2}$ between the advanced states, with the advanced face fields as parameters and the H-correction where needed (2.5.5), give the corner fields at the half step (eqs. 67, 68). Density, momentum, energy and $B_z$ then advance by the finite volumes of eq. 11 and $B_x$ and $B_y$ by CT (eqs. 16, 17), the cell-centred field follows from eqs. 26 and 27, and the next step is $\delta t = C_\circ\min[\delta x/(|v^{n+1}_x| + C^{n+1}_{fx}), \delta y/(|v^{n+1}_y| + C^{n+1}_{fy})]$ over all cells, again an estimate (eq. 69). In the reconstruction of the first step, source terms enter the primitive variables for the transverse field alone (§5.2). The left state at $x_{i-1/2}$ changes by $\delta B_{y,L,i-1/2,j} = (\delta t/2\delta x)\,v_{y,i-1,j}(B_{x,i-1/2,j} - B_{x,i-3/2,j})$ and that at $y_{j-1/2}$ by $\delta B_{x,L,i,j-1/2} = (\delta t/2\delta y)\,v_{x,i,j-1}(B_{y,i,j-1/2} - B_{y,i,j-3/2})$, with similar terms for the right states, added after the reconstruction and before the conversion to conserved variables. **[GK]** Eqs. 62 and 63 add the transverse flux difference with a plus sign. A conservative update subtracts the divergence of the flux, as eqs. 57, 58, 61 and 62 of [Gardiner and Stone](#gardiner-stone-2005) (2005) do, so the sign must be minus.

#### 2.6.4 CTU in three dimensions

[Gardiner and Stone](#gardiner-stone-2008) (2008) extend the single-step method of second order of [Gardiner and Stone](#gardiner-stone-2005) (2005) to three dimensions, combining CTU for the multidimensional integration with CT for the constraint on the field. Its interface states of PPM must contain multidimensional MHD source terms that respect the balance implied by $\nabla \cdot \mathbf{B} = 0$, and two forms of CTU, which solve the Riemann problem 6 or 12 times per cell and step, are compared, the first in detail (abstract). The method of 2005 rested on three ingredients: a PPM reconstruction with the multidimensional terms of MHD, fluxes of the area-averaged field on the edges built from the face fluxes of the volume-averaged field by the relation between the two kinds of average, and the unsplit CTU (§1; 2.3, 2.6.2, 2.6.3). Tests with field loops showed the new corner fields to be essential for stability and better than earlier Godunov methods with CT ([Balsara and Spicer](#balsara-spicer-1999) 1999), and CTU gave less dissipation and the exact reduction to one dimension for planar flows aligned with the grid. As CT needs no costly elliptic solutions, Godunov schemes with CT should be more cost-effective than divergence cleaning ([Crockett et al.](#crockett-et-al-2005) 2005; [Tóth](#toth-2000) 2000). Splitting would make the step to three dimensions trivial, but the field stays free of divergence across partial updates only if its three components are updated together, which violates the independence of the split operators. Even for hydrodynamics CTU is not trivial in three dimensions ([Saltzman](#saltzman-1994) 1994), and for MHD two ingredients must change: the reconstruction must include the multidimensional terms so that a balance law that follows from $\nabla \cdot \mathbf{B} = 0$ is respected, and CTU must supplement the transverse flux gradients with source terms. A field loop in the plane $(x, y)$ advected with constant velocity and $v_z \ne 0$ remains a challenging test: $B_z$ evolves unless the MHD source terms stay balanced in both the characteristic tracing and the transverse update. The test defines the stencil on which the constraint must hold: if $\nabla \cdot \mathbf{B} = 0$ holds on a stencil other than that of the fluxes of $B_z$, $B_z$ evolves unphysically in a conservative scheme, and a method that keeps $B_z$ constant to round-off keeps the constraint on the right stencil. The test thus shows again that the constraint must hold exactly, as [Brackbill and Barnes](#brackbill-barnes-1980) (1980) stressed, and it joins the tests of 2.16.

The interface states of PPM evolve the linearised system in primitive variables in one dimension, but MHD needs multidimensional terms, and the algorithm in three dimensions must reduce to those in two and one dimensions in the appropriate limits (§3). In primitive variables only the induction equation (eqs. 7 to 9) contains terms in $\partial B_x/\partial x$, $\partial B_y/\partial y$ and $\partial B_z/\partial z$, the MHD source terms. In two dimensions the constraint removed them from the equation for $B_z$, which keeps their balance exactly (§3.1, eqs. 10 to 12; 2.6.2). Without this, the state on a face normal to $x$ would gain a spurious evolution of $B_z$ through $v_z\,\partial B_x/\partial x$ (eq. 13) for a field loop with $B_z = 0$, constant $\mathbf{v}$ with $v_z \ne 0$ and $\beta = 2P/B^2 \gg 1$. Experience shows that the transverse update does not remove this error, which leads to a secular growth of $B_z$ that warps the loop. In three dimensions each component of the gradient of the electric field gains part of the transverse source term, limited, grouped as the splitting will separate them (§3.2, eqs. 14 to 16). For the equation for $B_z$, for instance, $v_z L_{zx}(\partial B_y/\partial y)$ joins the derivative along $x$ and $v_z L_{zy}(\partial B_x/\partial x)$ that along $y$, and the induction equation is recovered if $L_{xy}(\partial B_z/\partial z) = -L_{xz}(\partial B_y/\partial y)$ and so on (eq. 17). Split along $x$, $B_x$ stays constant and $B_y$ and $B_z$ evolve with $\partial_x(v_x B_y - B_x v_y) - v_y L_{yx}(\partial B_z/\partial z)$ and $\partial_x(v_x B_z - B_x v_z) - v_z L_{zx}(\partial B_y/\partial y)$ (eqs. 18 to 20). Minimising the size of the summed source terms gives $L_{zx}(\partial B_y/\partial y) = \mathrm{minmod}(-\partial B_x/\partial x, \partial B_y/\partial y)$, with $\mathrm{minmod}(x, y) = \mathrm{sign}(x)\min(|x|, |y|)$ for $xy > 0$ and zero otherwise, and the other limiters follow by cyclic permutation and eq. 17 (eqs. 21, 22). Physically, if $\partial B_x/\partial x$ and $\partial B_y/\partial y$ have opposite signs, their balance is built in by adding and subtracting the smaller of the two, so that the reduced source term belongs to one flux gradient only, and if they have the same sign there is no balance and the equation stays unmodified. With the properties of minmod and the constraint, the split system becomes $\partial B_x/\partial t = 0$, $\partial B_y/\partial t + \partial_x(v_x B_y) - B_x\,\partial v_y/\partial x - v_y\,\mathrm{minmod}(\partial B_x/\partial x, -\partial B_y/\partial y) = 0$ and $\partial B_z/\partial t + \partial_x(v_x B_z) - B_x\,\partial v_z/\partial x - v_z\,\mathrm{minmod}(\partial B_x/\partial x, -\partial B_z/\partial z) = 0$ (eqs. 23 to 25). The limited terms use the gradients of the cell averages, for eq. 25 in cell $(i, j, k)$ $\mathrm{minmod}[(B_{x,i+1/2,j,k} - B_{x,i-1/2,j,k})/\delta x, (B_{z,i,j,k-1/2} - B_{z,i,j,k+1/2})/\delta z]$ (eq. 26), and the states on the other faces follow by cyclic permutation. For $\partial/\partial y = 0$ or $\partial/\partial z = 0$ the algorithm reduces to that of two dimensions, for $\partial/\partial x = 0$ the state on a face normal to $x$ equals the cell state, and the field loop keeps $B_z = 0$.

MHD differs from the Euler equations in two ways that matter for CTU (§5). Splitting the equations in primitive and in conservative variables gives incompatible systems, so that the transverse flux corrections need source terms, as the interface states of PPM use the primitive form (2.6.3). The multidimensional source terms of the interface states of §3 also call for source terms in the transverse updates of the transverse field on the faces. Both stem from the balance of flux gradients in several directions that $\nabla \cdot \mathbf{B} = 0$ imposes. The CTU of [Saltzman](#saltzman-1994) (1994) in three dimensions solves the Riemann problem 12 times per cell and step (§5.1). It computes the interface states of PPM, from each of them two states advanced by $\delta t/3$ with one transverse flux gradient each and their fluxes, then states advanced by $\delta t/2$ with the transverse flux gradients built from these fluxes and their fluxes, and finally the conservative update. As written, this does not serve MHD: its intermediate steps are partial updates of the split conservative equations, which ignore the balance between flux gradients in different directions. The parallel gradients enter the interface states in split primitive form, the transverse ones in conservative form, and the two forms do not match, so that some source terms are lost, the scheme is formally of first order, and the field perpendicular to the loop of the test evolves secularly. With two predictor steps the correction is more involved. In the first, each normal component of the field on a face splits into two, each evolved by half a loop of CT, so that the divergence vanishes only in their mean. In the test of the loop, the two states on a face normal to $z$ then evolve $B_z$ equally and oppositely through $v_z\,\partial B_x/\partial x$ and $v_z\,\partial B_y/\partial y$, a failure of the balance. The balanced split system of eqs. 14 to 16 must therefore replace the conservative induction equation in both predictor steps. The two normal components need a common source term of opposite signs, which keeps the divergence free in the mean, and momentum and energy need source terms that balance terms such as $B_x\,\partial B_x/\partial x$ and $\mathbf{B}\cdot\mathbf{v}\,\partial B_x/\partial x$. The resulting 12-solve CTU, with partial updates in a non-conservative form chosen with care, is optimally stable for Courant numbers up to one, but complicated. It was implemented and tested (2.16), and its complexity motivates a simpler variant.

For the Euler equations the 6-solve variant is the 12-solve algorithm without its first predictor, with the fluxes of the interface states of PPM in the transverse update, or equally a formal extension of CTU in two dimensions that adds the flux gradients along and across the face to the interface states in two steps (§5.2). For MHD it takes six steps. First, it computes the interface states of PPM with the source terms of §3.2 and their fluxes (eqs. 46 to 48). Second, the CT algorithm of 2.3.5 gives the electric fields on all edges from these fluxes, with a reference field at $t^n$. Third, the states advance by $\delta t/2$ with the transverse flux gradients. The mass, momentum and energy of the left state at $x_{i+1/2}$ change by $-(\delta t/2\delta y)(\mathbf{F}^*_{y,i,j+1/2,k} - \mathbf{F}^*_{y,i,j-1/2,k}) - (\delta t/2\delta z)(\mathbf{F}^*_{z,i,j,k+1/2} - \mathbf{F}^*_{z,i,j,k-1/2}) + (\delta t/2)\,\mathbf{S}_{x,i,j,k}$, and those of the right state with the fluxes and sources of cell $i+1$ (eqs. 49, 50). The source of the momentum is $\mathbf{B}\,\partial B_x/\partial x$, and that of the energy $(B_y v_y)\,\mathrm{minmod}(-\partial B_z/\partial z, \partial B_x/\partial x) + (B_z v_z)\,\mathrm{minmod}(-\partial B_y/\partial y, \partial B_x/\partial x)$ (eqs. 51, 52). The field evolves with the electric fields of CT instead of the predictor fluxes: its normal component on the face by the loop of Stokes, $B^{n+1/2}_{x,i+1/2,j,k} = B^n_{x,i+1/2,j,k} - (\delta t/2\delta y)(\mathcal{E}^*_{z,i+1/2,j+1/2,k} - \mathcal{E}^*_{z,i+1/2,j-1/2,k}) + (\delta t/2\delta z)(\mathcal{E}^*_{y,i+1/2,j,k+1/2} - \mathcal{E}^*_{y,i+1/2,j,k-1/2})$ (eq. 53). Its transverse components change by the difference of $\mathcal{E}^*_x$ along $z$ for $B_y$, or along $y$ for $B_z$, averaged over the two edges of the cell, with the signs of the curl, plus $(\delta t/2)\,v_y\,\mathrm{minmod}(-\partial B_z/\partial z, \partial B_x/\partial x)$ for $B_y$ and $(\delta t/2)\,v_z\,\mathrm{minmod}(-\partial B_y/\partial y, \partial B_x/\partial x)$ for $B_z$ (eqs. 54 to 59). The source terms of the transverse field come from the split induction equation of §3.2, those of momentum and energy from the primitive form of the interface states, and the other faces follow by cyclic permutation. Fourth, Riemann solvers give the fluxes between the advanced states (eq. 60). Fifth, CT gives the electric fields at the half step from these fluxes, with a reference field from cell-centred fields averaged from the face fields at $t^{n+1/2}$ and from mass and momentum updated conservatively with the predictor fluxes. Sixth, finite volumes advance mass, momentum and energy from $t^n$ to $t^{n+1}$ and CT the normal fields on the faces. The algorithm is of second order and, unlike the 12-solve one, needs no source terms for the normal components of the field. It reduces exactly to CTU in two dimensions and to PPM in one dimension for flows aligned with the grid of the relevant symmetry, and it is well balanced, keeping $B_z = 0$ exactly in the test of the loop. It is, however, stable only for Courant numbers below $1/2$ in experiments, which the halved number of Riemann solutions per step compensates: two steps of the 6-solve method at a Courant number of $1/2$ cost about as much as one step of the 12-solve method at one.

[Gardiner and Stone](#gardiner-stone-2008) (2008, §7) conclude that the 6-solve CTU with CT extends and generalises the algorithm of two dimensions naturally, and they outline the 12-solve variant with test results. Both are accurate and robust at about the same cost, and the 6-solve algorithm is preferred for its simplicity and smaller use of memory. The interface states of PPM in three dimensions are a new and essential element, reduce exactly to those of two dimensions in the planar limit aligned with the grid, and satisfy a multidimensional balance law of the MHD source terms, whose violation makes the field evolve erroneously and secularly, as for a loop at high $\beta$. The tests compare with published results and add quantitative measures of accuracy, and the difference between the $L_1$ errors of the two algorithms for smooth waves stems mainly from their Courant numbers (2.16). The integrators have passed many further tests, among them problems of scientific interest such as the magnetorotational instability ([Balbus and Hawley](#balbus-hawley-1998) 1998) and the Rayleigh–Taylor instability in MHD ([Stone and Gardiner](#stone-gardiner-2007) 2007), and their combination with static and adaptive refinement of the mesh was left to a later paper.

In Athena, [Stone et al.](#stone-et-al-2008) (2008, §6) describe CTU in three dimensions as quite complex: stability up to a Courant number of one needs 12 Riemann solutions per cell and step and several fractional steps for the transverse corrections ([Saltzman](#saltzman-1994) 1994). [Gardiner and Stone](#gardiner-stone-2008) (2008) found that the 6-solve variant, formally stable only for $C_\circ \le 0.5$, matches the accuracy of the 12-solve one at about the same cost and is far simpler, so it is the main integrator of Athena in three dimensions. It reduces exactly to CTU in two dimensions or to the algorithm in one dimension for flows aligned with the grid. A column of field loops in the plane $(x, y)$ with $B_z = 0$, advected with a constant velocity in three dimensions, reduces the induction equation for $B_z$ to an advection plus a term proportional to $\nabla \cdot \mathbf{B}$, so that a scheme that keeps $B_z = 0$ exactly keeps the right discrete divergence zero (2.16). The algorithm takes twelve steps. The states on the faces along $x$, $y$ and $z$ are reconstructed at once by a method of 2.4, with source terms of $\nabla \cdot \mathbf{B}$ in the primitive variables, and Riemann solvers give the fluxes $\mathbf{f}^*$, $\mathbf{g}^*$ and $\mathbf{h}^*$ with the longitudinal fields as parameters (eqs. 71 to 73). CT gives the electric fields on the edges as in 2.3.5, with the reference field $\mathcal{E}^{r,n}_{i,j,k} = -(\mathbf{v}^n_{i,j,k} \times \mathbf{B}^n_{i,j,k})$, and advances the face fields by $\delta t/2$ (eqs. 16 to 18). The states advance by $\delta t/2$ with the transverse flux gradients; the hydrodynamic variables take the source vector $\mathbf{s}_x = (0, \mathbf{s}^M, s^E, 0, 0)$, with the momentum source $\mathbf{B}\,\partial B_x/\partial x$ and the energy source of GS08 eq. 52 (eqs. 76 to 79). The minmod limiter is needed because two transverse terms arise in three dimensions instead of one. The transverse fields on the faces advance with the electric fields and the limited sources of GS08 eqs. 54 to 59 (eqs. 80, 81). A reference field at $t^{n+1/2}$ follows from a conservative update of density and momentum and the averages of the face fields, the advanced states give new fluxes, with the H-correction where needed (eqs. 82 to 84; 2.5.5), and CT the electric fields at the half step. Finite volumes (eq. 11) and CT (eqs. 16 to 18) advance the solution to $t^{n+1}$, eqs. 26 to 28 give the cell-centred field, and the next step is $\delta t = C_\circ\min[\delta x/(|v_x| + C_{fx}), \delta y/(|v_y| + C_{fy}), \delta z/(|v_z| + C_{fz})]$ over all cells with $C_\circ \le 1/2$ (eq. 85). As in two dimensions, the reconstruction adds source terms only to the transverse field, after the reconstruction and before the conversion to conserved variables (§6.2, eqs. 86, 87). **[GK]** Eqs. 76 and 77 take the transverse fluxes of the left state at $x_{i-1/2}$ from cell $i$ and those of the right state from cell $i+1$, while their own source terms, eqs. 80 and 81, and eqs. 62 and 63 in two dimensions use cells $i-1$ and $i$, the cells from which the two states come. Eqs. 86 and 87 repeat the limited sources of the transverse update, eqs. 80 and 81, where the interface states need those of GS08 eqs. 24 and 25. For the transverse field the two limited sources, $\mathrm{minmod}(\partial B_x/\partial x, -\partial B_y/\partial y)$ in the states and $\mathrm{minmod}(-\partial B_z/\partial z, \partial B_x/\partial x)$ in the update, add up to the full $\partial B_x/\partial x$ when $\nabla \cdot \mathbf{B} = 0$. With the printed form the states would not reduce to those of §5.2 when $\partial/\partial z = 0$, and the field loop would gain $B_z$.

#### 2.6.5 The van Leer and Runge–Kutta integrators

Athena++ integrates in time by the method of lines ([Stone et al.](#stone-et-al-2020) 2020, §3.2.3). The reconstruction and the Riemann solver estimate the divergence of the fluxes at one time, and an integrator of the resulting system of ordinary differential equations completes a scheme of formal accuracy $O(\Delta x^n, \Delta t^m)$. The integrators must be unsplit, so that the constraint on the field holds at every substep. Athena offers the predictor-corrector VL2 of second order of [Stone and Gardiner](#stone-gardiner-2009) (2009) and CTU ([Colella](#colella-1990) 1990), but the characteristic projection of CTU is hard to use for relativistic flows, so Athena++ offers VL2 together with Runge–Kutta methods (RK) that preserve strong stability (SSP), store little, or both. It adopts the class 2S of low-storage methods of [Ketcheson](#ketcheson-2010) (2010) in the form of his Algorithm 3, with indices counted from zero and the index of $\delta$ shifted by one. With two registers, $\mathbf{u}^{(0)} = \mathbf{u}^n$ and $\mathbf{u}^{(1)} = 0$ at the start of each cycle, the latter set explicitly to clear old data, each stage $s = 0, \ldots, N-1$ performs $\mathbf{u}^{(1)} \leftarrow \mathbf{u}^{(1)} + \delta_s\mathbf{u}^{(0)}$ and $\mathbf{u}^{(0)} \leftarrow \gamma_{s0}\mathbf{u}^{(0)} + \gamma_{s1}\mathbf{u}^{(1)} + \beta_{s,s-1}\Delta t\,\mathbf{F}(\mathbf{u}^{(0)})$, and $\mathbf{u}^{n+1}$ is $\mathbf{u}^{(0)}$ after the last stage (eq. 11). Always $\delta_0 = 1$ and $\gamma_{01} = 1$, as the first stage is a forward Euler step from the data of the previous cycle. The integrators listed have $\delta_i = 0$ for $i > 0$, but the form also admits others, for instance RK4()4[2S] of [Ketcheson](#ketcheson-2010) (2010, Table 2), which is not SSP, and it extends to the three registers of the 3S* methods useful at high order ([Felker and Stone](#felker-stone-2018) 2018). RK1 is the forward Euler method, with $\gamma_0 = \{0, 1\}$ and $\beta_{0,-1} = 1$ (eq. 12). VL2, the default, is a predictor-corrector of midpoint type, whose predictor always uses the diffusive fluxes of first order of donor cell, with $\gamma_0 = \gamma_1 = \{0, 1\}$, $\beta_{0,-1} = 1/2$ and $\beta_{1,0} = 1$ (eq. 13). RK2, also called SSPRK(2,2) or the method of Heun, is the explicit two-stage SSP method of second order that is optimal in its error bounds ([Gottlieb, Ketcheson and Shu](#gottlieb-et-al-2009) 2009, eq. 3.1), with $\gamma_1 = \{1/2, 1/2\}$ and $\beta_{1,0} = 1/2$ (eq. 14). RK3, or SSPRK(3,3), is the optimal three-stage method of third order (eq. 3.2), with $\gamma_1 = \{1/4, 3/4\}$, $\gamma_2 = \{2/3, 1/3\}$, $\beta_{1,0} = 1/4$ and $\beta_{2,1} = 2/3$ (eq. 15). RK2 and RK3 have the SSP coefficient $c = 1$, so their limit $C_0 = 1$ equals that of RK1, which in practice is stable only with the fluxes of donor cell. With fluxes of higher order their stability is hard to prove, but $C_0 = 1$ seems to work with PLM and PPM for most problems. VL2 is stable up to $C_0 = 1$ in one dimension and $C_0 = 1/2$ in two and three, and positive for $C_0 \le 1/3$ with fluxes of first order in both steps ([Stone and Gardiner](#stone-gardiner-2009) 2009). The most useful combinations are RK1 with donor cell for tests, VL2 or RK2 with PLM or PPM, and RK3 with PPM.

[Beckwith and Stone](#beckwith-stone-2011) (2011, §3.5) list the steps of the VL+CT of [Stone and Gardiner](#stone-gardiner-2009) (2009) in its extension to relativistic MHD, which thus also documents the integrator of Athena. The primitive variables follow from the conserved ones at the cell centres, and a Riemann solver gives upwind fluxes of first order between the cell states (eq. 54), with the conserved entropy that the relativistic fallbacks of 2.14 use. CT gives the electric fields on the edges with the reference field $\mathcal{E}_i = -\epsilon_{ijk}B_k V_j$ at $t^n$, and fluxes and fields are checked to be real and stored. The hydrodynamic variables advance by $\delta t/2$ with the flux differences along all three directions and the face fields by CT. The cell-centred field at the half step is the average of the face fields, and the primitive variables at the half step are recovered and checked, with fallbacks in cells where they are unphysical. PLM reconstructs the states at the half step, with states of first order where the reconstructed velocity would exceed that of light. Riemann solvers give the fluxes at the half step, with the longitudinal field of each face as parameter (eq. 55), and the stored fluxes of first order replace any that are not real. A reference field at $t^{n+1/2}$ and CT give the electric fields at the half step. The full step then updates the hydrodynamic variables with these fluxes and the face fields by CT, and the primitive variables at $t^{n+1}$ are recovered, again with the fallbacks of 2.14.

[White, Stone and Gammie](#white-et-al-2016) (2016, §3.7) describe the same integrator of van Leer of second order in time for all quantities. From the conserved variables at step $n$ follow the primitive variables, the solutions of the Riemann problems and their fluxes, and the source terms. Fluxes and sources advance all conserved variables, the field included, by half a step, and the same procedure at step $n + 1/2$ advances step $n$ to step $n + 1$. Schematically, $C^{n+1/2} = C^n + (\Delta t/2)S^n + (\Delta t/2\Delta V)\sum_{\mathrm{faces}} F^n\Delta A$ and $C^{n+1} = C^n + \Delta t\,S^{n+1/2} + (\Delta t/\Delta V)\sum_{\mathrm{faces}} F^{n+1/2}\Delta A$ (eqs. 33a, 33b). The integrator of van Leer is TVD and introduces no spurious extrema. Athena++ also has the TVD methods of Runge–Kutta of second and third order of [Shu and Osher](#shu-osher-1988) (1988). RK2, the method of Heun, proved no better than VL2 and needs reconstructions of second order in both substeps, whereas VL2 converges at second order with a reconstruction of first order in its first half step. RK3 and methods of higher order need extra storage, and second order in time suffices there. CTU with CT ([Gardiner and Stone](#gardiner-stone-2005) 2005, [2008](#gardiner-stone-2008)) needs estimates advanced in time that are hard to generalise to arbitrary coordinates.

In Athena++ the parameter `integrator` of the block `<time>` selects `vl2`, `rk1`, `rk2`, `rk3`, `rk4` or `ssprk5_4` (`src/task_list/time_integrator.cpp`, `TimeIntegratorTaskList::TimeIntegratorTaskList`). The constructor sets for each stage the weights $\delta$, $\gamma_1$, $\gamma_2$, $\gamma_3$ and $\beta$, with $\gamma_3$ for a third register, and the fractions of the step at which the stage begins and ends. A stage first adds $\delta$ times $\mathbf{u}$ to the register $\mathbf{u}_1$, then forms $\gamma_1\mathbf{u} + \gamma_2\mathbf{u}_1 + \gamma_3\mathbf{u}_2$ (`src/mesh/weighted_ave.cpp`, `MeshBlock::WeightedAve`) and adds $\beta\,\Delta t$ times the divergence of the fluxes (`TimeIntegratorTaskList::IntegrateHydro`; 3.5). The first stage of VL2 computes its fluxes with donor cell, every other stage with the reconstruction of 2.4 (`TimeIntegratorTaskList::CalculateHydroFlux`). The upper limits of the Courant number are 1 for VL2 in one dimension and 1/2 in two and three, 1 for RK1, RK2 and RK3, 1.3925 for `rk4` and 1.508 for `ssprk5_4`, and a larger `cfl_number` is lowered to the limit with a warning. `rk4` is RK4()4[2S] of [Ketcheson](#ketcheson-2010) (2010, Table 2), a method without SSP, with a stability similar to that of classical RK4 and a limit set by fluxes of first order. `ssprk5_4` is the optimal SSPRK(5,4) of [Gottlieb, Ketcheson and Shu](#gottlieb-et-al-2009) (2009, §3.1), whose SSP coefficient of 1.508 relates its limit to that of RK1, although PLM and PPM without active limiters are unstable under RK1, so that the guarantees of SSP do not hold for these discretisations. Neither of the two works with shearing periodic boundaries, nor with orbital advection together with mesh refinement, and with orbital advection all integrators gain stages for its splitting (2.9).

`Hydro::CalculateFluxes` reconstructs the states along each direction for each line of cells, in MHD over one ghost cell in the transverse directions as well, calls the Riemann solver with the widths of the cells, and stores the fluxes and, in MHD, the electric fields and weights of 2.3.5 (`src/hydro/calculate_fluxes.cpp`). It adds the fluxes of diffusion unless super time-stepping treats them (2.11), and the method of fourth order adds corrections to the fluxes (2.7). `Hydro::AddFluxDivergence` applies the integral form: it subtracts from each cell the weight times the sum over the directions of the differences of area times flux between its two faces, divided by its volume, with areas and volumes from the coordinates (`src/hydro/add_flux_divergence.cpp`; 2.8). `Hydro::NewBlockTimeStep` divides in each cell and direction the physical width of the cell by $|v| + c$, or by a speed of the radiation or of the cosmic rays if that is larger (`src/hydro/new_blockdt.cpp`). Here $c$ is the fast speed in MHD, computed with the normal field $B_x + |B_{x,i-1/2} - B_x|$, which the face value can only raise, and the sound speed in hydrodynamics. The minimum over the block times `cfl_number` gives the hyperbolic limit, which orbital advection may lower. Viscosity, conduction, Ohmic and ambipolar diffusion and the diffusion of scalars give a parabolic limit, also times `cfl_number`, the Hall effect joins the hyperbolic one, and a user function may set a further limit. The step of the block is the smallest of these limits, without the parabolic one under super time-stepping (2.11). `Mesh::NewTimeStep` takes the minimum over all blocks and processes, starting from twice the previous step, so that the step at most doubles from one cycle to the next (`src/mesh/mesh.cpp`). It shortens the step so as to end at `tlim`, and under super time-stepping it caps the ratio of the step to the parabolic limit at `sts_max_dt_ratio`, if set. For a fixed fluid the step is `tlim` divided by `nlim`.

### 2.7 The fourth-order method

#### 2.7.1 Finite volumes of fourth order

[Felker and Stone](#felker-stone-2018) (2018) present a finite-volume method of fourth order for ideal MHD that couples quadrature rules of high order in semi-discrete conservation laws to UCT, the upwind form of constrained transport of [Londrillo and Del Zanna](#londrillo-del-zanna-2004) (2004), which keeps the field free of divergence (abstract). A new form of UCT reconstructs the field at the corners of cells with PPM in two dimensions, so that the scheme extends the CT of second order of Athena (2.7.3). Tests of hydrodynamics validate the base algorithm, and multidimensional tests of MHD show convergence at fourth order for smooth problems, robustness at discontinuities and better accuracy than the scheme of second order (2.7.4). Godunov methods, built on local Riemann problems, capture shocks and resolve nonlinear waves and have been carried over to MHD in many ways, and those of second order rank among the upwind schemes used most in astrophysics (§1). Schemes of higher order improve smooth solutions much faster with resolution, and they use memory more locally and so more efficiently, an advantage that grows with the size of the blocks on processors with many cores. The authors knew of no finite-volume method for MHD of fourth or higher order in active use in astrophysics. Discontinuous Galerkin methods reach any order but are rarely used beyond the fourth, as oscillations at shocks are hard to prevent, finite differences have reached third and fifth order, and finite volumes of high order offer the accuracy of both with robust capturing of shocks. Several such methods couple WENO reconstructions with GLM-MHD to correct the divergence. MHD adds non-convexity and non-strict hyperbolicity to the difficulties of the Euler equations, is intrinsically multidimensional and so resists splitting, and its constraint must hold to numerical precision against magnetic monopoles ([Brackbill and Barnes](#brackbill-barnes-1980) 1980). [McCorquodale and Colella](#mccorquodale-colella-2011) (2011) formulated a method of fourth order for hydrodynamics on mapped multiblock grids with AMR, with quadrature rules evaluated by finite differences, Runge–Kutta integration of fourth order, and PPM for robustness at strong discontinuities, which serves as the basis here. CT ([Evans and Hawley](#evans-hawley-1988) 1988) evolves the field by the induction equation itself and keeps the constraint to round-off, but its original form is limited to second order, and with upwind hydrodynamic fluxes it may hold two independent representations of the field, whose consistency must be ensured case by case. The framework of UCT of [Londrillo and Del Zanna](#londrillo-del-zanna-2000) (2000, [2004](#londrillo-del-zanna-2004)) extends CT to hybrid methods that keep the constraint and agree with the underlying Godunov scheme by construction, the best of their examples being a third-order scheme with WENO reconstruction.

In the hydrodynamic subsystem, the finite-volume method of fourth order rests on quadrature rules of high order on the faces, above all for the fluxes, which [Barad and Colella](#barad-colella-2005) (2005) introduced for the Poisson equation and [McCorquodale and Colella](#mccorquodale-colella-2011) (2011) extended to nonlinear systems of conservation laws (§2). [Guzik et al.](#guzik-et-al-2015) (2015) combined these with mapped coordinates ([Colella et al.](#colella-et-al-2011) 2011) into a method of fourth order on mapped grids with adaptive refinement. The paper assumes a uniform Cartesian mesh of cubic cells of width $h$ in two dimensions, although such methods keep local conservation and preserve a uniform flow on smooth mappings, and they work with many underlying schemes, such as WENO and PPM. Beyond second order the midpoint approximation must be abandoned: averages over cells, faces and edges, written $\langle Q\rangle$, must be distinguished from pointwise values at their centres, and products of averages equal averages of products only to second order. A Taylor expansion about the centre, whose odd terms cancel over the cell, converts between the two at fourth order with the Laplacian of finite differences, $\Delta Q_{i,j} = (Q_{i-1,j} - 2Q_{i,j} + Q_{i+1,j})/h^2 + (Q_{i,j-1} - 2Q_{i,j} + Q_{i,j+1})/h^2$, as $\langle Q\rangle = Q + (h^2/24)\Delta Q$ and its inverse (eqs. 3, 4), and on faces with Laplacians of the derivatives transverse to the normal. The semi-discrete system is integrated by the method of lines, which separates the orders in time and space and admits any explicit multistage integrator that is stable for the central difference operator of fourth order (§2.1; [Colella et al.](#colella-et-al-2011) 2011, §4.2). Most results use RK4, the low-storage SSP method of fourth order of [Gottlieb, Ketcheson and Shu](#gottlieb-et-al-2009) (2009) with five stages and three registers, and studies of convergence also use RK3 ([Shu and Osher](#shu-osher-1988) 1988, eq. 2.19), with three stages and two registers.

The fluxes average to fourth order over the faces in four parts, following [Guzik et al.](#guzik-et-al-2015) (2015, §3.2) (§2.2). Reconstruction acts on the primitive variables, whose transformation from the conserved ones is pointwise and nonlinear (§2.2.1). The cell averages of the conserved variables are converted to values at the centres, $\mathbf{U}_{i,j} = \langle\mathbf{U}\rangle_{i,j} - (h^2/24)\Delta\langle\mathbf{U}\rangle_{i,j}$, which give pointwise primitive variables $\mathbf{W}(\mathbf{U}_{i,j})$ of fourth order. The cell averages also give primitive averages of second order, and the two combine into cell averages of fourth order, $\langle\mathbf{W}\rangle_{i,j} = \mathbf{W}(\mathbf{U}_{i,j}) + (h^2/24)\Delta\mathbf{W}(\langle\mathbf{U}\rangle_{i,j})$, with floors on density and pressure in each inversion and again at the end (eqs. 7 to 9). PPM reconstructs the face averages from these, initialising them by the stencil of four cells $\langle\mathbf{W}\rangle_{i-1/2,j} = \tfrac{7}{12}(\langle\mathbf{W}\rangle_{i-1,j} + \langle\mathbf{W}\rangle_{i,j}) - \tfrac{1}{12}(\langle\mathbf{W}\rangle_{i+1,j} + \langle\mathbf{W}\rangle_{i-2,j})$, which limiters may split into differing left and right states (§2.2.2, eq. 10). Of the limiters tested, the original of [Colella and Woodward](#colella-woodward-1984) (1984), that of [Mignone](#mignone-2014) (2014), the limiter of [Colella and Sekora](#colella-sekora-2008) (2008) that preserves smooth extrema, and its modifications in [Colella et al.](#colella-et-al-2011) (2011, §4.3) and [McCorquodale and Colella](#mccorquodale-colella-2011) (2011, §2.4), the last three, which avoid clipping smooth extrema, gave similar results with small differences in dissipation. The two modifications lose strict preservation of monotonicity, as a non-monotone solution for the advection of a square wave in one dimension shows, and the variant of [McCorquodale and Colella](#mccorquodale-colella-2011) (2011) needs a stencil of seven cells, too costly for MHD. The method therefore uses PPM with interfaces of fourth order, PPM4 in the terms of [Peterson and Hammett](#peterson-hammett-2013) (2013), and a limiter that preserves smooth extrema after [Colella et al.](#colella-et-al-2011) (2011, §4.3), without its check of the monotonicity of the derivative estimates in the limiting of the initial face values (their eq. 86), accepting the extra dissipation, especially at strong discontinuities. The paper lists errata in this literature: a missing factor in the last term of eq. 19 of [Colella and Sekora](#colella-sekora-2008) (2008) and a missing alternative between two conditions in their eq. 20, factors $1/2$ that do not belong in eqs. 85a, 85c and 95b to 95d of [Colella et al.](#colella-et-al-2011) (2011), and an index $j-1$ that must be $j+1$ in one term of the minimum in eq. 1.8 of [Colella and Woodward](#colella-woodward-1984) (1984). Some shock tubes of MHD needed reconstruction in characteristic variables to suppress oscillations. The eigenvectors are those of [Stone et al.](#stone-et-al-2008) (2008, App. A), but the projection differs fundamentally from theirs: the eigenvectors of each cell, computed from its averaged primitive variables, project all averages of the stencil, which are then interpolated and limited as before, and the limited states return to primitive variables (2.4.4). [Matsumoto et al.](#matsumoto-et-al-2019) (2019) summarise this procedure for MP5 reconstruction, with examples for WENO in [Balsara and Shu](#balsara-shu-2000) (2000). The projection is costly, as each cell gets a stencil of its own, and may be impractical for relativity or complex physics, where artificial viscosity, flattening of slopes or stronger limiting are alternatives. The fluxes of fourth order on the faces follow from any Riemann solver that depends only on the left and right primitive states, such as HLLE, HLLC, HLLD, the solver of Roe or LLF (§2.2.3). The face averages of the states are converted to pointwise values at the face centres, a Riemann solver gives the pointwise fluxes there, a second solution between the face-averaged states gives face averages of the fluxes of second order, and their transverse Laplacian corrects the pointwise fluxes to face averages of fourth order (eqs. 14 to 18). Applying the Laplacian to the fluxes and to the primitive variables keeps the stencils small at the price of one more Riemann solution and inversion, and the flux divergence then updates the conserved variables (eq. 5).

In Athena++, the parameter `xorder` of the block `<time>` set to `4` selects the method of fourth order for hydrodynamics on uniform Cartesian meshes with four ghost cells, without mesh refinement or shearing box, and refused with MHD (2.4.1). `EquationOfState::ConservedToPrimitiveCellAverage` performs the inversion of eqs. 7 to 9 with $C = h^2/24$ and the Laplacian of the coordinates, and reapplies the floors at the end (`src/eos/eos_high_order.cpp`). For this order `Hydro::CalculateFluxes` corrects the fluxes on each face by $C$ times the Laplacian of the face-averaged fluxes transverse to the face (`src/hydro/calculate_fluxes.cpp`). With the parameter `correct_ic` of the block `<time>` true, `Mesh::CorrectMidpointInitialCondition` turns the values that a problem generator sets at the cell centres into cell averages of fourth order, $\langle\mathbf{U}\rangle = \mathbf{U} + C\Delta\mathbf{U}$, for the conserved variables and passive scalars, between two exchanges of the ghost cells (`src/mesh/mesh.cpp`). The parameter `correct_err` of the same block serves problem generators that compute errors against averages of fourth order (2.16).

#### 2.7.2 Tests of the hydrodynamic subsystem

[Felker and Stone](#felker-stone-2018) (2018, §3) validate the method, RK4 with PPM, for $\mathbf{B} = 0$ against the scheme of second order of Athena++, VL2 with PLM, which uses the predictor-corrector of [Stone and Gardiner](#stone-gardiner-2009) (2009) with a predictor of first order, PLM of the primitive variables and the midpoint approximation throughout (2.6.5). Both use HLLC on uniform Cartesian grids. A slotted cylinder after [Colella et al.](#colella-et-al-2011) (2011, §4.4.5) is advected as a passive scalar: an isothermal flow of uniform density in rotation with $\omega = 1$ carries the velocity component $v_3$ normal to the plane, and on $100 \times 100$ cells the slot is five cells wide, whereas the initial condition of [Colella et al.](#colella-et-al-2011) (2011) is asymmetric by one cell. The limiters of both schemes keep $v_3$ monotone, while unlimited reconstructions of second or fourth order produce oscillations as large as the cylinder. The limited solution of fourth order keeps the discontinuous slot, which that of second order loses, and its transition to the background is much narrower. As the total $v_3$ is conserved to machine precision, the decay of the volume average of $v_3^2$ measures the dissipation per step, which is much slower with RK4 and PPM, so that reconstruction of high order helps even for highly discontinuous data. Linear sound and entropy waves of amplitude $\varepsilon = 10^{-6}$ on $\rho = 1$, $P = 3/5$ and $\gamma = 5/3$, with $c_s = 1$, travel at the angle $\theta = \tan^{-1}2$, about 63.43 degrees, to the $x_1$-axis, with a wavelength of one, on a periodic domain twice as long as wide, so that one wavelength fits along each boundary. Their eigenfunctions are set to fourth order by evaluating them at the cell centres and applying the Laplacian. After one wavelength, on grids of $8 \times 4$ to $128 \times 64$ cells, the errors of RK3 and RK4 with PPM converge much faster than those of VL2 with PLM, and RK4 with PPM on $16 \times 8$ cells is about as accurate as VL2 with PLM on $128 \times 64$. The sound wave of RK3 with PPM converges at third order only for most resolutions at a Courant number of 0.4, as the error in time dominates. At 0.8 the change to third order moves below $N_{x_1} = 8$, at 0.2 the errors nearly match those of RK4, and at the largest resolution convergence stops near $L_1 \approx \varepsilon^2 = 10^{-12}$, where nonlinear steepening invalidates the linear approximation. In the problem of [Shu and Osher](#shu-osher-1988) (1988), a shock moving from $x_1 = -0.8$ meets a sinusoidal profile of density on $[-1, 1]$, and the solutions at $t_f = 0.47$ are compared with one of RK4 and PPM on 8000 cells (2.16). Limiters that preserve smooth extrema are essential against clipping the many short peaks at low resolution, near which VL2 with PLM dissipates strongly. On 200 cells both schemes capture the smooth features of long wavelength, but the fourth order improves the accuracy most where the profile has short wavelengths.

The regression test `hydro_linwave_2d` runs the linear wave of 2.16 with the combinations of integrator and reconstruction `vl2` with `2c` and `3`, `rk2` with `3c`, `rk3` with `3f` and `4`, `rk4` with `4c` and `ssprk5_4` with `4`, on 16 to 256 cells along $x_1$ (`tst/regression/scripts/tests/hydro4/hydro_linwave_2d.py`). It bounds the RMS of the $L_1$ errors for each resolution and wave from above and the rate of convergence at $N_{x_1} = 128$ from below, about 2 for the combinations of second order and 3.4 to 3.95 for those of fourth order. The test `hydro_linwave_3d` runs `rk4` with `4c` and `ssprk5_4` with `4` in three dimensions on 16 to 64 cells along $x_1$ and half as many along $x_2$ and $x_3$, with `correct_ic` and `correct_err` true, and requires rates of at least 3.95 and 3.94 for the two waves (`tst/regression/scripts/tests/hydro4/hydro_linwave_3d.py`).

#### 2.7.3 Upwind CT of fourth order

The CT of fourth order of [Felker and Stone](#felker-stone-2018) (2018, §4, App. A) is not part of the copy, whose reconstruction refuses the fourth order with MHD as not yet merged (2.4.1). The finite volumes of 2.7.1 do not evolve the induction equation, $\partial\mathbf{B}/\partial t + \nabla \times \mathbf{E} = 0$ with $\mathbf{E} = -\mathbf{v} \times \mathbf{B}$ (eq. 27). CT evolves face averages of the field by the theorem of Stokes, in semi-discrete form in two dimensions (eqs. 28a, 28b), with the magnetic flux as conserved quantity and line averages of the electric field at the corners as fluxes, so that the constraint follows from local conservation. The staggered field is, however, a second representation besides the cell averages, and the ways of coupling it to the upwind cell averages of a Godunov scheme vary widely and may not prevent numerical instabilities. The CT of [Gardiner and Stone](#gardiner-stone-2005) (2005) couples to CTU by upwinding transversely the electric fields that the Godunov fluxes contain, and it was extended to three dimensions ([Gardiner and Stone](#gardiner-stone-2008) 2008) and to VL2 ([Stone and Gardiner](#stone-gardiner-2009) 2009). Of its algorithms, $\mathcal{E}^c_z$ is stable, free of oscillations and has the right viscosity (2.3.5), and it is the only CT of the later papers of the authors and of Athena. Some later literature takes $\mathcal{E}^\circ_z$ for the final scheme of 2005 ([Balsara](#balsara-2014) 2014, §9.5). Later statements of eq. 42 of [Gardiner and Stone](#gardiner-stone-2005) (2005), moved to the lower corner, have the wrong signs in their derivative terms, in [Stone and Gardiner](#stone-gardiner-2009) (2009, eq. 22) and in [Stone et al.](#stone-et-al-2008) (2008), whose equation [Felker and Stone](#felker-stone-2018) (2018) number 79, eq. 70 of the local copy (2.3.5). They number the equations of [Gardiner and Stone](#gardiner-stone-2005) (2005) cited here one lower than the local copy.

$\mathcal{E}^c_z$ is consistent with the Godunov algorithm for planar solutions in one dimension, but at most of second order. Its estimate from a single face, eq. 41 of [Gardiner and Stone](#gardiner-stone-2005) (2005), truncates the higher derivatives of a Taylor expansion. The face values of the electric field are midpoint approximations, and the cell-centred field, the average of the face averages (eqs. 19, 20 there), is of second order. Most importantly, $\mathcal{E}^c_z$ captures the Riemann fan of several dimensions at the corner only through split approximations: its upwinded slopes are differences between the face flux of a Riemann solver in one dimension and the reference field at the cell centre, which caps the accuracy at second order whatever the reconstruction. The first three limits could be lifted by approximations of higher order, the last not simply, and App. A proves that upwinding fluxes of approximate Riemann solutions on the faces, rather than smooth approximations of $\mathcal{E}_3$, keeps the scheme from reducing to planar waves along the diagonals. The UCT of [Londrillo and Del Zanna](#londrillo-del-zanna-2000) (2000, [2004](#londrillo-del-zanna-2004)) is free of these limits, and App. A shows $\mathcal{E}^c_z$ to agree with it to second order, although its derivation does not follow UCT. UCT treats the induction equation in two phases, reconstruction and upwinding, by analogy with the fluxes of a Godunov scheme of high order, but for fluxes at the corners in two dimensions and with care for the constraint and the continuity of field lines. A UCT of fourth order thus needs a reconstruction from the faces to the corners and an upwinding at the corners that approximates a multidimensional Riemann problem, all at fourth order, at the end of each stage after the fluxes of 2.7.1.

Limited reconstruction may leave four states at a corner, which the paper labels by the faces across which they differ, after eq. 39 of [Amano](#amano-2015) (2015), rather than by the counter-intuitive compass points of [Londrillo and Del Zanna](#londrillo-del-zanna-2004) (2004) (§4.1). The field cannot be represented freely by piecewise polynomials, since each component must be single-valued at the faces normal to it for the field lines to be continuous, so that at a corner $B_1$ may jump only across $x_2$. In UCT the face averages of the normal components are the primary representation and are never reconstructed. The cell averages that the hydrodynamics needs and the states at the corners are reconstructed from them. After the reconstruction of the hydrodynamics, the electric field at each corner is reconstructed to fourth order from the components of velocity and field (eq. 36), by transverse reconstructions with PPM4 of the states of $\mathbf{v}$ and $\mathbf{B}$ already reconstructed on the faces. PPM is not essential here, and WENO was left for later. $B_2$ is reconstructed along $x_1$ from the face data of $x_2$, $B_1$ along $x_2$ from those of $x_1$, and both components of the velocity along both directions, with the two estimates of the velocity averaged, since limited reconstructions need not commute. The four states of the electric field follow from these (Figure 7). After the update of eqs. 28a and 28b, an inverse reconstruction recomputes the cell averages of the field at fourth order without limiting (eqs. 38 to 40). A transverse Laplacian turns face averages into values at the face centres, interpolation along the normal gives values at the cell centres, and a Laplacian turns these into cell averages. Its stencil is wide, wider along $x_1$ than along $x_2$ for $B_1$, and its intermediate values are reused for neighbouring cells, which pays off only for large blocks.

Upwinding at the corners extends the selection rule of a Riemann solver from two states to four (§4.2). Solvers of Roe type linearise the Jacobian, need a characteristic decomposition and can give unphysical states, while those of HLL type ([Harten, Lax and van Leer](#harten-et-al-1983) 1983) average over an approximate fan and are efficient and positive but less accurate, which reconstruction of high order can make up for, so that HLL or even LLF may suffice. Both were extended to MHD ([Cargo and Gallice](#cargo-gallice-1997) 1997; [Miyoshi and Kusano](#miyoshi-kusano-2005) 2005). In CT the corners are the right places for the Riemann problem of the field. [Londrillo and Del Zanna](#londrillo-del-zanna-2000) (2000) gave a UCT with fluxes of Roe type, in which only the dissipative fluxes are averaged, within a splitting of the flux vector, and [Londrillo and Del Zanna](#londrillo-del-zanna-2004) (2004, §3.2) two central-upwind forms with the one-state HLL solver, MC-HLL-UCT of second and CENO-HLL-UCT of third order. [Felker and Stone](#felker-stone-2018) (2018) use the one-state HLL form: averaging the overlapping fans along $x_1$ and $x_2$ at the corner gives the flux of eq. 41, with nonnegative dissipative terms from the smallest and largest speeds in each direction. These speeds are the extremes of those of the four Riemann solutions at the faces next to the corner, the estimates of [Einfeldt et al.](#einfeldt-et-al-1991) (1991) with Roe averages, where [Londrillo and Del Zanna](#londrillo-del-zanna-2004) (2004) used those of [Davis](#davis-1988) (1988). The form generalises the upwinding and averaging of four states of [Gardiner and Stone](#gardiner-stone-2005) (2005), and it amounts to a simple Riemann solver in several dimensions for the induction equation, like the multidimensional solvers for MHD of [Balsara](#balsara-2010) (2010, [2012](#balsara-2012)), that reduces to the HLL fluxes of the field for plane-parallel solutions.

App. A compares $\mathcal{E}^c_z$ with UCT in limiting cases, for Godunov fluxes from the one-state HLL solver, whose flux of $B_2$ on a face normal to $x_1$, an upwinded $\mathcal{E}_3$, splits into a smooth part and the explicit dissipation of HLL, written with nonnegative speeds as in eq. 55 of [Londrillo and Del Zanna](#londrillo-del-zanna-2004) (2004). In a stationary domain (App. A.1) the smooth electric fields vanish, the upwinded derivatives of $\mathcal{E}^c_z$ reduce to averages and the reference fields to zero, and its corner value becomes the formula without directional bias and with the right viscosity, eq. 40 of [Gardiner and Stone](#gardiner-stone-2005) (2005). With a reconstruction of second order this equals the UCT value (eq. A.7). For a plane-parallel flow along the grid with $v_1 \ne 0$ and $v_2 = 0$ (App. A.2), $\mathcal{E}^c_z$ acts as a switch between the HLL fluxes on the two faces normal to $x_2$, with states reconstructed in neighbouring cells, so that it is consistent to second order but cannot match UCT exactly in general (eq. A.28). Agreement to second order follows under further assumptions: the speeds of [Davis](#davis-1988) (1988), a supersonic flow with $v_1 \ge c_f$, for which the dissipation along $x_1$ vanishes, and equal reconstructed states of $B_2$ across $x_1$.

#### 2.7.4 Tests of MHD

The tests of MHD of [Felker and Stone](#felker-stone-2018) (2018, §5) rely on the CT of fourth order, so that they cannot be repeated with the copy either (2.7.3). They probe the accuracy, the stability, the capture of shocks and the suppression of magnetic monopoles of RK4 with PPM and UCT, on problems that 2.16 takes up with their families. The reference is again VL2 with PLM, now with the CT of second order of [Gardiner and Stone](#gardiner-stone-2005) (2005) and [Stone and Gardiner](#stone-gardiner-2009) (2009), which the paper regards as a UCT of second order. HLLD gives the hydrodynamic fluxes unless stated otherwise. The Courant number is $C_0 = \Delta t/\min(h/|\lambda_1^{\max}|, h/|\lambda_2^{\max}|)$, where $\lambda_1^{\max}$ is the largest value over the cells of $|\langle v_1\rangle| + c_{f,1}$, with the fast magnetosonic speed along $x_1$ computed from the cell averages, and $\lambda_2^{\max}$ its counterpart along $x_2$ (eq. 42). The linear analysis of stability of [Colella et al.](#colella-et-al-2011) (2011) served as a guide to its limit, but all tests use the cautious value $C_0 = 0.4$. The error of each variable is the mean absolute difference of its cell averages from those of the initial state (eq. 24). The convergence plots show the square root of the sum of the squares of these errors over the hydrodynamic variables and the components of the field (eqs. 25, 43).

The linear waves of 2.7.2 are set up again from the exact eigenvectors of linearised MHD, with the same domain, wavelength and direction and the same background $\rho = 1$, $P = 3/5$ and $\gamma = 5/3$, to which the field $\mathbf{B} = (1, \sqrt{2}, 1/2)$ in the frame of the wave vector is added (§5.1). The fluid is at rest except in the entropy wave, which has $v_1 = 1$. The fast, Alfvén, slow and entropy waves then travel at 2, 1, 1/2 and 1. Their eigenvectors are those of [Gardiner and Stone](#gardiner-stone-2005) (2005, App. A). The paper warns that the eigenvectors of [Gardiner and Stone](#gardiner-stone-2008) (2008, App. A), to which the tests in three dimensions of [Stone et al.](#stone-et-al-2008) (2008, §8.6) and [Stone and Gardiner](#stone-gardiner-2009) (2009, §6.1) refer for their setup, belong to another background, $\mathbf{B} = (1, 3/2, 0)$. The fluid variables are set to fourth order as in 2.7.2 (eqs. 22, 23). The face averages of the field follow by the theorem of Stokes from the analytic vector potential at the corners of the cells, so that the initial divergence vanishes to machine precision (eq. 44). Applied naively, this made the errors stop converging prematurely. The potential is the sum of a background of order one and a perturbation of order $\varepsilon$ (eq. 45), and differencing such sums loses significance in double precision. Differencing the two parts separately, each between quantities of similar size, removed the problem (eq. 46). On grids of $8 \times 4$ to $128 \times 64$ cells all four waves converge at fourth order (Figure 9). At $N_{x_1} = 128$ the error of RK4 with PPM lies nearly four orders of magnitude below that of VL2 with PLM, a larger gain than in hydrodynamics. RK3 with PPM gives almost the errors of RK4, except in the fast wave, where it converges at third order over most resolutions. At a fixed Courant number the fast wave needs about a quarter of the steps of the slow wave to cross one wavelength. The slow wave thus collects about four times the spatial error, which dominates its error at all resolutions. The semi-discrete form lets the same spatial operator be combined with whichever integrator an application needs, and the cheaper RK3 suffices where the resolution in time does not dominate the error.

The circularly polarised Alfvén wave, first described by [Tóth](#toth-2000) (2000, §6.3.1), uses the same domain, wavelength and direction, but its profiles are exact nonlinear solutions of ideal MHD of finite amplitude (§5.2). With the parameters of [Gardiner and Stone](#gardiner-stone-2005) (2005, §3.3.2), the background has $\rho = 1$ and $P = 0.1$, and in the frame of the wave vector $\mathbf{B} = (1, 0.1\sin 2\pi x, 0.1\cos 2\pi x)$ and $\mathbf{v} = (v_x, 0.1\sin 2\pi x, 0.1\cos 2\pi x)$ (eqs. 47a, 47b). This wave escapes the parametric instability through which other Alfvén waves decay into magnetosonic ones. The field is again set from the vector potential. The wave travels to the left for $v_x = 0$ and stands for $v_x = 1$, the harder variant, as the multidimensional updates of the face-averaged field must then cancel exactly. On $32 \times 16$ cells at $t = 5$, a scatter plot of the transverse field $B_y$ of all cells against their position along the wave shows that RK4 with PPM almost reproduces the initial profile, while VL2 with PLM diffuses away over half of its amplitude (Figure 10). The limiter that preserves smooth extrema is what prevents such dissipation at fourth order. On coarser grids, such as $N_{x_1} = 16$, dispersion dominates the error of second order, while that of fourth order stays negligible at every resolution tested. Both schemes keep the wave fronts uniform, as the samples at nearby phases, which would reveal grid noise in several dimensions, hardly spread. [Mignone, Tzeferacos and Bodo](#mignone-et-al-2010) (2010, Figure A.2) also recovered $B_y$ nearly perfectly, with WENO-Z and MP5 of fifth order, for the travelling wave on $32 \times 16 \times 16$ cells. At $t = 1$, on $16 \times 8$ to $256 \times 128$ cells, both waves converge at fourth order, and their errors nearly coincide with those of the linear Alfvén wave scaled by $10^{-5}$, the ratio of the amplitudes (Figure 11). As [Stone and Gardiner](#stone-gardiner-2009) (2009, §6.2) found for VL2 with PLM, the relative dissipation depends neither on the amplitude nor on nonlinear effects, and the resolution alone sets the numerical diffusivity. A three-dimensional version after [Gardiner and Stone](#gardiner-stone-2008) (2008, §6.3, which the paper numbers §5.3), on $16 \times 8 \times 8$ to $128 \times 64 \times 64$ cells, also converges at fourth order. Its errors exceed those in two dimensions by 48 to 50 per cent for the standing and by 13 to 15 per cent for the travelling wave, owing to the spatial error of the inclination to $x_3$. As in [Gardiner and Stone](#gardiner-stone-2008) (2008, Figure 4), the travelling wave has the larger errors. The scheme in three dimensions has no new parts, but its cost relative to VL2 with PLM rises well above that in two dimensions, mainly through the additional transverse reconstructions with PPM and the upwinding of $\mathcal{E}_1$ and $\mathcal{E}_2$. The convergence studies end at $t = 1$, following earlier papers, while the scatter plots run to $t = 5$ on coarse grids to bring out the difference in diffusivity. In runs to $t = 5$, VL2 with PLM does not attain its second order on the coarsest grids because of its large dispersion error, whereas RK4 with PPM reaches its asymptotic order without such delay. [Susanto et al.](#susanto-et-al-2013) (2013, Figure 1) found the same difference between schemes of second and fourth order.

Advecting a field loop, as [Gardiner and Stone](#gardiner-stone-2005) (2005) did in two dimensions and [Gardiner and Stone](#gardiner-stone-2008) (2008) in three, tests the discretisation of CT closely (§5.3). A cylinder of radius $R = 0.3$ about the origin holds a field of weak, uniform strength, set by the vector potential $A_3 = A_0(R - r)$ inside and zero outside, with $A_0 = 10^{-3}$ and $B_z = 0$ (eq. 48), so that $\beta = 2P/B^2 = 2 \times 10^6$ inside. The fluid has $\rho = 1$, $P = 1$ and $\gamma = 5/3$, and as in [Gardiner and Stone](#gardiner-stone-2005) (2005) the grid has $2N \times N$ cells with $N = 64$ over $[-1, 1] \times [-0.5, 0.5]$, with periodic boundaries. Unlike there, the velocity $\mathbf{v} = (2, 1, 2)$, of magnitude 3, points along the diagonal of the box $[-1, 1] \times [-0.5, 0.5] \times [-1, 1]$, so that the loop returns to its place after each unit of time. The run ends after two periods, at $t_f = 2$. The fourth order keeps the cylinder of magnetic pressure more uniform and its edge sharper, and the hole that reconnection opens at its centre is much larger with VL2 and PLM (Figure 12). Slices through the centre along both axes and both diagonals are symmetric about the diagonal parallel to the flow. At the leading edge VL2 with PLM rises nearly 20 per cent above the initial maximum, while RK4 with PPM rises about 8 per cent above it at the trailing edge. Both show the small asymmetries between leading and trailing edges that advection may produce. Along a diagonal, VL2 with PLM spreads the edges over 6 to 8 cells and RK4 with PPM over 4, and the latter approximates the flat top better despite minor oscillations. The mean magnetic energy of the domain decays much more slowly at fourth order, which the paper credits to the reconstruction with PPM at the corners in the upwinding of UCT. Most importantly, $B_3$ stays zero to round-off throughout, although $v_3 \ne 0$ puts nonzero terms into its update. Its deviations gather near the path of the loop, but stay far below the machine epsilon of double precision, about $2.22 \times 10^{-16}$ (Figure 13). The integral of $B_3^2$ over the domain grows faster at first with RK4 and PPM, which evaluates the induction equation more often per step, but the count of cells where $B_3$ differs from zero grows at a rate similar to that of VL2 with PLM (Figure 14). Advecting a poloidal field along $x_3$ is not trivial for general schemes of CT ([Stone et al.](#stone-et-al-2008) 2008). The test confirms that the UCT of fourth order keeps the field free of divergence by construction, and it also preserves the concentric field lines (not shown).

The shock tube of [Brio and Wu](#brio-wu-1988) (1988), the analogue in MHD of that of [Sod](#sod-1978) (1978), tests the capture of shocks and nonlinear waves (§5.4). With $\gamma = 2$ and $B_1 = 0.75$, the left state has $\rho = 1$, $P = 1$ and $B_2 = 1$, the right state $\rho = 0.125$, $P = 0.1$ and $B_2 = -1$, both at rest and with $B_3 = 0$ (eq. 49). At $t = 0.1$, solutions on 256 cells are compared with one of RK4 with PPM on 8192 cells (Figure 15). The fourth order needs the characteristic reconstruction of 2.7.1, as primitive variables gave spurious oscillations of more than 10 per cent of the range of the solution, and even PLM of the primitive variables oscillates nonphysically at second order. With primitive reconstruction, the worst oscillations appear in the velocity between the slow shock near $x_1 \approx 0.14$ and the fast rarefaction near $x_1 \approx 0.36$ (Figure 16). This is a known weakness of reconstructions of high order, which [Matsumoto et al.](#matsumoto-et-al-2019) (2019) show for MP5. RK4 with PPM shows none of the staircase of MP5 at the compound slow wave around $x_1 \approx -0.02$, and it resolves the slow shock slightly better. In the shock tube RJ2a of [Ryu and Jones](#ryu-jones-1995) (1995, Figure 2a), all seven waves of MHD emerge from the data of eq. 50 (§5.5). The left state has $\rho = 1.08$, $\mathbf{v} = (1.2, 0.01, 0.5)$ and $P = 0.95$, the right state $\rho = 1$, $\mathbf{v} = 0$ and $P = 1$. The field has $B_1 = B_3 = 2/\sqrt{4\pi}$ on both sides, with $B_2 = 3.6/\sqrt{4\pi}$ on the left and $4/\sqrt{4\pi}$ on the right, values that the paper gives as decimals. On 512 cells the fourth order captures all features and resolves each discontinuity within 2 to 5 cells, against a reference on 8192 cells (Figure 17). On 128 cells with the more diffusive HLLE in both schemes, its profile of $B_2$ compares well with that of VL2 with PLM, above all at the rotational discontinuities (Figure 18). Where stability or cost forces HLLE on a coarse grid, the low diffusivity of high order may thus still pay off, even in a flow dominated by discontinuities. Both figures use characteristic reconstruction at fourth order again, but here primitive reconstruction gave tolerable oscillations, of the order of those of VL2 with PLM.

Three problems in two dimensions test the robustness of the scheme on $500^2$ cells. The vortex of [Orszag and Tang](#orszag-tang-1979) (1979), with the initial conditions of [Stone et al.](#stone-et-al-2008) (2008, §8.4) rescaled to the domain $[-0.5, 0.5]^2$, tests the resolution of interacting shocks in MHD turbulence while monopoles stay suppressed (§5.6). Its solutions at $t = 0.5$ and $t = 1$ resolve the vortex at the origin better than one of second order, which is not shown (Figures 19, 20). In the blast wave, a periodic domain $[-0.5, 0.5]^2$ with $\rho = 1$, $P = 0.1$ and $\gamma = 5/3$ holds a strong field $B_0 = 1$ along the main diagonal, $\mathbf{B} = (B_0/\sqrt{2}, B_0/\sqrt{2}, 0)$, and an overpressure of $P = 10$ within $r = 0.1$ of the origin (§5.7, eq. 51). At $t = 0.2$, just before the shock crosses the periodic boundary, the expanding shell has been focused along the field into an elliptical region of low density (Figure 21). Its density and pressure are better resolved than in the results of second order of [Stone et al.](#stone-et-al-2008) (2008, Figure 28) and [Stone and Gardiner](#stone-gardiner-2009) (2009, Figure 8). Across the field, the outermost front is a fast wave dominated by the magnetic pressure, which soon separates widely from the contact of the initial overpressure. Along the field, the slow shock stays closer to the elongated contact, and the fast front vanishes from density and gas pressure. For the background $\beta = 0.2$ it becomes an incompressible Alfvén mode along the field lines, which appears in the magnetic pressure only slightly apart from the slow shock. The rotor of [Balsara and Spicer](#balsara-spicer-1999) (1999), in which a rapidly rotating dense disc shears the field into strong rotational discontinuities, starts from the initial conditions of [Stone et al.](#stone-et-al-2008) (2008, Figure 25), the first rotor test of [Tóth](#toth-2000) (2000) (§5.8). The background has $\rho = 1$, $P = 1$, $B_1 = 5/(2\sqrt{\pi})$ and $\gamma = 7/5$, and a disc of $\rho = 10$ within $r = 0.1$ of the origin rotates at the angular velocity $\omega = 20$, without smoothing. At $t = 0.15$ the solution keeps its symmetry well, notably in the concentric circles of the Mach number in the rarefaction from the origin (Figure 22).

[Felker and Stone](#felker-stone-2018) (2018, §6) conclude that the framework of UCT yields a staggered CT, free of divergence, that is consistent with finite volumes of fourth order for the hydrodynamic subsystem, built on the quadrature rules and intermediate steps of fourth order of [McCorquodale and Colella](#mccorquodale-colella-2011) (2011). Compared with the CT of second order of [Gardiner and Stone](#gardiner-stone-2005) (2005), the method lowers the error by orders of magnitude in smooth linear and nonlinear problems, and it remains robust at discontinuities in tests of several dimensions. Methods of high order gain most in demanding problems with long run times and complex smooth features. The many arithmetic operations of the method per memory reference can also offset the growing cost in time and power of such references relative to operations in floating point on modern processors.

### 2.8 Curvilinear coordinates

#### 2.8.1 Grids and geometry

[Skinner and Ostriker](#skinner-ostriker-2010) (2010) describe how cylindrical coordinates were added to Athena, changing its Cartesian code as little and as transparently as possible (abstract). The equations are formulated so that they stay consistent with CT and leave the existing modules unchanged, above all the Riemann solvers with their wave speeds and eigenfunctions (§1). The transport of angular momentum, critical in discs dominated by rotation, is treated with care. The paper modifies the reconstruction of second and third order, the characteristic evolution, the finite volumes and CT, and it gives a suite of tests in one to three dimensions (2.8.3 to 2.8.7). Symmetry lowers the cost of solving the eight coupled equations of ideal MHD. Discs whose properties do not depend statistically on the azimuth can be modelled in the radial–vertical plane, discs with little vertical structure in the radial–azimuthal plane, and winds and jets in axisymmetry. For rotating flows aligned with the grid, curvilinear coordinates also conserve the total angular momentum better than a Cartesian grid, and they make boundary conditions much simpler to impose. Among the publicly available codes for astrophysical MHD that capture shocks, only PLUTO ([Mignone et al.](#mignone-et-al-2007) 2007) and VAC ([Tóth](#toth-1996) 1996) then handled MHD in curvilinear coordinates.

In Athena++ the framework of the mesh and its refinement works on logically rectangular arrays of cells, without reference to a geometry, so that in principle any coordinates can be used ([Stone et al.](#stone-et-al-2020) 2020, §2.3). The cells may be spaced nonuniformly, with sizes that vary smoothly with position along each dimension independently. Uniform and logarithmic spacings are built in, and a user may supply a function for any other. The class `Coordinates` constructs and stores the sizes, areas and volumes of the cells, from which the vector and tensor operators of each coordinate system are built. Calculations without relativity, or with special relativity, use Cartesian, cylindrical or spherical-polar coordinates. General relativity offers Minkowski and Schwarzschild coordinates, spherical Kerr–Schild coordinates and any stationary metric that a user specifies (2.14). New coordinate systems are straightforward to add. The pole of spherical-polar and similar coordinates needs a boundary condition of its own. Its ghost cells and faces take the values of the cells and faces across the pole, with the symmetry that the pole implies. The fluxes through the faces on the pole have no effect, as these faces have no area, and the electric fields on the radial edges that touch the pole are replaced by their average, since they must agree (3.3).

The option `--coord` of `configure.py` chooses one file of `src/coordinates/`: `cartesian`, `cylindrical` or `spherical_polar` without general relativity, and the others only with it. Since 2024 a single class `Coordinates` serves all of them. `src/coordinates/coordinates.cpp` defines its functions for Cartesian geometry as weak symbols, which the chosen file overrides where its geometry differs (`src/coordinates/coordinates.hpp`). The constructor sets the positions and widths of the faces of each block, ghost cells included, from the generator of the mesh in each direction (`Coordinates::Coordinates`). The default generator spaces the faces uniformly or, for a ratio `x1rat`, `x2rat` or `x3rat` of the block `<mesh>` other than one, with widths that grow by that ratio from cell to cell, and a problem generator may enrol a generator of its own (`src/mesh/mesh.hpp`, `DefaultMeshGeneratorX1`; `Mesh::EnrollUserMeshGenerator`; 3.6). The constructor warns when neighbouring widths differ by more than 10 per cent, and next to reflecting boundaries, and polar ones along $x_2$, it mirrors the widths into the ghost cells. `Coordinates::Initialize` of the chosen file then sets the volume centres and the geometric coefficients.

In Cartesian coordinates the volume centre is the midpoint of the cell, and areas, lengths and volumes are products of widths (`src/coordinates/cartesian.cpp`). In cylindrical coordinates $(R, \phi, z)$ a cell has the volume $\tfrac{1}{2}(R_{i+1/2}^2 - R_{i-1/2}^2)\Delta\phi\Delta z$, its radial face at $R_{i-1/2}$ the area $R_{i-1/2}\Delta\phi\Delta z$, its face normal to $z$ the area $\tfrac{1}{2}(R_{i+1/2}^2 - R_{i-1/2}^2)\Delta\phi$, and its azimuthal edge at $R_{i-1/2}$ the length $R_{i-1/2}\Delta\phi$ (`src/coordinates/cylindrical.cpp`). Athena uses the same volumes and areas, written with the midpoint $R_i$, for which $R_i\Delta R = \tfrac{1}{2}(R_{i+1/2}^2 - R_{i-1/2}^2)$ ([Skinner and Ostriker](#skinner-ostriker-2010) 2010, §7, eqs. 70, 71). The radial volume centre is $\tfrac{2}{3}(R_{i+1/2}^3 - R_{i-1/2}^3)/(R_{i+1/2}^2 - R_{i-1/2}^2) = R_i + \Delta R^2/(12R_i)$, the centroid $\langle R\rangle_i$ of the cell (eq. 48; [Mignone](#mignone-2014) 2014). In spherical-polar coordinates $(r, \theta, \phi)$ the volume is $\tfrac{1}{3}(r_{i+1/2}^3 - r_{i-1/2}^3)|\cos\theta_{j-1/2} - \cos\theta_{j+1/2}|\Delta\phi$. The radial face has the area $r_{i-1/2}^2|\cos\theta_{j-1/2} - \cos\theta_{j+1/2}|\Delta\phi$, the polar face $\tfrac{1}{2}(r_{i+1/2}^2 - r_{i-1/2}^2)\sin\theta_{j-1/2}\Delta\phi$ and the azimuthal face $\tfrac{1}{2}(r_{i+1/2}^2 - r_{i-1/2}^2)\Delta\theta$, and the edges along $\theta$ and $\phi$ have the lengths $r_{i-1/2}\Delta\theta$ and $r_{i-1/2}\sin\theta_{j-1/2}\Delta\phi$ (`src/coordinates/spherical_polar.cpp`).
