The volume centres are $\tfrac{3}{4}(r_{i+1/2}^4 - r_{i-1/2}^4)/(r_{i+1/2}^3 - r_{i-1/2}^3)$ in radius and the centroid weighted by $\sin\theta$ in the polar angle, the difference of $\sin\theta - \theta\cos\theta$ across the cell divided by that of $-\cos\theta$. The polar range must lie within $[0, \pi]$ in two and three dimensions, and in one dimension be symmetric about $\pi/2$. The physical widths at the volume centres, such as $R\Delta\phi$, enter the time step and the reconstruction (2.6.5, 2.4). The scale factors of the metric and their derivatives, $h_2$, $h_{31}$ and $h_{32}$ at faces and volume centres, equal $R$, 1 and 1 in cylindrical and $r$, $r$ and $\sin\theta$ in spherical-polar coordinates, and they serve orbital advection, diffusion and passive scalars (2.9, 2.11, 2.12). Further functions give lengths and areas between volume centres for non-ideal MHD, positions averaged over faces for the prolongation of the field under mesh refinement, and geometric terms for radiation and cosmic rays (2.11, 3.4, 2.15). The Laplacians of the method of fourth order keep their Cartesian form (2.7.1).

#### 2.8.2 The equations in curvilinear coordinates

[Skinner and Ostriker](#skinner-ostriker-2010) (2010, §2) start from the conservative equations of ideal MHD written without reference to coordinates, with the total pressure $P^* = P + B^2/2$, the total energy $E = \epsilon + \rho v^2/2 + B^2/2$, an ideal gas $P = (\gamma - 1)\epsilon$, and units with $\mu = 1$, so that $\mathbf{B}$ stands for $\mathbf{B}/\sqrt{4\pi}$ in cgs units (eqs. 1a to 1d). A static gravitational potential $\Phi$ may add source terms to the momentum and the energy, and the energy may include radiative heating and cooling. The system reads $\partial_t\mathbf{Q} + \nabla\cdot\mathbf{F} = 0$, with the momentum tensor $\mathsf{M} = \rho\mathbf{v}\mathbf{v} - \mathbf{B}\mathbf{B} + P^*\mathsf{I}$ and the induction tensor $\mathsf{J} = \mathbf{v}\mathbf{B} - \mathbf{B}\mathbf{v}$ among its fluxes, and with source terms kept outside it (eqs. 2 to 6). Extending the Cartesian algorithm brings in scale factors and the source terms of the covariant derivatives. In cylindrical coordinates the divergence of a tensor $\mathsf{T}$ gains the terms $-T_{\phi\phi}/R$ in its radial and $T_{\phi R}/R$ in its azimuthal component (eqs. 8, 9). These geometric source terms act as fictitious forces, such as the centrifugal and Coriolis forces, and they make the update of the finite volumes no longer fully conservative, but all except one can be removed. The continuity and energy equations have none (eqs. 10, 16). The symmetry of $\mathsf{M}$ lets the azimuthal momentum be written with $(1/R^2)\partial_R(R^2M_{R\phi})$, which makes it the conservation law of the angular momentum $R\rho v_\phi$, without source term, as [Mignone et al.](#mignone-et-al-2007) (2007) note (eqs. 11 to 13). As the flux calculations of Athena use linear momenta, the equation is divided by $R$ again, so that $\rho v_\phi$ is evolved with the radial flux $R^2M_{R\phi}$ and no source term (eq. 14). The only geometric source term left is $M_{\phi\phi}/R = (\rho v_\phi^2 - B_\phi^2 + P^*)/R$ in the radial momentum, which gravity often balances in part. In the induction equation the azimuthal component has the source term $J_{R\phi}/R$ (eq. 18b). Any source term among the fluxes of the field would endanger the divergence constraint, so the equation is rewritten for the conserved quantity $B_\phi/R$, with fluxes divided by $R$ and no source term (eq. 19). The changed fluxes do no harm, as Athena evolves the field by CT, not by differences of fluxes (2.8.5). The reconstruction uses an equivalent reduced form, $\partial_tB_\phi + \partial_RJ_{R\phi} + \partial_zJ_{z\phi} = 0$, with the original fluxes (eq. 20; 2.8.3).

[Stone et al.](#stone-et-al-2020) (2020, §3.2.4) write the azimuthal momentum of Athena++ in the usual form with geometric source terms. In cylindrical coordinates it reads $\partial_t(\rho v_\phi) + (1/R)\partial_R(RM_{R\phi}) + (1/R)\partial_\phi M_{\phi\phi} + \partial_zM_{z\phi} = -M_{R\phi}/R$ (eq. 16), and in spherical-polar coordinates $\partial_t(\rho v_\phi) + (1/r^2)\partial_r(r^2M_{r\phi}) + [1/(r\sin\theta)]\partial_\theta(\sin\theta M_{\theta\phi}) + [1/(r\sin\theta)]\partial_\phi M_{\phi\phi} = -(M_{\phi r} + \cot\theta M_{\phi\theta})/r$ (eq. 17), with $M$ the total stress tensor. Written for the angular momentum, such as $R\rho v_\phi$, both become conservation laws again, with the geometric factors inside the divergence of the fluxes.

#### 2.8.3 Reconstruction and characteristic evolution

Athena computes the interface states from the equations in primitive variables, projected onto one direction, linearised and evolved ([Skinner and Ostriker](#skinner-ostriker-2010) 2010, §4). The projection along $\phi$ follows from the Cartesian one of [Gardiner and Stone](#gardiner-stone-2005) (2005, §3.1) with $\partial_y \to R^{-1}\partial_\phi$, but the projection along $R$ gains terms from the scale factors. With $\partial_\phi = \partial_z = 0$ it reads $\partial_t\mathbf{w} + \mathsf{A}\,\partial_R\mathbf{w} = \mathbf{s}$, with $\mathbf{w} = (\rho, v_R, v_\phi, v_z, P, B_\phi, B_z)$ and the wave matrix $\mathsf{A}$ of the Cartesian equations, so that the eigensystem already implemented serves unchanged (eqs. 21 to 23; 2.4.4). The vector $\mathbf{s}$ collects the source terms of MHD from the divergence constraint, those of gravity and the geometric ones. The momentum equation is first written with $\nabla\cdot\mathbf{B} = 0$ enforced, so that errors in the divergence cannot affect the momentum during the reconstruction (eq. 25). The radial momentum then has the source term $(v_\phi^2 - B_\phi^2/\rho)/R$ and the azimuthal momentum $-(v_\phi v_R - B_\phi B_R/\rho)/R$, which the primitive form must keep to preserve the structure of $\mathsf{A}$, although eq. 13 avoids it in conservative form (eqs. 26a to 26c). The density and the pressure gain $-\rho v_R/R$ and $-\gamma Pv_R/R$ (eqs. 24, 28). In two dimensions the divergence constraint turns the induction equation into the Cartesian form plus the curvature terms $-v_\phi B_R/R$ for $B_\phi$ and $-v_RB_z/R$ for $B_z$ (eqs. 29, 30). In three dimensions the limited source terms of MHD of [Gardiner and Stone](#gardiner-stone-2008) (2008) carry over with $\partial_xB_x \to R^{-1}\partial_R(RB_R)$ and $\partial_yB_y \to R^{-1}\partial_\phi B_\phi$ (eqs. 31 to 34; 2.6.4). The geometric source vector, $\mathbf{s}_{\rm geom} = -(1/R)(\rho v_R, -v_\phi^2 + B_\phi^2/\rho, v_\phi v_R - B_\phi B_R/\rho, 0, \gamma Pv_R, v_\phi B_R, v_RB_z)$, belongs to the radial direction alone and vanishes as $R \to \infty$. It enters only the interface states, not the final update of the finite volumes (eq. 35). Gravity adds the components of $-\nabla\Phi$ to the primitive momentum and nothing to the pressure.

The reconstruction is split by direction and follows [Colella and Woodward](#colella-woodward-1984) (1984) and [Colella](#colella-1990) (1990), with linear and parabolic profiles for second and third order in smooth flow (§5). Each profile must reproduce the cell average of its variable over the volume of the cell (eq. 36), which differs from the Cartesian case only along $R$, where the volumes change. A linear profile $a = a_{L,i} + s\Delta a_i$, with $s$ running from 0 to 1 across the cell, then has $a_i = a_{L,i} + \tfrac{1}{2}\Delta a_i(1 + \gamma_i)$, with the correction for curvature $\gamma_i = \Delta R/(6R_i)$ (eqs. 37, 38, 44, 45). As the cell averages of a linear profile follow the centroids $\langle R\rangle_i$, linear profiles are reproduced exactly if the Cartesian central difference is divided by $1 - \Delta R^2/(12R_{i+1}R_{i-1})$ and the one-sided differences by the analogous factors (eqs. 47 to 50). The differences are limited by a variant of the monotonised central limiter in characteristic variables ([LeVeque](#leveque-2002) 2002). As the projection back need not preserve monotonicity, they are monotonised again in primitive variables, now in a way that respects eq. 36. The limiter of [Colella and Sekora](#colella-sekora-2008) (2008), which preserves smooth extrema, had reached the Cartesian version of Athena but not its cylindrical one. A parabolic profile $a = a_{L,i} + s\Delta a_i + s(1 - s)a_{6,i}$ (eq. 51) has the coefficient $a_{6,i} = 6[a_i - a_{L,i} - \tfrac{1}{2}\Delta a_i(1 + \gamma_i)]$, equivalent to eq. 18 of [Blondin and Lufkin](#blondin-lufkin-1993) (1993) (eq. 52). The face values follow from the Cartesian formulae of [Colella and Woodward](#colella-woodward-1984) (1984, eq. 1.6) with every average $a_l$ replaced by $a_lR_l$ (eqs. 53, 54), which reproduces constant and linear profiles exactly and parabolic ones to the order required. After [Blondin and Lufkin](#blondin-lufkin-1993) (1993), both face values are set to $a_i$ where $a_i$ is an extremum (eq. 56). Where the parabola would create an extremum, one of them is reset, to $a_{L,i} = [6a_i - a_{R,i}(4 + 3\gamma_i)]/(2 - 3\gamma_i)$ or $a_{R,i} = [6a_i - a_{L,i}(4 - 3\gamma_i)]/(2 + 3\gamma_i)$, which for $\gamma_i = 0$ are the resets of [Colella and Woodward](#colella-woodward-1984) (1984, eq. 1.10) (eq. 57). The test for this case does not depend on the geometry (eq. 58). **[GK]** Eq. 58 as printed, $|a_{R,i} - a_{L,i}| \ge |a_{6,i}|$, is the condition under which the parabola has no extremum inside the cell, since $\Delta a_i + (1 - 2s)a_{6,i}$ then vanishes only outside $0 < s < 1$. The resets apply where $|a_{6,i}| > |a_{R,i} - a_{L,i}|$.

The interface states at $t^{n+1/2}$ follow by characteristic evolution after [Colella and Woodward](#colella-woodward-1984) (1984) and [Colella](#colella-1990) (1990), as averages over half a step of the solutions of the linearised system at the faces (§6). The left eigenvectors of $\mathsf{A}$ turn the system into independent advection equations for the characteristic variables, each moving at its eigenvalue $\lambda$ (eqs. 59 to 62). Each wave that reaches a face contributes the average of the reconstruction over its domain of dependence. In cylindrical coordinates this domain has, for the left state at $R_{i+1/2}$, the volume $(R_{i+1/2} - \lambda\Delta t/2)\lambda\Delta t\Delta\phi\Delta z$ (eqs. 63a, 63b). With $\chi = |\lambda|\Delta t/\Delta R$, the averages of the linear profile are $a_{R,i} - \tfrac{1}{2}\chi_R\Delta a_i(1 - \beta_{R,i})$ and $a_{L,i} + \tfrac{1}{2}\chi_L\Delta a_i(1 + \beta_{L,i})$, with the correction factors $\beta_{R,i} = \chi_R\Delta R/[6(R_{i+1/2} - \chi_R\Delta R/2)]$ and $\beta_{L,i} = \chi_L\Delta R/[6(R_{i-1/2} + \chi_L\Delta R/2)]$ (eqs. 64, 65). These vanish as $R \to \infty$ and equal $\gamma_i$ for $\chi = 1$. For the parabola, analogous averages reduce to eq. 1.12 of [Colella and Woodward](#colella-woodward-1984) (1984) as $\beta \to 0$ (eq. 66). The right eigenvectors sum the contributions back into primitive variables (eq. 67), as eq. 42 of [Stone et al.](#stone-et-al-2008) (2008) does in the Cartesian case (eq. 68). Second order for smooth flows requires all waves, including those that enter the cell from outside, and upwinding destroys it, as [Stone et al.](#stone-et-al-2008) (2008) noted. Near discontinuities, however, the full sum extrapolates the reconstruction of a cell beyond a discontinuity, with large errors. Athena in cylindrical coordinates therefore integrates only over the characteristics that reach each face from inside the cell, without the reference states, which the paper calls upwind-only integration (eqs. 69a, 69b). The sums then carry the factors $1 - \beta_R$ and $1 + \beta_L$ for PLM and the averages of eq. 66 for PPM.

Athena++ reconstructs without characteristic tracing (2.4.1), and in place of the corrected Cartesian formulae of Athena it follows [Mignone](#mignone-2014) (2014) throughout. [White, Stone and Gammie](#white-et-al-2016) (2016, §3.2) state its PLM, the modified van Leer limiter of [Mignone](#mignone-2014) (2014), which builds the states at $x_{i-1/2}$ from $q_{i-2}$ to $q_{i+1}$. It accounts for nonuniform spacing and for the difference between the centroids of volume and the geometric centres of cells, and it is proved TVD in all orthogonal coordinates. No such proof exists yet for non-orthogonal coordinates, for any method, and several methods in common use lose the TVD property already on orthogonal grids that are curvilinear or nonuniform. The slopes $\Delta_L$, $\Delta_C$ and $\Delta_R$ divide the differences of $q_{i-2}$ to $q_{i+1}$ by the distances between centroids, and $\Delta x_L$ and $\Delta x_R$ are the distances from the centroids of cells $i-1$ and $i$ to the face (eqs. 20, 21). The ratios $C^F$ and $C^B$ of a cell divide the distance from its centroid to that of its forward or backward neighbour by the distance from its centroid to the face between them. The left state is $q_{i-1}$ where $\Delta_L\Delta_C \le 0$, and otherwise $q_{i-1} + \Delta x_L\Delta_L\Delta_C(C^F_L\Delta_L + C^B_L\Delta_C)/[\Delta_L^2 + (C^F_L + C^B_L - 2)\Delta_L\Delta_C + \Delta_C^2]$, with the ratios of cell $i-1$. The right state follows in the same way from cell $i$, with $\Delta_C$, $\Delta_R$ and the ratios of that cell (eq. 22). The transverse field is reconstructed from its cell-centred values, and the longitudinal field needs no reconstruction. `Reconstruction::PiecewiseLinearX1` implements this limiter (2.4.3).

For PPM and the method of fourth order, the constructor of `Reconstruction` precomputes the weights of the face values in each direction (`src/reconstruct/reconstruction.cpp`, `Reconstruction::Reconstruction`). It marks as curvilinear the radius of cylindrical coordinates and the radius and polar angle of spherical-polar ones, and as nonuniform every direction with a ratio of widths other than one. A uniform Cartesian direction takes the weights of eq. 10, $(-1/12, 7/12, 7/12, -1/12)$ for the averages $q_{i-2}$ to $q_{i+1}$. A nonuniform one takes the coefficients of eq. 1.7 of [Colella and Woodward](#colella-woodward-1984) (1984), which follow from the widths of the neighbouring cells (2.4.5). Along a curvilinear direction, the four weights $w_s$ of the averages around a face at $x_f$ make the interpolation exact for the polynomials $x^0$ to $x^3$, $\sum_s w_s\langle x^n\rangle_s = x_f^n$, where the volume averages $\langle x^n\rangle_s$ carry the Jacobian of the coordinate ([Mignone](#mignone-2014) 2014). For the radius the Jacobian is $r^m$, with $m = 1$ in cylindrical and $m = 2$ in spherical-polar coordinates, and over a cell from $r_-$ to $r_+$, $\langle r^n\rangle = [(m+1)/(n+m+1)](r_+^{n+m+1} - r_-^{n+m+1})/(r_+^{m+1} - r_-^{m+1})$. For nonuniform radial spacing the system is solved at each face by LU decomposition with partial pivoting, and in the ghost cells at negative radius the weights are reversed. For uniform radial spacing the weights take closed forms after [Mignone](#mignone-2014) (2014), as functions of the index of the face counted from the first active face of the block. Along the polar angle the averages carry $\sin\theta$, and the system is always solved numerically. The overshoot test of the limiter of [Mignone](#mignone-2014) (2014) uses the ratios $(h_\pm + 1)/(h_\mp - 1)$ in place of the factor 2 of [Colella and Woodward](#colella-woodward-1984) (1984), which corresponds to $h_\pm = 3$ (2.4.5). For the radius $h_\pm = 3 \pm \Delta r/(2r_c)$ in cylindrical and $h_\pm = 3 + 2\Delta r(\pm 10r_c + \Delta r)/(20r_c^2 + \Delta r^2)$ in spherical-polar coordinates, with $r_c$ the midpoint of the cell, and for the polar angle $h_\pm$ follow from the sines and cosines of the face angles.

Two regression tests after [Mignone](#mignone-2014) (2014, §5.1.1, §5.1.2) advect a passive scalar radially, with cylindrical and with spherical-polar coordinates, and along the polar angle of the latter, with RK3 and PLM or PPM (`tst/regression/scripts/tests/scalars/mignone_radial_1d.py`, `tst/regression/scripts/tests/scalars/mignone_meridional_1d.py`; 2.8.7). Each runs two cases of the profile, A and B, on uniform grids of 32 to 2048 cells along the direction of advection. The $L_1$ errors must not exceed the values of [Mignone](#mignone-2014) (2014, Tables 1 and 4) by more than 0.5 per cent. For PLM the reference values come from Athena++ itself, as its van Leer limiter differs from the monotonised central limiter of [Mignone](#mignone-2014) (2014). So do those of PPM along the polar angle on 1024 and 2048 cells, where the two-dimensional version of the problem in Athena++ reaches an unexplained floor of the error, $3.45 \times 10^{-10}$ and $8.17 \times 10^{-11}$ in case A against $3.33 \times 10^{-10}$ and $2.42 \times 10^{-11}$ in the paper. Scripts in `tst/regression/scripts/utils/plot_mignone/` plot the profiles and the convergence, after Figures 2 to 4 of [Mignone](#mignone-2014) (2014).

#### 2.8.4 Finite volumes and geometric source terms

The finite volumes update volume averages with fluxes averaged over faces and over the step ([Skinner and Ostriker](#skinner-ostriker-2010) 2010, §7). In cylindrical coordinates the volume element is $R\,dR\,d\phi\,dz$, and with the volumes and areas of 2.8.1 the update reads $\mathbf{Q}^{n+1} = \mathbf{Q}^n - [\Delta t/(R_i\Delta R)](R_{i+1/2}\mathbf{F}_{R,i+1/2} - R_{i-1/2}\mathbf{F}_{R,i-1/2}) - [\Delta t/(R_i\Delta\phi)](\mathbf{F}_{\phi,j+1/2} - \mathbf{F}_{\phi,j-1/2}) - (\Delta t/\Delta z)(\mathbf{F}_{z,k+1/2} - \mathbf{F}_{z,k-1/2}) + \Delta t\,\mathbf{S}$, with the fluxes at $t^{n+1/2}$ and $\mathbf{S}$ the source terms averaged over the cell and the step (eq. 72). Applied to eq. 13 it would update the angular momentum. The radial part of the corresponding update of $\rho v_\phi$ by eq. 14, $-[\Delta t/(R_i^2\Delta R)](R_{i+1/2}^2F_{R,i+1/2} - R_{i-1/2}^2F_{R,i-1/2})$, needs no source term (eq. 73). Away from the origin and for smooth flows it agrees to second order with the update by eq. 11b, which needs the averaged source term $-M_{R\phi}/R = -(\rho v_Rv_\phi - B_RB_\phi)/R$ (eq. 74). The one geometric source term that remains, the average of $M_{\phi\phi}/R$ over the cell and the step (eq. 75), needs estimates centred in time for second order, not averages at $t^n$. Athena advances $\rho$ and $\rho v_\phi$ to the half step by finite volumes and $B_\phi$ by CT. Doing the same for the total pressure would be too costly, so its part comes from the pressures that the Riemann solver returns on the radial faces, averaged over the step, as $\langle P^*/R\rangle \approx (R_{i+1/2}P^*_{i+1/2} + R_{i-1/2}P^*_{i-1/2})/(2R_i^2)$ (eq. 76). Centrifugal balance proved easier to maintain with the analytic acceleration $\mathbf{g} = -\nabla\Phi$ in the momentum equation than with differences of the potential. Gravity then adds $\langle\rho\rangle^{n+1/2}\mathbf{g}(\langle\mathbf{x}\rangle)$, with $\mathbf{g}$ at the volume centroid, which is exact for solid-body rotation at uniform density, where $g \propto R$ (eq. 77). The energy keeps the conservative update by the potential that Athena already had. In the interface states, where gravity enters only the primitive momentum, $\mathbf{g}$ is taken at the area centroids of the faces, whose radii on the faces normal to $\phi$ and $z$ equal that of the volume centroid.

[Stone et al.](#stone-et-al-2020) (2020, §3.2.4) discretise the source terms of eqs. 16 and 17 so that Athena++ conserves angular momentum to machine precision. The source term of eq. 16 is written as $M_{R\phi}/R \approx (R_{i+1/2} - R_{i-1/2})(R_{i+1/2}M_{R\phi,i+1/2} + R_{i-1/2}M_{R\phi,i-1/2})/[(R_{i-1/2} + R_{i+1/2})V_R]$, with $V_R = (R_{i+1/2}^2 - R_{i-1/2}^2)/2$ and the stresses on the radial faces taken from the momentum fluxes of the Riemann solver that update the cell (eq. 18). The discrete equation, flux differences included, is then algebraically identical to the conservative difference of the angular momentum. In spherical-polar coordinates the first source term of eq. 17 takes a similar form, with $V_r = (r_{i+1/2}^3 - r_{i-1/2}^3)/3$. The second becomes $\cot\theta M_{\phi\theta}/r \approx (S_{j+1/2} - S_{j-1/2})(S_{j+1/2}M_{\phi\theta,j+1/2} + S_{j-1/2}M_{\phi\theta,j-1/2})/[r_i(S_{j-1/2} + S_{j+1/2})V_\theta]$, with $S = \sin\theta$, $V_\theta = \cos\theta_{j-1/2} - \cos\theta_{j+1/2}$ and the momentum fluxes on the faces normal to $\theta$ (eq. 19). The source terms of the remaining momentum components take the appropriate volume averages, and general relativity brings coordinate source terms of its own (2.14).

`Coordinates::AddCoordTermsDivergence` adds these terms to the conserved variables at each stage of the integrator, with the weight of the stage and with the primitive variables and fluxes of that stage (`src/task_list/time_integrator.cpp`; 2.6.5). In cylindrical coordinates it adds $\langle 1/R\rangle M_{\phi\phi}$ to the radial momentum, with $\langle 1/R\rangle = \Delta R/V_R = 1/R_i$ and $M_{\phi\phi} = \rho v_\phi^2 + P + (B_R^2 - B_\phi^2 + B_z^2)/2$ from the volume-centred primitive variables and field, where $P = c_s^2\rho$ for an isothermal gas (`src/coordinates/cylindrical.cpp`). It subtracts the term of eq. 18 from the azimuthal momentum. In spherical-polar coordinates the radial momentum gains $\langle 1/r\rangle(M_{\theta\theta} + M_{\phi\phi})$, with $\langle 1/r\rangle = \tfrac{3}{2}(r_{i+1/2}^2 - r_{i-1/2}^2)/(r_{i+1/2}^3 - r_{i-1/2}^3)$ and $M_{\theta\theta} + M_{\phi\phi} = \rho(v_\theta^2 + v_\phi^2) + 2P + B_r^2$ (`src/coordinates/spherical_polar.cpp`). The polar and azimuthal momenta lose radial terms in the form of eq. 18, with the weights $r_{i\pm1/2}^2$. The polar momentum gains $\langle 1/r\rangle\langle\cot\theta\rangle M_{\phi\phi}$, with $\langle\cot\theta\rangle = (\sin\theta_{j+1/2} - \sin\theta_{j-1/2})/V_\theta$, and the azimuthal momentum loses the term of eq. 19, with $\langle 1/r\rangle$ as the factor $1/r_i$, which the polar flux divergence carries as well. Without a polar direction in the mesh, this last term uses the volume-centred stress $M_{\phi\theta} = \rho v_\phi v_\theta - B_\phi B_\theta$ instead. The stresses of viscosity enter these terms as well, unless super time-stepping applies them, in which case `Coordinates::AddCoordTermsDivergence_STS` adds their part (2.11). Athena++ thus evaluates $M_{\phi\phi}$ at each stage from the volume-centred variables, where Athena advances them to the half step and takes the pressure from the fluxes. Gravity is a source term of its own (2.9). **[GK]** With the radial flux difference of `Hydro::AddFluxDivergence`, the term of eq. 18 gives the azimuthal momentum the radial update $-[\Delta t/(R_iV_R)](R_{i+1/2}^2F_{i+1/2} - R_{i-1/2}^2F_{i-1/2})$, which is eq. 73 of Athena, as $V_R = R_i\Delta R$.

#### 2.8.5 Constrained transport

CT carries over to cylindrical coordinates in its integral form, as the conservation of magnetic flux through the faces of a cell, which preserves the divergence constraint by construction ([Skinner and Ostriker](#skinner-ostriker-2010) 2010, §8). With $\partial_t\mathbf{B} + \nabla\times\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}} = 0$ and $\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}} = -\mathbf{v}\times\mathbf{B}$ (eq. 78), the theorem of Stokes on each face updates the area-averaged normal component of the field by the electric fields averaged along the edges and over the step (eqs. 79 to 81). On a radial face, $B_R$ changes by $-\Delta t/(R_{i\pm1/2}\Delta\phi)$ times the difference of $\mathcal{E}_z$ between the edges at $\phi_{j+1/2}$ and $\phi_{j-1/2}$, plus $\Delta t/\Delta z$ times that of $\mathcal{E}_\phi$ between the edges at $z_{k+1/2}$ and $z_{k-1/2}$. On an azimuthal face, $B_\phi$ changes by $\Delta t/\Delta R$ times the difference of $\mathcal{E}_z$ between $R_{i+1/2}$ and $R_{i-1/2}$, minus $\Delta t/\Delta z$ times that of $\mathcal{E}_R$ between $z_{k+1/2}$ and $z_{k-1/2}$. On a face normal to $z$, $B_z$ changes by $-\Delta t/(R_i\Delta R)$ times the difference of $R\mathcal{E}_\phi$ between $R_{i+1/2}$ and $R_{i-1/2}$, plus $\Delta t/(R_i\Delta\phi)$ times that of $\mathcal{E}_R$ between $\phi_{j+1/2}$ and $\phi_{j-1/2}$. The face averages are the primary representation of the field. The cell-centred field averages them, with the weights $R_{i\pm1/2}/(2R_i)$ for $B_R$, the average of second order in cylindrical coordinates, and with plain averages for $B_\phi$ and $B_z$ (eqs. 82a to 82c). Through consistency relations, these averages tie the Godunov fluxes of the cell-centred field to the corner electric fields, as in [Gardiner and Stone](#gardiner-stone-2005) (2005). **[GK]** Eq. 79c as printed lacks the radii in the difference of $\mathcal{E}_\phi$, which it gives as $\Delta t(\mathcal{E}_{\phi,i+1/2} - \mathcal{E}_{\phi,i-1/2})/(R_i\Delta R)$. The theorem of Stokes needs the lengths $R_{i\pm1/2}\Delta\phi$ of the azimuthal edges, which eq. 79a uses for the radial faces, and without them the divergence of the field would not be preserved.

The main change to the CTU+CT algorithm of [Gardiner and Stone](#gardiner-stone-2005) (2005) concerns the corner value of $\mathcal{E}_z$, which combines gradients along $\phi$ at different radii (§8.2). For $\mathcal{E}_R$ and $\mathcal{E}_\phi$ the gradients combined share their curvature, which cancels. As in 2.3.5, $\mathcal{E}_z$ is integrated to the corner from the four neighbouring face centres and averaged. At $(i-1/2, j-1/2)$ the average of the four face values is corrected by $R_{i-1/2}\Delta\phi/8$ times the difference of the azimuthal gradients below and above the corner, and by $\Delta R/8$ times that of the radial gradients on either side (eqs. 83, 84). Each gradient is taken from the upwind side, which the direction of the mass flux through the face centre decides, as for $\mathcal{E}^c_z$ (eq. 85). The azimuthal gradients are the differences between the face and centre values of $\mathcal{E}_z$ divided by $R_i\Delta\phi/2$ in cell $i$ and by $R_{i-1}\Delta\phi/2$ in cell $i-1$ (eqs. 86a, 86b), so that $\Delta\phi$ cancels but the ratios of the radii remain and must enter the algorithm. The scheme does not depend on the Riemann solver.

Athena++ applies CT with the areas of the faces and the lengths of the edges of 2.8.1, as `Field::CT` does in any coordinates (2.3.4). It carries the electric fields to the corners without geometric factors, with the same differences between face and centre values in every coordinate system, rather than with the ratios of radii of eqs. 86a and 86b (`src/field/calculate_corner_e.cpp`, `Field::ComputeCornerE`; 2.3.5). It also interpolates the cell-centred field linearly to the volume centre (2.3.4), where Athena weights $B_R$ by the radii of the faces.

#### 2.8.6 The integrator of Athena in cylindrical coordinates

[Skinner and Ostriker](#skinner-ostriker-2010) (2010, §3) recall the steps of Athena, from the interface states through fluxes of first order, CT of the states, transverse corrections and fluxes of second order to the updates of the field by CT and of the other variables by finite volumes. The Riemann solvers need no change to their wave speeds, eigenfunctions or fluxes, and the tests use HLLE, HLLC, HLLD and the solver of Roe (2.5). In cylindrical coordinates the six-solve CTU+CT of [Stone et al.](#stone-et-al-2008) (2008) takes thirteen steps (§9). First, the source terms of first order are computed at $t^n$ in conservative and primitive form: the geometric terms (eq. 75, and eq. 35 for the primitive variables; 2.8.3), gravity by eq. 77, and the source terms of MHD that follow from the divergence constraint (2.6.4). Reconstruction with characteristic evolution gives the interface states, and the primitive source terms of each direction are added to them, and the Riemann solver gives the fluxes of first order. The corner electric fields follow from these fluxes and from the cell-centred field at $t^n$, $\mathcal{E}_z$ by eqs. 84 and 85, and CT advances the face fields by half a step. The gradients of the transverse fluxes and the source terms of the transverse directions then correct the interface states. The density and momentum are advanced by half a step with the first-order fluxes and source terms, the half-step face fields are averaged to the cell centres by eq. 82, and the two give the cell-centred electric field at the half step. The corrected states yield the fluxes of second order and, with that electric field, the corner fields of second order. For the source terms at the half step, the density and azimuthal momentum are advanced once more with the new fluxes, and the total pressure follows from eq. 76. Together with $B_\phi$ at the half step they give eq. 75. Gravity in the momentum uses the density and $\mathbf{g}$, and gravity in the energy the mass fluxes on the faces. The full step then updates mass, momentum and energy by finite volumes and the face fields by CT (eq. 79), the cell-centred field follows from eq. 82, and the next step obeys the Courant condition with the width $R_i\Delta\phi$ along $\phi$, as the Riemann solvers return linear speeds. Hydrodynamics needs neither the source terms of MHD nor the magnetic parts of the geometric terms nor electric fields. Compared with the Cartesian code, the interface states change substantially along $R$, slightly along $\phi$ and not at all along $z$. The finite volumes gain their scale factors and geometric source terms, the source terms differ between the primitive and the conservative form, and the total pressure enters differently at $t^n$ and at the half step. The corner fields and the update by CT take the scale factors of 2.8.5.

#### 2.8.7 Tests in curvilinear coordinates

[Skinner and Ostriker](#skinner-ostriker-2010) (2010, §10) verify the cylindrical version of Athena with problems in one to three dimensions, in hydrodynamics and MHD, with smooth and discontinuous solutions of varying symmetry. Some come from tests of Cartesian codes ([Gardiner and Stone](#gardiner-stone-2005) 2005, [2008](#gardiner-stone-2008); [Londrillo and Del Zanna](#londrillo-del-zanna-2000) 2000; [Sakurai](#sakurai-1985) 1985) and some are new, and where possible the results are compared with the Cartesian code. Two magnetohydrostatic equilibria test the balance of forces (§10.1). In the first, the azimuthal field $B_\phi = B_0/R$, free of divergence, balances its outward pressure against its inward tension, with $B_0 = 1$, $\mathbf{v} = 0$, $\rho = 1$ and $P = 1$ (eq. 87). The error at $t = 10$, the root mean square over the variables of their mean absolute deviation from the initial state (eq. 88), converges at second order with HLLD and third-order reconstruction (Figure 1). It does so for Roe or HLLD, second or third order and the integrators in one to three dimensions alike, the latter run on grids with a few cells in the transverse directions. Only the orders of convergence of the integrators can be compared, as in several dimensions the volumes of the cells, and with them the time steps, change with $R$. Solid-body rotation $v_\phi = \Omega_0R$ balanced by the potential $\Phi = (\Omega_0R)^2/2$, and a constant $v_z \ne 0$, gave similar results. In the second, the radial field $B_R = B_0\cos\psi/R$, with $\psi = 2\pi(\phi - \phi_{\min})/(\phi_{\max} - \phi_{\min})$ so that all profiles are periodic in $\phi$, exerts no net azimuthal force together with $P = P_0 + B_0^2(1 + \sin^2\psi)/(2R^2)$. The potential $\Phi_1 = -B_0^2/(2\rho_0R^2)$ with $\rho = \rho_0(1 + \sin^2\psi)$ balances the radial gradient of the pressure (eqs. 89 to 92). With $B_0 = P_0 = \rho_0 = 1$ and solid-body rotation at $\Omega_0 = \pi/4$ balanced by $\Phi_2 = (\Omega_0R)^2/2$, on $N \times N$ cells over $R \in [1, 2]$ and $\phi \in [0, \pi/4]$, periodic in $\phi$ and $z$ and with fixed values in $R$, the error at $t = 10$ again converges at second order (Figure 2). Boundaries of Neumann type in $R$ gave similar results.

The stability of rotating discs, with the integrator in two dimensions, tests how well angular momentum is conserved close to the stability limit that the criterion of Rayleigh sets (§10.2). By this criterion a rotation $\Omega(R)$ is stable to axisymmetric, infinitesimal disturbances where $(R^2\Omega)^2$ increases outward (eq. 93). Laboratory flows of Couette type at Reynolds numbers up to $2 \times 10^6$ that violate it show strong turbulent transport of angular momentum, while flows that satisfy it show none ([Ji et al.](#ji-et-al-2006) 2006). For $\Omega \propto R^{-q}$ the criterion predicts stability for $q < 2$, so that Keplerian rotation, with $q = 1.5$, is stable. The test sets $v_\phi = \Omega_0R^{1-q}$ on a uniform background with a potential in rotational equilibrium, and perturbs $v_\phi$ by random values uniform in $[-\epsilon, \epsilon]$, with $\epsilon$ of order $10^{-4}$ and the same perturbation for each $q$ (eqs. 94, 95). The grid has $200 \times 400$ cells over $[3, 7] \times [0, \pi/2]$, with $\rho_0 = 200$, $P_0 = 1$ and $\gamma = 5/3$, so that $c_s \approx 0.09$. With $\Omega_0 = 2\pi$, $v_\phi$ falls to about 0.9, and the Mach numbers of about 10 to 20 make the flow dominated by rotation. The mean perturbed flux of angular momentum, $\langle R\rho v_R\,\delta v_\phi\rangle$ scaled by $\langle RP\rangle$ (eq. 96), stays at the level of the perturbation for $q < 2$ and grows exponentially for $q > 2$ over $t \in [0, 300]$ (Figure 3). For $q = 2.05$ the instability saturates around $t = 90$, after which the mass leaves the grid. Without perturbation, the cumulative mean of the background flux of angular momentum, which measures the rate of radial accretion (eq. 97), stays small for $q = 1$, typical of galactic discs, and for the Keplerian $q = 1.5$ (Figure 4). For $q = 1$ the velocity $v_\phi$ is constant and so reconstructed exactly.

A strong hydrodynamic blast wave with the parameters of [Gardiner and Stone](#gardiner-stone-2008) (2008) compares the cylindrical with the Cartesian version (§10.3). The Cartesian domain is $[-0.5, 0.5] \times [-0.75, 0.75]$ and the cylindrical one $[1, 2] \times [-0.5, 0.5]$ in $(R, \phi)$, whose arc at $R = 1.5$ has the same length of 1.5. A circle of radius 0.1 with $P = 10$ lies in a medium with $P_0 = 0.1$ and $\rho_0 = 1$, on $200 \times 300$ cells, with third-order reconstruction and HLLC with upwind-only integration of the states. At $t = 0.2$ the contours and the profiles through the centre of the two versions agree excellently (Figures 5, 6). The Cartesian code keeps the symmetry perfectly, as its grid noise inside the shell shows, and the cylindrical one keeps it well on its nonuniform grid. A steady, axisymmetric, rotating wind in the potential $\Phi_g = -GM/R$ tests the transport of angular momentum through a sonic transition (§10.4). It conserves the entropy $K = P\rho^{-\gamma}$, the mass flux $\dot{M} = R\rho v_R$ and the specific angular momentum $J = Rv_\phi$, and along streamlines the Bernoulli function $v^2/2 + h + \Phi_g$, with the enthalpy $h = c_s^2/(\gamma - 1)$ (eqs. 98 to 102). Scaled to the density and sound speed at infinity and to the Bondi radius $R_B = GM/c_\infty^2$, the mass flux $\lambda = \dot{M}/(R_B\rho_\infty c_\infty)$ and the angular momentum $\omega = J/(R_Bc_\infty)$ enter a dimensionless Bernoulli equation for the radial Mach number $M_R$ (eqs. 103 to 108). For $\omega = 0$ it describes a cylindrical counterpart of the spherical wind of Parker. The transonic solutions pass through saddle points of X type at the sonic point, $M_R = 1$, at the larger root $\chi_+$ of a quadratic in $\chi = R/R_B$, while the smaller root $\chi_-$ is a point of O type. They exist for $0 < \omega < \omega_{\max} = (3 - \gamma)/(4\tilde{B}^{1/2})$ and above a minimum of $\chi$, with the critical mass flux $\lambda_c$ (eqs. 109 to 111). With $\gamma = 5/3$, $\omega = 0.3$ and the scaled Bernoulli constant $\tilde{B} = 1$, $\chi_+ \approx 0.479$ and $\lambda_c \approx 1.377$. The domain $\chi \in [\chi_-, 2]$, with $\chi_- \approx 0.188$, is initialised by solving eq. 108 for $M_R$ by bisection to a tolerance of $10^{-10}$, in units with $GM = c_\infty = 1$, and the boundaries hold the solution fixed. The error at $t = 5$ converges at second order with Roe and PLM in one dimension, and alike with HLLC, PPM and the integrators in two and three dimensions (Figure 8).

A weak field loop is advected in two and three dimensions, along $\phi$ only, unlike the oblique advection of the Cartesian test (§10.5). The domain $[1, 2] \times [-2/3, 2/3]$ in $(R, \phi)$ has the area of the Cartesian one, with periodic boundaries in $\phi$ and fixed ones in $R$. The fluid has $\rho_0 = P_0 = 1$ and $v_\phi = \Omega_0R$ with $\Omega_0 = 4/3$, so that the loop crosses the grid once by $t = 1$. The field follows from $A_z = A_0(a_0 - r)$ within the distance $a_0 = 0.3$ of the centre of the loop, with $A_0 = 10^{-3}$, so that $P/B^2 = 10^6$ inside and the loop is carried passively (eq. 112). The magnetic energy and the field lines, the contours of $A_z$, keep their circular shape from $t = 0$ to $t = 2$ (Figure 9). The mean magnetic energy decays as $A[1 - (t/\tau)^\alpha]$ for $t \ll \tau$, with $A = 7.02 \times 10^{-8}$, $\tau = 1.46 \times 10^4$, $\alpha = 0.342$ and a residual of 0.0580 (Figure 10). This is less dissipation than [Gardiner and Stone](#gardiner-stone-2005) (2005) found for the oblique Cartesian advection, with $\tau = 1.06 \times 10^4$ and $\alpha = 0.291$. Blast waves in a strong field, of plasma beta $\beta_p = 2P/B^2$, compare the two versions in two and three dimensions (§10.6). With $B_0 = 1$ and $\beta_p = 0.2$, after [Gardiner and Stone](#gardiner-stone-2008) (2008), the hydrodynamic setup above runs with HLLD and a uniform field at 45 degrees to the $x$- or $R$-axis. Although in cylindrical coordinates the angle between the field and the grid changes with $\phi$, the solution stays highly symmetric (Figures 11, 12), and a three-dimensional version on $200^3$ cells with $z \in [-0.5, 0.5]$ agrees as well (Figure 13). With $B_0 = 10$ along $x$ and $\beta_p = 0.02$, after [Londrillo and Del Zanna](#londrillo-del-zanna-2000) (2000), a circle of radius 0.125 with $P = 100$ lies in a medium with $P_0 = 1$ and $\rho_0 = 1$. The Cartesian version covers $[-0.5, 0.5]^2$, both use $200^2$ cells, third-order reconstruction and HLLD with upwind-only integration, and the profiles through the centre at $t = 0.02$ agree, also in three dimensions on $200^3$ cells (Figures 14 to 16).

A cylindrical version of the wind of Weber and Davis, after [Sakurai](#sakurai-1985) (1985), tests a steady, axisymmetric MHD flow with a planar field in the potential $-GM/R$ (§10.7). It conserves $K$, $\dot{M}$, $f = RB_R$ and $\beta = B_R/(\rho v_R)$, as well as the angular velocity $\Omega = [v_\phi - B_\phi/(\beta\rho)]/R$ of the frame in which the field lies parallel to the flow and exerts no force on it. It also conserves the angular momentum $J = R(v_\phi - \beta B_\phi)$ of fluid and field, and a Bernoulli function in that frame that includes the centrifugal potential (eqs. 113a to 113c). At the Alfvén point, where the Alfvén Mach number in that frame is one, $\beta = \rho_A^{-1/2}$ and $J = R_A^2\Omega$. In the scaled radius $x = R/R_A$ and density $y = \rho/\rho_A = M_A^{-2}$, the Bernoulli function depends on three parameters $\eta$, $\theta$ and $\omega$ (eqs. 114, 115), and wind solutions must make it stationary in both $x$ and $y$ at the slow and at the fast magnetosonic point (eqs. 116a, 116b). For given $\theta$ and $\omega$ these are six equations for $\eta$, the scaled Bernoulli constant $\tilde{B}$ and the positions of the two points, with a unique solution if any. With the parameters of the solar wind of Weber and Davis that [Sakurai](#sakurai-1985) (1985) uses, $\gamma = 1.2$, $\theta = 1.5$ and $\omega = 0.3$, the solution has $\eta = 2.3609$, $\tilde{B} = 7.8745$, $(x_s, y_s) = (0.5243, 2.4986)$ and $(x_f, y_f) = (1.6383, 0.5374)$ (Table 1). On $x \in [0.4, 1.8]$, which contains all three critical points, with $GM = 1$ and the boundaries held fixed, the error at $t = 5$ converges at second order with Roe, PLM and the integrator in one dimension (Figure 17). It does so alike for HLLD, PPM and the integrators in two and three dimensions, while angular momentum passes between fluid and field.

[Skinner and Ostriker](#skinner-ostriker-2010) (2010, §11) conclude that the CTU and CT of Athena carry over to cylindrical coordinates through changes to the reconstructions of second and third order, to the finite volumes and finite areas and through geometric source terms, without changes to most of the code, the Riemann solvers and eigensystems in particular. The approach could extend to spherical coordinates in Athena and to other Godunov codes of higher order. The code and its tests were public and in use for studies of global accretion discs.

[Stone et al.](#stone-et-al-2020) (2020, §3.5) present tests of curvilinear coordinates, with and without mesh refinement, as a new capability of Athena++. In the radial advection of [Mignone](#mignone-2014) (2014, §5.1.1), a passive scalar with a Gaussian profile, of width and centre set by the parameters $a$ and $b$, moves with a linear velocity field, with cylindrical or spherical-polar coordinates (§3.5.1, Figure 26). PPM clips the smooth extrema, as curvilinear coordinates use a variant of the original limiter rather than the one that preserves smooth extrema on Cartesian grids, an extension left for later work. The radial problem and a two-dimensional counterpart of the meridional one of [Mignone](#mignone-2014) (2014, §5.1.2) converge at second order with PLM and at fourth order with PPM (Figures 27, 28). The errors of PLM differ slightly from those of [Mignone](#mignone-2014) (2014), whose modified monotonised central limiter errs less than the van Leer limiter in the tests that are not monotone and more in those that are. The problem generator sets a uniform background of unit density that is not evolved, as the parameter `active` of the block `<hydro>` is `background` (`src/pgen/mignone_advection.cpp`). Its velocity is radial, $v_r = \alpha r$, or polar in the meridional case, $v_\theta = \alpha r\theta$, set from the exact cell averages. The radial profile is $\exp[-a^2(r - b)^2]$, and the meridional one $[\tfrac{1}{2} + \tfrac{1}{2}\cos(a(\theta - b))]^2$ within $|\theta - b| < \pi/a$ and zero outside. At time $t$ the exact solution is the initial profile at $re^{-\alpha t}$, multiplied by $e^{-(m+1)\alpha t}$ with $m = 1$ or 2, or at $\theta e^{-\alpha t}$, multiplied by $e^{-\alpha t}\sin(\theta e^{-\alpha t})/\sin\theta$. Gauss–Legendre quadrature with 12 points of the profiles, weighted by the Jacobian, gives the cell averages of the initial state and of the exact solution. At the end the scalars are compared with the latter in the $L_1$ norm weighted by volume and in the maximum norm, and the errors written to a file (`Mesh::UserWorkAfterLoop`). The input files run the radial case to $t = 1$ on 64 cells over $r \in [0, 2]$, reflecting at $r = 0$, with RK3, PLM and a Courant number of 0.9 (`inputs/hydro/athinput.mignone_radial`). The meridional case runs on 32 cells over $\theta \in [0, \pi/2]$, reflecting at the pole, in a thin shell of 4 cells around $r = 11$, with VL2, and both take $a = 10$ and $b = 0$ (`inputs/hydro/athinput.mignone_meridional`). The regression tests of 2.8.3 run both.

Carrying a field loop across the pole tests the coordinate singularity, near which the truncation error is nonuniform and the ghost cells across the pole must take the data from the opposite azimuth (§3.5.2). A uniform flow $v_x = 1$, perpendicular to the polar axis of a spherical-polar mesh, carries a passive loop with the vector potential $A_z = B_0\exp[-(z - z_0)^2/\sigma^2]\max(R - [(x - x_0)^2 + (y - y_0)^2]^{1/2}, 0)$, after [Gardiner and Stone](#gardiner-stone-2005) (2005), centred at $(x_0, y_0, z_0) = (-\sqrt{2}/2, 0, \sqrt{2}/2)$ (eq. 29). The loop has the radius $R = 0.5$, the thickness $\sigma = 0.2$ and $\beta = 2p/B_0^2 = 10^5$ at its mid-plane. PLM, HLLD and an adiabatic gas with $\gamma = 5/3$ are used on $0.1 < r < 2$, $0 < \theta < \pi/2$ and $0 < \phi < 2\pi$, with $160 \times 80 \times 160$ cells, logarithmic in $r$. The loop diffuses numerically, most at its centre, where oppositely directed field lines lie close together, but it keeps its structure through the highly anisotropic cells and the singularity (Figure 29). The problem generator in the copy differs from this description (`src/pgen/field_loop_poles.cpp`). Its loop has a top-hat profile over $|z - z_c| < 0.2$ instead of the Gaussian, and its uniform flow, of speed `vy0`, runs along $y$. The field strength follows from $\beta$ and the pressure $\rho_0c_s^2$, and the components $A_r = A_z\cos\theta$ and $A_\theta = -A_z\sin\theta$ are evaluated on the edges, as averages over two points next to finer neighbours. They give the face fields by the theorem of Stokes, skipping the faces on the pole. Boundary functions of the problem hold the density, the flow and the pressure in the radial ghost cells and those of the outer polar boundary, and copy the field from the nearest active faces. With adaptive refinement, blocks are refined where the field exceeds $0.2B_0$ and coarsened where it stays below $0.1B_0$. The input file centres the loop at $(x_c, y_c, z_c) = (0, 1, 0.5)$ with $\beta = 10^5$, $\rho_0 = 1$, $c_s = 0.1$ and `vy0` $= -1$, on $32 \times 16 \times 64$ cells with a radial ratio of 1.0981, a polar boundary at $\theta = 0$ and periodic azimuth, and it runs to $t = 3.14$ with VL2 and PLM (`inputs/mhd/athinput.field_loop_poles`). A second file checks the divergence of the field under adaptive refinement, with blocks of $8 \times 4 \times 8$ cells, two levels, refinement forced at cycle 30 and 60 cycles in all (`inputs/mhd/athinput.field_loop_poles_checkdivb`). Its history records the sum of $|\nabla\cdot\mathbf{B}|$, scaled by the field strength and the smallest edge of each cell, over the magnetised cells and their number.

To show mesh refinement in curvilinear coordinates, [Stone et al.](#stone-et-al-2020) (2020, §3.5.3) rerun the blast waves of their §3.4.4, hydrodynamic and with a field, in spherical-polar coordinates (2.16, 3.4). The domain is $0.5 < r < 1.5$, $\pi/6 < \theta < \pi/2$ and $-\pi/5 < \phi < \pi/5$, with radial spacing that keeps the cells close to unit aspect ratio, and the initial field is uniform and parallel to the polar axis. The blasts stay spherically symmetric and agree closely with the Cartesian results (Figures 30, 31). Their pressure against the distance from the centre follows the solution of Sedov and Taylor, with a peak slightly smeared by the larger radial cells at larger radii (Figure 32). The regression tests `blast_cyl` and `blast_sph` run the hydrodynamic blast of the problem generator `blast` with cylindrical and with spherical-polar coordinates (`tst/regression/scripts/tests/curvilinear/blast_cyl.py`, `tst/regression/scripts/tests/curvilinear/blast_sph.py`; 2.16). A sphere of radius 0.25 with 100 times the ambient pressure of 0.1 is centred at $(R, \phi, z) = (2, 0, 0)$ on $64^3$ cells over $R \in [1, 3]$, $\phi \in [-\pi/6, \pi/6]$ and $z \in [-1, 1]$, run to $t = 0.3$ (`inputs/hydro/athinput.blast_cyl`). In spherical-polar coordinates it is centred at $(r, \theta, \phi) = (2, \pi/3, 0)$ on $64 \times 64 \times 96$ cells over $r \in [1, 3]$, $\theta \in [\pi/6, \pi/2]$ and $\phi \in [-\pi/4, \pi/4]$, run to $t = 0.4$ (`inputs/hydro/athinput.blast_sph`). Both use VL2, PLM, a Courant number of 0.3, $\gamma = 5/3$ and outflow boundaries. At the end the problem generator finds the maxima of the pressure along the coordinate lines through the centre and through the six cells ten cells away from it along the axes (`src/pgen/blast.cpp`, `Mesh::UserWorkAfterLoop`). It converts their positions to Cartesian distances from the centre and divides the range of these by the largest physical width of the central cell. The tests require this distortion not to exceed one.

Two further problem generators and a utility serve these and other tests. `slotted_cylinder` advects a slotted cylinder of passive scalar, after [Zalesak](#zalesak-1979) (1979, §VII) and [Colella et al.](#colella-et-al-2011) (2011, §4.4.5), by rigid rotation at the angular velocity $2\pi\omega$ about a point of the plane (`src/pgen/slotted_cylinder.cpp`; 2.7.2). The cylinder has the radius 0.15 and a slot of width 0.05 that rises 0.25 from its lowest point. Gauss–Legendre quadrature in two dimensions gives the cell averages and the errors at the end, and with adaptive refinement blocks refine on the largest gradient of the scalars. The input files place the cylinder at $(0, 0.25)$ on $100 \times 100$ cells over the periodic square $[-0.5, 0.5]^2$, rotating once about the origin by $t = 1$, with VL2, PLM and a Courant number of 0.4 (`inputs/hydro/athinput.slotted_cylinder2d`). The refined variant adds blocks of $4 \times 4$ cells, two regions of static refinement over the cylinder and parameters of adaptive refinement, which act once `refinement` of the block `<mesh>` is changed from `none` (`inputs/hydro/athinput.slotted_cylinder2d_refined`). `disk` sets up a Keplerian disc in vertical hydrostatic equilibrium about the point mass $GM$ of the source terms (2.9), in cylindrical or spherical-polar coordinates (`src/pgen/disk.cpp`). With $P/\rho = (p_0/\rho_0)(R/r_0)^{p}$, slopes $d$ and $p$ of density and temperature, and $r = (R^2 + z^2)^{1/2}$, the density is $\rho_0(R/r_0)^{d}\exp[(GM\rho/P)(1/r - 1/R)]$, above a floor. The rotation is $v_\phi = (GM/R)^{1/2}[(d + p)(P/\rho)/(GM/R) + 1 + p - pR/r]^{1/2}$, less the rotation of the frame or the velocity of orbital advection where these are used. Boundary functions of the problem keep the initial state in the ghost cells. The input files run 100 orbits at $r_0 = 1$, to $t = 628$, with $GM = r_0 = \rho_0 = 1$, $p_0/\rho_0 = 0.01$, $p = -0.5$ and $d = -2.25$, on 64 cells over $R$ or $r \in [0.5, 2]$ with a ratio of 1.0219 and 256 cells in azimuth, the spherical one with 32 cells over $\theta \in [1.2458, 1.8958]$ (`inputs/hydro/athinput.disk_cyl`, `inputs/hydro/athinput.disk_sph`). `GaussLegendre::integrate` evaluates integrals over intervals, rectangles and boxes of the coordinates by tensor products of rules with 2 to 64 points, whose abscissae and weights the file tabulates, and the problem generators put the Jacobian into the integrand (`src/utils/gl_quadrature.cpp`).

### 2.9 Source terms, the shearing box and orbital advection

#### 2.9.1 Physical source terms

Athena++ keeps the physical source terms of hydrodynamics apart from the geometric ones of the coordinates (2.8.4), in the class `HydroSourceTerms` that each `Hydro` holds (`src/hydro/srcterms/hydro_srcterms.hpp`). Its constructor reads the parameters and decides which terms apply (`src/hydro/srcterms/hydro_srcterms.cpp`, `HydroSourceTerms::HydroSourceTerms`). The mass `GM` of the block `<problem>` places a point mass at the origin. It requires cylindrical or spherical-polar coordinates, and in cylindrical coordinates with a resolved $z$ also orbital advection, whose own source terms then include it (2.9.4). The accelerations `grav_acc1` to `grav_acc3` of the block `<hydro>` are constant along the coordinate directions. A frequency `Omega0` of the block `<orbital_advection>` selects the source terms of orbital advection where that is enabled, those of the shearing box in Cartesian coordinates with a shear rate `qshear`, and otherwise those of a rotating frame in cylindrical or spherical-polar coordinates (2.9.2 to 2.9.4). Self-gravity and a function that the problem generator enrols with `Mesh::EnrollUserExplicitSourceFunction` add further terms (2.10, 3.6). `HydroSourceTerms::AddSourceTerms` applies the terms in this order to the conserved variables, the function of the user to the passive scalars as well. The integrator calls it in each main stage, with the weight of the stage, the primitive variables and fluxes of the stage and the time at its start, unless the fluid is not evolved (`src/task_list/time_integrator.cpp`, `TimeIntegratorTaskList::AddSourceTerms`; 2.6.5).

`HydroSourceTerms::PointMass` subtracts $\Delta t\,\rho\,\langle 1/r\rangle\,GM/r_v$ from the radial momentum of each cell, with the volume average $\langle 1/r\rangle$ of 2.8.4 and the radial volume centre $r_v$, an approximation of $\rho GM/r^2$ (`src/hydro/srcterms/pointmass.cpp`). For a non-barotropic gas the energy loses the work of gravity, computed from the radial mass fluxes $F_\rho$ on the two faces of the cell. In cylindrical coordinates this work is $\tfrac{1}{2}GM[F_{\rho,i-1/2}/(R_vR_{i-1/2}) + F_{\rho,i+1/2}/(R_vR_{i+1/2})]$ and in spherical-polar coordinates $\tfrac{1}{2}GM(F_{\rho,i-1/2} + F_{\rho,i+1/2})/r_v^2$, each times $\Delta t$. In cylindrical coordinates the point mass thus acts with the cylindrical radius alone, in the plane $z = 0$. `HydroSourceTerms::ConstantAcceleration` adds $\Delta t\,\rho g_n$ to the momentum along each direction $n$ with a nonzero $g_n$, and $\Delta t\,\rho g_nv_n$ to the energy, from the primitive variables of the stage (`src/hydro/srcterms/constant_acc.cpp`). The accelerations act along the coordinate directions, which are fixed in space only in Cartesian coordinates, and they serve problems such as the Rayleigh–Taylor instability.

`HydroSourceTerms::RotatingSystemSourceTerms` adds the Coriolis and centrifugal forces of a frame that rotates at $\Omega_0$ about the polar axis (`src/hydro/srcterms/rotating_system_srcterms.cpp`). In cylindrical coordinates, with the speed of the frame $v_c = R_v\Omega_0$ at the volume centre, the radial momentum gains $\Delta t\langle 1/R\rangle(2v_c\rho v_\phi + \rho v_c^2)$, the Coriolis force $2\rho\Omega_0v_\phi$ and the centrifugal force $\rho\Omega_0^2R$. The azimuthal momentum loses $\Delta t\langle 1/R\rangle v_c(\rho v_R + \bar{F}_\rho)$, the Coriolis force $-2\rho\Omega_0v_R$ with half of the radial momentum taken from the cell and half from the mean $\bar{F}_\rho$ of the radial mass fluxes on its two faces. A non-barotropic energy gains the work of the centrifugal force, $\Delta t\langle 1/R\rangle v_c^2\bar{F}_\rho$, from the same fluxes, as for the point mass. In spherical-polar coordinates $v_c = r_v\sin\theta_v\,\Omega_0$, and with $f = \rho\langle 1/r\rangle(2v_cv_\phi + v_c^2)$ the radial momentum gains $\Delta t\,f$ and the polar momentum $\Delta t\,f\langle\cot\theta\rangle$, with $\langle\cot\theta\rangle$ of 2.8.4. The azimuthal momentum loses $\Delta t\langle 1/r\rangle v_c$ times the sum of $\rho v_r$ and the mean radial mass flux, plus $\langle\cot\theta\rangle$ times the sum of $\rho v_\theta$ and the mean polar mass flux. The energy gains $\Delta t\,v_c^2/r_v$ times the mean radial mass flux plus $\langle\cot\theta\rangle$ times the mean polar one. These terms are the Coriolis force $-2\rho\,\boldsymbol{\Omega}_0\times\mathbf{v}$, the centrifugal force $\rho\Omega_0^2\varpi$ along the cylindrical radius $\varpi = r\sin\theta$, and the work of the latter. Spherical-polar meshes in one dimension are refused.

#### 2.9.2 The shearing box

[Stone and Gardiner](#stone-gardiner-2010) (2010) describe the shearing box of Athena for the dynamics of accretion discs (abstract). The Coriolis and tidal terms of the momentum equation are differenced in time by the method of Crank and Nicolson, for accuracy and stability, which conserves the energy of epicyclic oscillations in hydrodynamics to round-off. The tidal terms of the energy are written as differences of an effective potential, which keeps the total energy, potential energy included, exact to round-off as well. An orbital advection for MHD built on CT keeps the field free of divergence, frees the time step from the orbital velocity and evens out the truncation error across radius (2.9.4). The radial boundaries need corrections to conserve the net vertical magnetic flux (2.9.3). Similar corrections would be needed for mass, momentum and energy, but with orbital advection these are conserved to better than 0.03 per cent over hundreds of orbits. Much of what is known about the saturation of the MRI comes from simulations of MHD, many of them in the shearing box, the local approximation of [Hill](#hill-1878) (1878) that [Hawley, Gammie and Balbus](#hawley-et-al-1995) (1995) introduced to studies of the MRI (§1). The equations are written in a Cartesian frame corotating with the disc at a radius $r_0$, valid where the domain is small compared with $r_0$. The approximation cannot give global properties such as the net accretion rate, the radial profiles of density and temperature or the emitted spectrum, but it serves as a laboratory for the local dynamics of discs. Most earlier simulations of the MRI used operator-split methods such as ZEUS or simple finite differences, to which the source terms are easily added, while Godunov methods of higher order in conservative form had begun to be applied. The C and Fortran versions of Athena differ here, and the paper describes the C version, whose methods were first introduced by [Gardiner and Stone](#gardiner-stone-2006) (2006) and used for hydrodynamic shearing waves by [Shen, Stone and Gardiner](#shen-et-al-2006) (2006). They preserve the energy of epicyclic motion and the total energy to round-off and alias trailing into leading waves hardly at all, a gain that can be shown quantitatively only for hydrodynamics, as a weak field makes epicyclic motion unstable. [Gressel and Ziegler](#gressel-ziegler-2007) (2007) adopted and extended similar methods.

In the frame at $r_0$ that corotates at $\Omega_0 = \Omega(r_0)$, with Cartesian coordinates $(x, y, z)$ and unit vectors $\hat{\mathbf{i}}$, $\hat{\mathbf{j}}$ and $\hat{\mathbf{k}}$, the equations of ideal MHD gain source terms (§2). The momentum equation gains $\rho\Omega_0^2(2qx\hat{\mathbf{i}} - z\hat{\mathbf{k}}) - 2\Omega_0\hat{\mathbf{k}}\times\rho\mathbf{v}$, the tidal and the vertical gravity of the rotating frame and the Coriolis force, and the energy equation gains the work of the former, $\Omega_0^2\rho\mathbf{v}\cdot(2qx\hat{\mathbf{i}} - z\hat{\mathbf{k}})$ (eqs. 1 to 6). The shear parameter $q = -\tfrac{1}{2}\,d\ln\Omega^2/d\ln r$ is $3/2$ for Keplerian flow, units with $\mu = 1$ are used, and an ideal gas is assumed, although the techniques generalise to other equations of state (eq. 7). The equations admit uniform orbital motion, $\mathbf{v}_K = -q\Omega_0x\,\hat{\mathbf{j}}$, independent of time, varying only with $x$ and directed along $y$, properties on which orbital advection rests (eq. 8). Most difficulties come from the tidal and Coriolis terms in the orbital plane, so the horizontal and vertical source terms are treated apart.

Source terms split from the flux divergence leave ordinary differential equations that any method can integrate, as the Fortran version does (§3). When hydrodynamic turbulence was left to decay in the shearing box, however, the kinetic energy of the fluctuations grew at late times instead of decaying, and the growth was traced to the truncation error of the source terms, which amplified epicyclic motions. With the fluctuations of momentum $m = \rho v_x$ and $n = \rho(v_y + q\Omega_0x)$, a stress-free medium obeys $\partial m/\partial t = 2\Omega_0n$ and $\partial n/\partial t = (q - 2)\Omega_0m$, which conserve $m^2 + 2n^2/(2 - q)$, for Keplerian flow the epicyclic energy $(m^2 + 4n^2)/(2\rho)$ (eqs. 9 to 13). Time differences of Crank and Nicolson, $[m] = 2\Omega_0\delta t\,\bar{n}$ and $[n] = (q - 2)\Omega_0\delta t\,\bar{m}$ with $[x] = x^{n+1} - x^n$ and $\bar{x} = (x^{n+1} + x^n)/2$, conserve this energy exactly in the discrete sense (eqs. 14 to 18). The Coriolis force does no work, and a discretisation that is not orthogonal to the velocity on average in time spuriously changes the epicyclic energy. Forward Euler makes the oscillations grow and backward Euler makes them decay, while their average conserves them, much as forward Euler makes orbits in a central potential spiral outward. This discretisation is the default of Athena in hydrodynamics and MHD.

For the momentum, the fluctuations obey conservation laws with the source terms $2\Omega_0n$ and $(q - 2)\Omega_0m$ and the fluxes $\mathbf{F}_m = \mathbf{F}_{\rho v_x}$ and $\mathbf{F}_n = \mathbf{F}_{\rho v_y} + q\Omega_0x\mathbf{F}_\rho$ (§3.1, eqs. 19 to 22). Finite volumes with the source terms averaged between $t^n$ and $t^{n+1}$ (eqs. 23, 24) are solved for $m^{n+1}$ and $n^{n+1}$. With $m^* = m^n - (\delta t/2)\nabla\cdot\mathbf{F}_m$ and $n^* = n^n - (\delta t/2)\nabla\cdot\mathbf{F}_n$, the updates add to the flux differences $[4\Omega_0\delta t/(2 + (2 - q)(\Omega_0\delta t)^2)][n^* + \tfrac{1}{2}(q - 2)\Omega_0\delta t\,m^*]$ for $m$ and $[2(q - 2)\Omega_0\delta t/(2 + (2 - q)(\Omega_0\delta t)^2)](m^* + \Omega_0\delta t\,n^*)$ for $n$ (eqs. 25, 26). The cell averages relate as $m_{ijk} = (\rho v_x)_{ijk}$ and $n_{ijk} = (\rho v_y)_{ijk} + q\Omega_0\rho_{ijk}x_i$, and the divergence of $\mathbf{F}_n$ follows from those of $\mathbf{F}_{\rho v_y}$ and $\mathbf{F}_\rho$ (eqs. 27 to 32). With the continuity equation, the update of $\rho v_y$ thus carries the term $-\tfrac{1}{2}q\Omega_0\delta t[(F_\rho)_{x,i-1/2} + (F_\rho)_{x,i+1/2}]$ from the radial mass fluxes (eq. 33), and $\rho v_x$ is updated by eq. 25. The CTU+CT integrator also needs the source terms elsewhere, each added by forward Euler for $\delta t/2$, which suffices: in the interface states of the velocity at the faces normal to $x$, before their conversion to conserved variables, in the transverse flux gradients along $x$ that correct the interface states of the other directions, and in the velocities at the half step that give the cell-centred electric field of CT. **[GK]** Eqs. 32 and 33 as printed multiply the sum of the mass fluxes by $x_i$. With $x_{i\pm1/2} = x_i \pm \delta x/2$, the divergence of $x\mathbf{F}_\rho$ is $x_i\nabla\cdot\mathbf{F}_\rho + [(F_\rho)_{x,i-1/2} + (F_\rho)_{x,i+1/2}]/2$, so that the coefficient of the sum is $q\Omega_0/2$, as the units of the term also require.

For the energy, the effective potential $\Phi_{SB} = -q\Omega_0^2x^2 + \tfrac{1}{2}\Omega_0^2z^2$ turns the source term into the work of gravity (§3.2, eqs. 34, 35). It is differenced by the work done at the faces of each cell: $-(\delta t/2)$ times the sum, over the two faces normal to $x$, of the mass flux through each face times the gradient of $\Phi_{SB}$ over the half of the cell next to it (eq. 36). Summed over the domain, this leaves only the product of the net mass flux and the drop of the potential across the domain, so that the total energy $E + \rho\Phi_{SB}$ changes only by work at the boundaries and cannot be lost to truncation error, although the evolved energy excludes the potential energy. The transverse flux gradients along $x$ that correct the interface states of $E$ gain the source terms for $\delta t/2$, while the reconstruction of the pressure needs none. In simulations of the MRI, [Gardiner and Stone](#gardiner-stone-2006) (2006) found that $E$ changed, to machine precision, exactly by the work at the boundaries. Vertical gravity, simpler to treat, is differenced through the potential in four steps: in the interface states of $v_z$, in the transverse corrections along $z$, in the velocities that give the electric field at the half step, and in the final update with the density averaged over the step (§3.3). The energy gains the analogous work at the faces normal to $z$ (eq. 37). Together with suitable vertical boundaries, this lets Athena carry on the studies of the MRI in vertically stratified discs. **[GK]** Eq. 35 as printed gives the source term as $+\rho\mathbf{v}\cdot\nabla\Phi_{SB}$, the opposite of the work in eq. 3, since $-\rho\mathbf{v}\cdot\nabla\Phi_{SB} = \Omega_0^2\rho\mathbf{v}\cdot(2qx\hat{\mathbf{i}} - z\hat{\mathbf{k}})$. Eq. 36 has the sign of the work.

The tests of the source terms are axisymmetric, on periodic domains in the $(x, z)$ plane of size $L_x \times L_z$, with $\Omega_0 = 10^{-3}$, $q = 3/2$, an isothermal gas with $C_s = 10^{-3}$, $\rho_0 = 1$, $p = \rho_0C_s^2$ and $v_y = -q\Omega_0x$, third-order reconstruction, HLLC or HLLD and the CTU+CT integrator (§3.4). An initial radial velocity $v_x = 0.1C_s$ in domains with $L_x = L_z = 1$, 10 and 50, on $64^2$ cells and over thousands of orbits, gives epicycles of constant amplitude (Figure 1). The largest domain, with only 14 steps per orbit, shows a dispersion error of up to about 2 per cent, which converges at second order with resolution. The nonlinear axisymmetric density waves of [Fromang and Papaloizou](#fromang-papaloizou-2007) (2007) start from a numerical solution, which Fromang provided, on 40 cells over $L_x = 9.593$, with amplitude $\rho_{\max}/\rho_0 = 1.05$. After 10, 50 and 100 periods $T = 5254.31$, the relative error of the density is smooth, without signs of dispersion, and at most 1.5 per cent, and the amplitude $\max(\rho) - \rho_0$ has fallen by only 3 per cent after 100 periods, comparing very well with the results of [Fromang and Papaloizou](#fromang-papaloizou-2007) (2007) at much higher resolution (Figure 2). Axisymmetric modes of the MRI without net flux, with $L_x = L_z = 1$ and the vertical field $B_z = (2p_0/\beta)^{1/2}\sin(2\pi x/L_x)$ for $\beta = 4000$, repeat the run S1c of [Hawley and Balbus](#hawley-balbus-1992) (1992). The MRI grows, saturates and turns into two-dimensional MHD turbulence (Figure 3), which the MRI cannot sustain in two dimensions, so that the field decays at a rate set by the numerical diffusion. The energy $B_x^2/2$ decays more slowly with third- than with second-order reconstruction at every resolution (Figure 4). A short decay, as the regions of highest magnetic pressure expand, followed by exponential growth over ten orders of magnitude in energy, points to an accurate dispersion relation for slow magnetosonic waves.

[Stone et al.](#stone-et-al-2020) (2020, §3.2.6) state that Athena++ implements the shearing box with the same algorithm as Athena. The source terms in the copy follow another discretisation in time: `HydroSourceTerms::ShearingBoxSourceTerms` adds explicit source terms in each stage of the integrator, from the primitive variables of the stage, rather than the update of Crank and Nicolson of eqs. 25 and 33 (`src/hydro/srcterms/shearing_box.cpp`; 2.9.1). The radial momentum gains $2\Delta t(\Omega_0\rho v_y + q\Omega_0^2\rho x)$, with $x$ at the cell centre, and the momentum along $y$ loses $2\Delta t\,\Omega_0\rho v_x$. A non-barotropic energy gains $\Delta t[(F_\rho)_{i-1/2}(\phi_c - \phi_{i-1/2}) + (F_\rho)_{i+1/2}(\phi_{i+1/2} - \phi_c)]/\Delta x$ with $\phi = q\Omega_0^2x^2$, the form of eq. 36. With the parameter `shboxcoord` of the block `<orbital_advection>` set to 2, the orbital direction is $x_3$ instead of $x_2$, for two-dimensional problems in the $(x, z)$ plane. Vertical gravity is no part of these terms, and the problem generators of stratified boxes add it as a source term of their own (2.9.4). The problem generator `ssheet` sets up hydrodynamic problems on shearing-periodic grids in two or three dimensions: the pure orbital shear, an epicycle started by $v_x = 0.1c_s$, and the compressible shearing wave of [Johnson and Gammie](#johnson-gammie-2005) (2005), with $v_x$ and $v_y$ proportional to $\cos(k_xx + k_yy)$ (`src/pgen/ssheet.cpp`; 2.9.3). Its background has $\rho = 1$ and $p = 10^{-6}$, or $p = \rho c_s^2$ for an isothermal gas, and the orbital velocity $-q\Omega_0x$ is left out where orbital advection carries it. For the shearing wave, optional history outputs project $v_x$ and $\delta v_y$ onto the wave, with the radial wavenumber $k_x + q\Omega_0k_yt$ that the shear swings, for comparison with the solution of [Johnson and Gammie](#johnson-gammie-2005) (2005). The input file runs this wave with an amplitude of $4 \times 10^{-4}$ and the wavenumbers $n_x = -4$ and $n_y = 1$ on $64 \times 64$ cells over $[-2, 2]^2$, with $c_s = \Omega_0 = 10^{-3}$, $q = 3/2$, VL2 and PLM to $t = 8000$ (`inputs/hydro/athinput.ssheet`).

#### 2.9.3 Shearing-periodic boundaries

Solutions that are not axisymmetric need radial boundaries that shift the solution by how far the background shear has displaced the radial edges against each other, as [Hawley, Gammie and Balbus](#hawley-et-al-1995) (1995) first discussed ([Stone and Gardiner](#stone-gardiner-2010) 2010, §4). Values at a point $(x, y, z)$ are carried to $(x \pm L_x, y \mp wt, z)$ for $\rho$, $\rho v_x$, $\rho v_z$ and $\mathbf{B}$, with $\rho v_y$ changed by $\mp\rho w$ and $E$ by $\mp\rho v_yw + \rho w^2/2$, where $w = q\Omega_0L_x$ is the jump of the orbital velocity from one radial edge to the other (eqs. 38 to 40). Athena first applies periodic boundaries along $x$, with these shifts of momentum and energy, and then remaps every quantity of the ghost cells conservatively along $y$ by the distance $wt$ that the boundaries have sheared. This distance is in general not a whole number of cells, and the fractional part is remapped with the reconstructions of the integrator. Each component of the field is remapped directly in the ghost cells, rather than evolved there from remapped electric fields as by [Gressel and Ziegler](#gressel-ziegler-2007) (2007). The ghost cells are then not free of divergence, which does no harm, as they serve only for interpolation.

The boundaries can break conservation, as [Gressel and Ziegler](#gressel-ziegler-2007) (2007) noted, since after the remap the fluxes integrated over one radial face need not equal those over the other. If those of the azimuthal electric field $\mathcal{E}_y$ differ, the net vertical flux of the domain is not conserved, to which the MRI is sensitive. Athena therefore remaps $\mathcal{E}_y$ on each radial face and updates the field next to the boundary with the average of its own $\mathcal{E}_y$ and the remapped value of the corresponding cell on the opposite face. As the remap keeps the integral of $\mathcal{E}_y$ along $y$ unchanged, both faces use the same integral, and the net vertical flux is conserved to machine precision. The same procedure could restore conservation for all fluxes, and for $\mathcal{E}_z$ it would conserve the net toroidal flux. With the orbital advection of 2.9.4, however, mass, momentum and energy stay accurately conserved without it, so that only $\mathcal{E}_y$ is corrected in general. The net toroidal flux then created stays very small in three-dimensional simulations of the MRI, its magnetic energy fluctuating randomly with peaks of about $10^{-9}$ of the thermal pressure for zero net vertical flux and an initial $\beta = 4000$. Matching the order of reconstruction in the ghost cells to that of the integrator keeps it small: second order in the ghost cells under a third-order integrator gave a mean toroidal flux ten times larger. Efficient use of more than about $10^3$ processors needs a decomposition in all three directions, and the ghost cells of a radial boundary may then need data from up to three processors on the opposite boundary. Athena exchanges the ghost cells by the periodic boundaries, which are easily parallelised, and then remaps them by a cyclic shift among the processors that hold neighbouring patches along $y$. This adds calls of MPI, but the code is simpler and the communication more regular, and so in most cases more efficient.

Athena++ selects these boundaries by the flag `shear_periodic` of the radial boundaries (`src/bvals/utils/boundary_flag.cpp`). `BoundaryValues` stores the difference of the orbital velocity across the mesh, $q\Omega_0L_x$, and before a stage `BoundaryValues::ComputeShear` turns the shear $q\Omega_0L_xt$, modulo the length of the mesh along $y$, into a whole number of cells and a fraction (`src/bvals/bvals.cpp`). It does so for the time at the start of the stage, for the fluxes, and at its end, for the ghost cells, and it finds the blocks along the opposite boundary that exchange data. Each block on a radial boundary sends its ghost cells, filled by the periodic exchange, to up to four blocks along $y$ that need them after the integer shift, and three blocks for fluxes and electric fields (`src/bvals/cc/bvals_shear_cc.cpp`). The fraction of a cell is then remapped conservatively, with the remap fluxes of PLM or PPM of orbital advection depending on the reconstruction of the integrator, and with one or two extra ghost cells along $y$ (`OrbitalAdvection::RemapFluxPlm`, `OrbitalAdvection::RemapFluxPpm`; 2.9.4). Without orbital advection, the momentum $\rho v_y$ of the ghost cells changes by $\pm q\Omega_0L_x\rho$ and the energy with it, positive at the inner and negative at the outer boundary, also when the mesh is first set up (`src/bvals/cc/hydro/bvals_shear_hydro.cpp`, `HydroBoundaryVariable::ShearQuantities`). Of the field, $B_y$ and $B_z$ on the ghost faces are remapped and $B_x$ follows from the divergence constraint in Cartesian form, so that unlike in Athena the ghost cells are free of divergence (`src/bvals/fc/bvals_shear_fc.cpp`). The electric fields $\mathcal{E}_y$ and $\mathcal{E}_z$ on the shearing faces become the averages of their own values and the remapped ones of the opposite face, those of $\mathcal{E}_z$ shifted by the shear velocity times $B_x$ without orbital advection (`src/bvals/fc/bvals_shear_emf.cpp`; `src/bvals/fc/flux_correction_fc.cpp`). The radial fluxes of the hydrodynamic variables, the passive scalars and radiation are averaged in the same way, those of $\rho v_y$ and $E$ shifted by the shear without orbital advection (`src/bvals/cc/bvals_shear_flux.cpp`; `src/bvals/cc/hydro/flux_correction_hydro.cpp`). Unlike Athena, which corrected only $\mathcal{E}_y$, Athena++ thus conserves mass, momentum and energy as well as the net vertical and azimuthal fluxes. The boundaries refuse mesh refinement by adaptation, static refinement along $y$ or, with MHD, along $z$, and blocks on different levels at the two radial boundaries. The block size along $y$ must exceed the number of ghost cells plus the extra ones, and relativity is excluded.

Three problem generators provide tests of shearing-box MHD. `hb3` follows paper III of [Hawley and Balbus](#hawley-balbus-1992) (1992), the MRI in two dimensions in the radial–vertical plane, which requires `shboxcoord` set to 2 and no orbital advection (`src/pgen/hb3.cpp`). On a background with $\rho = 1$ and $p = 10^{-5}$, or $p = \rho c_s^2$ for an isothermal gas, and the orbital velocity, it perturbs the pressure, or the density of an isothermal gas, randomly by relative amounts uniform in $[-\mathrm{amp}/2, \mathrm{amp}/2]$. Alternatively it sets a uniform $v_x$ of `amp` times the sound speed with a sinusoidal density. The field is either $B_z = B_0\sin(k_xx)$, without net flux, or a uniform $B_z$, with $B_0 = (2p/\beta)^{1/2}$, and the history records the integral of the Maxwell stress $-B_xB_y$. The input file runs the first kind on $64 \times 64$ cells over $[-0.5, 0.5]^2$ with $c_s = 0.00408$, $\Omega_0 = 10^{-3}$, $q = 3/2$, $\beta = 4000$ and `amp` $= 0.01$, for eight orbits with VL2 and PLM (`inputs/mhd/athinput.hb3`). `hgb` sets up the three-dimensional MRI after [Hawley, Gammie and Balbus](#hawley-et-al-1995) (1995), with the shear in the $(x, y)$ plane (`src/pgen/hgb.cpp`). Its fields are $B_z = B_0\sin(k_xx)$, a uniform $B_z$, the helical field $(0, B_0\cos(k_xx), B_0\sin(k_xx))$, the net toroidal and vertical field $(0, B_0, B_0)/\sqrt{2}$ or a uniform $B_y$, each with a chosen sign of the net flux. Its perturbations are random values $(0.4/\sqrt{3})\cdot 10^{-3}\,r/\sqrt{\gamma}$ of each velocity component, with $r$ uniform in $[-\mathrm{amp}/2, \mathrm{amp}/2]$, from which the mean momentum of each block is removed, and for a non-barotropic gas relative changes $2r$ of the pressure, a uniform $v_x$ of `amp` times the sound speed for epicycles, or a nonlinear shearing wave in an isothermal gas set from closed-form amplitudes. The density wave of [Fromang and Papaloizou](#fromang-papaloizou-2007) (2007) is listed but not implemented, and the history records the integrals of $-B_xB_y$ and $\delta v_x\delta v_y$. The input file runs the random perturbations with `amp` $= 0.025$ and $\beta = 100$ in the field $B_z = B_0\sin(k_xx)$, on $32 \times 24 \times 32$ cells over $[-0.5, 0.5] \times [-\pi/2, \pi/2] \times [-0.5, 0.5]$ with $c_s = \Omega_0 = 1$ and $q = 3/2$, for ten orbits to $t = 62.83$ (`inputs/mhd/athinput.hgb`). `jgg` sets up linear MHD in the shearing sheet after [Johnson, Guan and Gammie](#johnson-et-al-2008) (2008) (`src/pgen/jgg.cpp`). The first kind is a passive shearing wave of the field, $B_x = \mathrm{amp}\cos(k_yy)$ in two dimensions or $B_z = \mathrm{amp}\cos(k_yy)$ in three, on a uniform background at rest in the shearing frame. The second is the compressive shearing wave of their Figure 11, a fast wave on a background field perpendicular to its horizontal wavevector, with perturbations of density, velocity and field, the last from a vector potential. Optional history outputs project the field onto the wave for comparison with the linear solution. The input file runs the compressive wave with an amplitude of $10^{-6}$, $\beta = 20$ and the wavenumbers $(-2, 1, 1)$ on $32 \times 16 \times 16$ cells over $[-0.25, 0.25]^3$, with $c_s = \Omega_0 = 1$ and $q = 3/2$, to $t = 3$ (`inputs/mhd/athinput.jgg`).

Three regression tests use these problems (`tst/regression/scripts/tests/shearingbox/`). `mri2d` runs `hb3` with HLLD on the setup of its input file and compares the time averages over the last 400 records of the history of the Maxwell stress, the magnetic energy and their ratio, scaled by the pressure, with reference data. The stress and the energy may differ by 50 per cent and the ratio by 1. `mhd_shwave` runs the compressive wave of `jgg` with PPM and four ghost cells, without and with orbital advection, and compares the projection of $\delta B_y$ with the linear amplitude, which grows with $B_y(t) = B_{y,0} - q\Omega_0tB_x$ and with the wavenumber swung by the shear. It requires mean absolute errors below 0.01 for this projection and for a measure that should vanish. `ssheet` runs the shearing wave of 2.9.2 with HLLE, without and with orbital advection, and compares the cosine projection of $\delta v_y$ with the analytic solution in confluent hypergeometric functions. It compares the mean absolute errors of the run without orbital advection with the thresholds 0.2 for the cosine and 0.01 for the sine projection.

#### 2.9.4 Orbital advection

Orbital advection, as introduced by [Masset](#masset-2000) (2000) and developed by [Johnson, Guan and Gammie](#johnson-et-al-2008) (2008) and [Johansen, Youdin and Klahr](#johansen-et-al-2009) (2009), rests on two properties of the orbital motion $v_K$ of the shearing box: it does not change in time, and it depends on $x$ alone ([Stone and Gardiner](#stone-gardiner-2010) 2010, §5). With the velocity split as $\mathbf{v} = \mathbf{v}_K + \mathbf{v}'$ and the fluctuation $\mathbf{v}' = (v_x, v_y + q\Omega_0x, v_z)$, the equations for $\rho$, $\rho\mathbf{v}'$ and $E'$ each gain a term of linear advection, $v_K\,\partial/\partial y$, and the induction equation gains $\nabla\times(\mathbf{v}_K\times\mathbf{B})$ (eqs. 41 to 46). The momentum fluctuation has the source terms $2\Omega_0\rho v'_y\hat{\mathbf{i}} + (q - 2)\Omega_0\rho v'_x\hat{\mathbf{j}} - \rho\Omega_0^2z\hat{\mathbf{k}}$. The energy $E' = p/(\gamma - 1) + \rho\,\mathbf{v}'\cdot\mathbf{v}'/2 + B^2/2$ leaves out the kinetic energy of the orbital motion, its potential $\Phi'_{SB} = \Omega_0^2z^2/2$ retains the vertical gravity alone, and it gains $(B_xB_y - \rho v'_xv'_y)\,\partial v_K/\partial x$ (eqs. 47, 48).
