The equations are split into two systems. The first holds the advection terms and this term of the energy, which cannot take the form of a flux gradient and enters as a source term (eqs. 49 to 52). The second is ordinary MHD in the variables $\mathbf{q} = (\rho, \rho\mathbf{v}', E', \mathbf{B})$ (eqs. 53 to 56). The CTU+CT integrator solves it with the time differences of Crank and Nicolson and the potential of 2.9.2, both simpler here than in the original system. Its source terms in the reconstruction and the transverse flux gradients change to match, and the conversions of eqs. 31 and 32 drop out, since the Riemann solver now yields the fluxes of the fluctuations of momentum. **[GK]** Eqs. 45 and 55 as printed give the work of the vertical gravity as $+\rho\mathbf{v}'\cdot\nabla\Phi'_{SB}$, with the sign of eq. 35 (2.9.2). The work is $-\rho\Omega_0^2zv_z$.

The time step of the second system depends only on the fluctuations, $\delta t = C_\circ\min[\delta x/(|v'_x| + C_{fx}), \delta y/(|v'_y| + C_{fy}), \delta z/(|v'_z| + C_{fz})]$, with the Courant number $C_\circ$ and the fast magnetosonic speeds along the three directions (eq. 57). As the orbital flow is supersonic where $|x| > H/q$, with the scale height $H = C_s/\Omega_0$, domains of radial extent near $H$ or beyond allow much larger steps, the main gain of the method. The first system is linear advection along $y$, with the finite volumes $U^{n+1}_{i,j,k} = U^n_{i,j,k} - (\delta t/\delta y)(\bar{f}_{i,j+1/2,k} - \bar{f}_{i,j-1/2,k})$ for each conserved quantity $U$ (eq. 58). Each upwind flux $\bar{f}$ integrates $U^n$ over the interval of length $v_K\delta t$ on the upwind side of its interface (eq. 59). Cells inside the interval add their averages, and the single cell that it cuts adds the part given by the reconstructions of [Stone et al.](#stone-et-al-2008) (2008, §4; 2.4). This conservative remap by $v_K\delta t$ may extend over any number of cells and needs no limit of stability. **[GK]** Eqs. 58 and 59 as printed do not fit together. The integral of eq. 59 is the amount of $U$ that crosses the interface during $\delta t$, so that the update divides the difference of two such integrals by $\delta y$ alone. The factor $\delta t$ of eq. 58 belongs to fluxes defined as these integrals divided by $\delta t$.

Eq. 52 lends itself to CT with the effective electric field $\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}} = -\mathbf{v}_K\times\mathbf{B} = (-v_KB_z, 0, v_KB_x)$ (eq. 60), centred on the edges as elsewhere in Athena and applied by the update of [Stone et al.](#stone-et-al-2008) (2008, eqs. 16 to 18; 2.3.2). The components $\mathcal{E}_x$ and $\mathcal{E}_z$ follow by integrating $-v_KB_z$ and $v_KB_x$ over the distance $v_K\delta t$ on the upwind side of each edge (eqs. 61, 62). The field then stays free of divergence to round-off in the orbital step as well, and no source terms are needed. The differences of CT follow how the shear changes $B_y$, without the interpolation free of divergence on which the method of [Johnson, Guan and Gammie](#johnson-et-al-2008) (2008) depends. **[GK]** Eqs. 61 and 62 as printed integrate $v_KB$ over a length $v_K\delta t$, which yields the electric field times $v_K\delta t$. The substitution $y' = y - v_Kt$ in the average over the step shows that the electric field is $1/\delta t$ times the integral of the field alone, that is, $v_K$ times the mean of $B_x$ or $-B_z$ over the interval.

The term of eq. 51, the work of the Reynolds and Maxwell stresses on the orbital shear, needs its mean over the step and the cell. In the Lagrangian frame of the remap only $B_y$ changes, as $B_y(t + \delta t) = B_y(t) + B_x(\partial v_K/\partial x)\delta t$ with $\partial v_K/\partial x = -q\Omega_0$. A Lagrangian step therefore first adds the source term averaged in time, $\hat{E}'_{i,j,k} = E'_{i,j,k} - q\Omega_0\delta t\,B_{x,i,j,k}[B_{y,i,j,k}(0) - B_{x,i,j,k}q\Omega_0\delta t/2] + q\Omega_0\delta t\,\rho v'_{x,i,j,k}v'_{y,i,j,k}$, with the cell-centred field as it was before the orbital step, and the remap of eq. 58 then carries $\hat{E}'$ (eq. 63). Adiabatic runs of the MRI confirm that this treatment works well. The total energy can change only through a net stress at the radial boundaries, and further tests showed that it is conserved when this stress vanishes.

The tests run in two dimensions, in the $(x, y)$ plane, and in three, on domains $|x| \le L_x/2$, $|y| \le L_y/2$ and $|z| \le L_z/2$, with the parameters and methods of 2.9.2: $\Omega_0 = 10^{-3}$, $q = 3/2$, an isothermal gas unless stated otherwise, $\rho_0 = 1$, $v_{y,0} = -q\Omega_0x$, third-order reconstruction, HLLC or HLLD and CTU+CT (§5.1). The hydrodynamic shearing wave of [Johnson and Gammie](#johnson-gammie-2005) (2005), [Balbus and Hawley](#balbus-hawley-2006) (2006) and [Shen, Stone and Gardiner](#shen-et-al-2006) (2006) has $L_x = L_y = 1$, $C_s = 1.29 \times 10^{-3}$ and the initial wavevector $(-8, 2)\,2\pi/L$, and its amplitude should grow at most 17-fold, at $t\Omega_0 = 8/3$. With and without orbital advection, at 4, 8 and 16 cells per initial wavelength, the kinetic energy of $v_x$ is followed closely from 8 cells per wavelength on, with almost no aliasing at late times (Figure 5). It falls by five orders of magnitude without phases of lasting growth. A weak field loop with $\beta = 2 \times 10^6$, set up as by [Gardiner and Stone](#gardiner-stone-2005) (2005) in a domain with $L_x = 3$, $L_y = 8$ and $C_s = 10^{-3}$, receives $v_x = C_s$. It then oscillates in epicycles while the shear distorts it, and the outer quarter of the loop passes through the radial boundary where the displacement is largest. Its field lines show none of the stripes of wrongly computed electric fields, so that the upwind CT of [Gardiner and Stone](#gardiner-stone-2005) (2005) suits orbital advection too, and nothing caused by the boundaries of 2.9.3 (Figure 6). The compressible MHD shearing wave of [Johnson](#johnson-2007) (2007), which [Johnson, Guan and Gammie](#johnson-et-al-2008) (2008) introduced as a sensitive test, repeats their Figure 11 against a numerical integration of the ordinary differential equations of the analytic solution, provided by Johnson. A cube of side $L = 0.5$ on $N \times N/2 \times N/2$ cells, with $N = 16$, 32 and 64, holds an isothermal gas with $C_s = \Omega_0 = 1$. The initial state is $\rho = 1 + 5.48082 \times 10^{-6}\cos(\mathbf{k}_0\cdot\mathbf{x})$, $\delta\mathbf{v} = (-4.5856, 2.29279, 2.29279) \times 10^{-6}\cos(\mathbf{k}_0\cdot\mathbf{x})$ and $\mathbf{B}/\sqrt{4\pi} = (5.48082 \times 10^{-7}, 1.0962 \times 10^{-6}, 0)\cos(\mathbf{k}_0\cdot\mathbf{x}) + (0.1, 0.2, 0)$, with $\mathbf{k}_0 = (-2, 1, 1)\,2\pi/L$. The diagnostic $\delta B_y = (2/V)\int(B_y - \bar{B}_y)\cos(\mathbf{k}(t)\cdot\mathbf{x})\,dV$ uses the expected mean $\bar{B}_y = 0.2 - 0.15\,\Omega_0t$ and the wavevector $\mathbf{k}(t) = (-2 + q\Omega_0t, 1, 1)\,2\pi/L$ that the shear swings (eq. 64). At 32 cells per initial wavelength it cannot be told apart from the semi-analytic solution (Figure 7).

Simulations of the nonlinear MRI in three dimensions start without net vertical flux, $\mathbf{B} = (0, 0, (2p_0/\beta)^{1/2}\sin(2\pi x/L_x))$ with $\beta = 400$, in a domain with $L_x = L_y = 8H$ and $L_z = H$, where $H = C_s/\Omega_0 = 1$ for $C_s = 10^{-3}$, at 32 cells per $H$. With and without orbital advection their magnetic energies agree very well in character, while the step with it is on average 4.3 times longer once the flow is nonlinear (Figure 8). As the MRI is chaotic, only averages over time can agree. [Johnson, Guan and Gammie](#johnson-et-al-2008) (2008) noted that, without orbital advection, the truncation error depends on $x$ and imprints spurious patterns on radial profiles of averages over time. Without orbital advection the density indeed has a minimum and the stress a maximum near the centre, where the speed, and probably the truncation error, is smallest, while with it both profiles are far smoother (Figure 9). The runs omit the corrections at the boundaries by [Gressel and Ziegler](#gressel-ziegler-2007) (2007), yet mass is conserved to within $10^{-3}$ without and $10^{-5}$ with orbital advection over their 16 orbits. Longer runs without net flux, with $L_x = L_z = H$, $L_y = 4H$ and 32 to 256 cells per $H$, also confirm that the Maxwell stress does not converge with resolution. Without orbital advection the coarsest of them loses about 8 per cent of its mass within 100 orbits and the finest 1.5 per cent, against 0.03 per cent at every resolution with it. Runs with an adiabatic gas test the energy terms of the orbital step. They repeat the run Z2 of [Gardiner and Stone](#gardiner-stone-2006) (2006), with the net vertical field $B_z = (2p_0/\beta)^{1/2}$, $\beta = 400$, $L_x = L_z = H$, $L_y = 2\pi H$, $64 \times 128 \times 64$ cells, $p_0 = 10^{-6}$ and $\gamma = 5/3$. With and without orbital advection the total energy grows at very similar mean rates, and after saturation all of the gain is internal energy.

Orbital advection thus proves at least as accurate as integration without it, though perhaps not more so (§6). Its chief benefit is that the background shear no longer limits the time step, which makes accretion flows over wide radial ranges far cheaper to study, and the method built on CT is simpler than earlier ones and keeps the field free of divergence to machine precision. A run of the MRI with $L_x = L_y = 32H$, $L_z = H$ and 32 cells per $H$, that is $1024 \times 1024 \times 32$ cells, otherwise like that of Figure 8, shows clear spiral waves of density driven by the MRI (Figure 10). Their pitch angle matches that of smaller domains, so that the size of the box does not set them. Long-lived density features in tall boxes, which [Johansen, Youdin and Klahr](#johansen-et-al-2009) (2009) reported, appear as well when this run and others are analysed more closely. The mean Maxwell stress hardly differs from that of far smaller domains, which indicates turbulent stresses confined to scales of at most about $H$. [Johnson, Guan and Gammie](#johnson-et-al-2008) (2008) found spurious features in the mean radial profile of density with their orbital advection too, at radii where the orbital displacement came close to a whole number of cells. The wide run shows no significant extrema at such radii, so that stress and truncation error vary smoothly with radius (Figure 11).

[Stone and Gardiner](#stone-gardiner-2010) (2010, §6) conclude that Athena has gained shearing-box source terms whose time differences of Crank and Nicolson keep epicyclic oscillations at exactly constant amplitude, and an orbital advection built on CT that keeps the field free of divergence and gives more accurate solutions at lower cost. These methods had already served studies of hydrodynamic turbulence in shearing boxes ([Shen, Stone and Gardiner](#shen-et-al-2006) 2006), and studies of the MRI in radially wide domains, with and without vertical stratification, were under way.

[Stone et al.](#stone-et-al-2020) (2020, §3.2.6) state that Athena++ implements orbital advection after the methods of [Stone and Gardiner](#stone-gardiner-2010) (2010), to make local shearing-box calculations faster and more accurate. The method goes back to [Masset](#masset-2000) (2000), for hydrodynamics in the code FARGO, and [Johnson, Guan and Gammie](#johnson-et-al-2008) (2008) and [Benítez-Llambay and Masset](#benitez-llambay-masset-2016) (2016) extended it to MHD, while PLUTO has it as well ([Mignone et al.](#mignone-et-al-2012b) 2012b). In Athena++ it also serves global disc calculations on cylindrical and spherical-polar grids.

Each block of Athena++ holds an object of the class `OrbitalAdvection` (`src/orbital_advection/orbital_advection.hpp`). The parameter `OAorder` of the block `<orbital_advection>` leaves orbital advection off with 0, the default, and selects it with 1 or 2, the order of its splitting from the rest of the integration (`OrbitalAdvection::OrbitalAdvection`). The orbital direction, $x_2$ in Cartesian and cylindrical and $x_3$ in spherical-polar coordinates, must be periodic and uniformly spaced, `shboxcoord` must be 1, and relativity is excluded. Where the orbital direction is not resolved, the velocity is still taken relative to the orbital motion, but nothing is remapped. The default orbital velocities, relative to the frame rotating at `Omega0`, are $-q\Omega_0x$ in Cartesian coordinates, with $q$ from `qshear`, and $(GM/R)^{1/2} - \Omega_0R$ in cylindrical ones, or $(GM/d)^{1/2}R/d - \Omega_0R$ with $d = (R^2 + z^2)^{1/2}$ when $z$ is resolved. In spherical-polar coordinates the default is $(GM/\varpi)^{1/2} - \Omega_0\varpi$ with $\varpi = r\sin\theta$, and `GM` comes from the block `<problem>` (`src/orbital_advection/default_orbital_velocity.cpp`). A problem generator may enrol another profile and its derivatives with `Mesh::EnrollOrbitalVelocity` and `Mesh::EnrollOrbitalVelocityDerivative`, but shearing-periodic boundaries accept only the default one.

The conserved and primitive variables refer to the motion relative to the orbital velocity. `OrbitalAdvection::ConvertOrbitalSystem` adds the orbital motion back for outputs, the history and the diffusive fluxes, unless the parameter `orbital_system` of an output asks for the relative variables (`src/orbital_advection/orbital_system_conversion.cpp`). In an orbital step, the displacement of each cell along the orbital direction splits into whole cells and a fraction. Each block exchanges with its two neighbours along that direction the cells that the shift carries across, restricted or prolongated where a neighbour lies on another level (`src/bvals/orbital/bvals_orbital.cpp`, `OrbitalBoundaryCommunication`). The new averages of the conserved variables and passive scalars are the shifted ones less the difference of the remap fluxes of the fraction (`OrbitalAdvection::CalculateOrbitalAdvectionCC`). These fluxes integrate, over the fraction of the upwind cell next to each interface, the linear profile with the slope of [van Leer](#van-leer-1974) (1974) of 2.4.3 for `xorder` up to 2, and otherwise the parabola of PPM with the limiter of 2.4.5 that preserves smooth extrema (`src/orbital_advection/orbital_remapping.cpp`). For the field, $\mathcal{E}_1$ and $\mathcal{E}_3$ are integrals of $-B_3$ and $B_1$ over the swept distance, from whole cells and the remap flux of the fraction (`OrbitalAdvection::CalculateOrbitalAdvectionFC`). `Field::CT` applies them with a weight of one, as electric fields integrated over the step. In spherical-polar coordinates, $B_2$ and $-B_1$ give $\mathcal{E}_1$ and $\mathcal{E}_2$ in the same way.

Unlike the remap of [Stone and Gardiner](#stone-gardiner-2010) (2010), that of Athena++ limits the time step (`OrbitalAdvection::InitializeOrbitalAdvection`; `src/hydro/new_blockdt.cpp`). The displacement may not exceed a block length less the extra ghost cells of the remap, or half a block length less these cells towards a finer neighbour. The displacements of a cell and of its near neighbours along the other directions must differ by less than one cell, or half a cell for `OAorder` set to 1. The task list sends, receives and remaps the conserved variables after the source terms and before the usual exchange of ghost cells, and the field after its integration (`src/task_list/time_integrator.cpp`). With `OAorder` set to 1 a remap by the whole step follows the main stages, and with 2 remaps by half steps come before and after them. They take stages of their own where shearing-periodic boundaries or mesh refinement require it.

`HydroSourceTerms::OrbitalAdvectionSourceTerms` replaces the terms of the point mass, the shearing box and the rotating frame (`src/hydro/srcterms/orbital_advection_srcterms.cpp`; 2.9.1, 2.9.2). In Cartesian coordinates with the default velocity, the momentum gains the terms of eq. 54, $2\Delta t\,\Omega_0\rho v'_y$ along $x$ and $-(2 - q)\Delta t\,\Omega_0\rho v_x$ along $y$. A non-barotropic energy gains $q\Omega_0\Delta t(\overline{\rho v_x}v'_y - B_xB_y)$, where $\overline{\rho v_x}$ averages the momentum of the cell with the mean of the radial mass fluxes on its faces. The term of eq. 51 thus enters explicitly in each main stage, not by the Lagrangian step of eq. 63 within the remap. In curvilinear coordinates, with the orbital velocity in the inertial frame $v_c = v_K + \Omega_0\varpi$, the terms comprise the cross term $2\rho v_cv'_\phi$ of the centrifugal force and the change of the angular momentum $\varpi v_c$ along radial and polar motion. They add the centrifugal force of $v_c$ less the gravity of the point mass where the two do not cancel, and in the energy the work of these forces and that of the stresses on the shear of the orbital angular velocity.

The problem generators `ssheet`, `hgb`, `jgg`, `field_loop` and `disk` leave the orbital velocity out of their initial states when orbital advection is selected, while `hb3` refuses it. On shearing-periodic grids `field_loop` gives the loop of [Gardiner and Stone](#gardiner-stone-2005) (2005) the radial velocity $c_s$ of the test of Figure 6 (`src/pgen/field_loop.cpp`). `disk` sets up a disc around the mass `GM` in cylindrical or spherical-polar coordinates (`src/pgen/disk.cpp`). `strat` sets up the vertically stratified shearing box of [Stone et al.](#stone-et-al-1996) (1996), with the vertical gravity $-\rho\Omega_0^2z$ as a source term of the user, smoothed near the vertical boundaries, and boundaries that extend an isothermal atmosphere in hydrostatic balance into the ghost cells (`src/pgen/strat.cpp`). Its input file runs the MRI without net flux on $8 \times 32 \times 48$ cells, without orbital advection (`inputs/mhd/athinput.strat`). The regression tests `ssheet` and `mhd_shwave` of 2.9.3 run with `OAorder` set to 2 as well as without it.

### 2.10 Self-gravity

#### 2.10.1 Self-gravity by fast Fourier transform

Athena++ obtains the gravitational potential of the gas from the Poisson equation $\nabla^2\phi = 4\pi G\rho$, either by FFT or by multigrid, chosen when the code is configured (`configure.py`). The option `--grav=fft` needs `-fft` as well, which links the library FFTW, while `--grav=mg` selects multigrid, the subject of the rest of 2.10. As 1.2 notes, [Stone et al.](#stone-et-al-2020) (2020, §5) present the FFT solver as following Athena and multigrid as the cheaper method at large sizes. Each block holds an object of the class `Gravity` with the potential `phi`, ghost cells included, a coarse copy of it for refinement, and $4\pi G$ (`src/gravity/gravity.hpp`). The problem generator fixes the constant in `Mesh::InitUserMeshData`, through `Mesh::SetGravitationalConstant` for $G$ or `Mesh::SetFourPiG` for $4\pi G$ (`src/mesh/mesh.hpp`). A boundary variable for values at cell centres, enrolled among those of the block, carries the potential into the ghost cells (`Gravity::Gravity`). The potential acts on the momentum and the energy through source terms described later in 2.10, and the history adds the gravitational energy, the integral of $\rho\phi/2$ (`src/outputs/history.cpp`).

With FFT the mesh holds an object of the class `FFTGravityDriver` (`src/gravity/fft_gravity.cpp`). Its `Solve` copies the density of the blocks of each process into one FFT block, transforms it forward, multiplies every mode by a kernel and transforms back. It then copies the potential into the blocks, and a task list of its own fills their ghost cells (`FFTGravityDriver::Solve`; `src/task_list/grav_task_list.cpp`). The kernel `FFTGravity::ApplyKernel` divides each mode by an eigenvalue of the Laplacian. In mode 0 it is that of the difference operator of second order, $\sum_d(2\cos\theta_d - 2)/\Delta x_d^2$ with $\theta_d = 2\pi m_d/N_d$, where the mode number $m_d$ is folded into $[-N_d/2, N_d/2]$. In mode 1 it is $-|\mathbf{k}|^2$ with the wavenumbers $k_d = 2\pi m_d/L_d$. The mode of zero wavenumber is set to zero, which removes the mean density from the source, and the result is scaled by $4\pi G/N$, with $N$ the number of cells of the mesh, since the transforms are not normalised. The main loop uses the discrete kernel, so that the potential satisfies the difference form of the Poisson equation to round-off and is periodic on the mesh (`src/main.cpp`). It solves once when the mesh is initialised and again after every stage that is followed by a main stage, so that each main stage uses the potential of the current density (`Mesh::Initialize`; `TaskList::CheckNextMainStage`). The task list for the potential sends, receives and sets the ghost cells and prolongates them on refined meshes, while it leaves the physical boundaries alone with FFT (`GravityBoundaryTaskList`).

The classes `FFTDriver` and `FFTBlock` are general, and they also serve the driving of turbulence (2.15; `src/fft/athena_fft.hpp`, `src/fft/fft_driver.cpp`). The driver refuses meshes of non-uniform spacing. It gathers the blocks of each process into one FFT block, which must be a cuboid of the same size on every process, so that the number of blocks of the mesh divided by the number per process equals the number of processes (`FFTDriver::FFTDriver`). The FFT blocks decompose the mesh along the directions that hold more than one of them. Without MPI, the block uses complex transforms of FFTW, planned once on its input array and executed from it into its output array (`FFTBlock::CreatePlan`, `FFTBlock::Execute`). With MPI, it uses the parallel transforms in two and three dimensions of Plimpton, which remap the data between decompositions, with swaps and cyclic permutations of the axes, so that each transform along one direction acts on data held whole by one process (`src/fft/plimpton/`, under the GNU General Public Licence; `FFTBlock::InitializeMPI`). `FFTBlock::LoadSource` and `FFTBlock::RetrieveResult` copy data between the blocks and the FFT block, the latter scaling it by the norm factor.

The problem generator `jeans` sets up a linear wave with self-gravity in an adiabatic or isothermal gas without field (`src/pgen/jeans.cpp`). Unless its angles are given, the wavevector follows the main diagonal of the domain, at $\alpha_3 = \arctan(L_1/L_2)$ about $z$ and $\alpha_2 = \arctan[\tfrac{1}{2}(L_1\cos\alpha_3 + L_2\sin\alpha_3)/L_3]$ about $y$. The wavelength $\lambda$ is the smallest projection of the box onto it among the resolved directions. With $\rho_0 = 1$ and $p_0 = 1/\gamma$, so that $c_s = 1$ for an adiabatic gas, or with the isothermal sound speed, the parameter `njeans`, the ratio $n_J$ of $\lambda$ to the Jeans length, fixes $G = \pi c_s^2n_J^2/(\rho_0\lambda^2)$. The waves then obey $\omega^2 = k^2c_s^2(1 - n_J^2)$ with $k = 2\pi/\lambda$, and they are stable for $n_J < 1$ and grow for $n_J > 1$. The density is $\rho_0[1 + A\sin(kx) + A^2\sin(kx_1)]$, with $x$ measured along the wavevector and a further term of second order along $x_1$. The pressure of an adiabatic gas is $p_0[1 + \gamma A\sin(kx)]$, and the velocity is zero for a stable wave and $(\omega/k)A\cos(kx)$ along the wavevector for the growing mode. With `compute_error` set, the L1 errors of density, momentum and pressure against the standing or growing solution, $\rho_0[1 + A\sin(kx)\cos(\omega t)]$ or $\rho_0[1 + A\sin(kx)e^{\omega t}]$ with the momenta that the continuity equation implies, are written to `jeans-errors.dat`, together with the square root of the sum of their squares, which the file calls the RMS error, and the largest ratio of a maximum error to its L1 error. The input file has $64 \times 32 \times 32$ cells over $[0, 1] \times [0, 0.5]^2$, periodic, $A = 10^{-6}$, $n_J = 0.5$, VL2, PLM and a Courant number of 0.3, and runs to $t = 2$ (`inputs/hydro/athinput.jeans_3d`). The wavevector is then $2\pi(1, 2, 2)$, with $\lambda = 1/3$.

The regression test `jeans_3d` runs this problem to $t = 1$ on blocks of $16^3$ cells with FFT and with multigrid, each without MPI and with one, two and four processes (`tst/regression/scripts/tests/grav/jeans_3d.py`). This RMS error must stay below $10^{-7}$ without MPI, must equal that of one process with MPI exactly, and must differ between the numbers of processes by less than $5 \times 10^{-4}$. The test `unstable_jeans_3d_fft` runs the growing mode with $n_J = 1.5$ to $t = 0.046$, about one e-folding time $1/|\omega|$, on $64 \times 32 \times 32$ and $128 \times 64 \times 64$ cells with FFT (`tst/regression/scripts/tests/grav/unstable_jeans_3d_fft.py`). Each error must stay below $10^{-7}$ and fall at the doubled resolution by at least a factor $4/1.5$, second-order convergence with a margin, with a warning when it falls by less than $4/1.1$. Its counterpart with multigrid follows later in 2.10. The problem generator `fft` tests the transforms alone, without time steps (`src/pgen/fft.cpp`). It loads $e^{-r}$, with $r$ the distance from the origin, and times `ncycle` + 1 pairs of forward and backward transforms. It then transforms once with the identity as kernel and writes the mean absolute errors of the real and imaginary parts, with the zone-cycles per second, to `fft-errors.dat`. Its input file has $64^3$ cells over $[-0.5, 0.5]^3$ in blocks of $32^3$ and 100 cycles (`inputs/hydro/athinput.fft`). The regression test `fft` runs it without MPI and with one, two and four processes (`tst/regression/scripts/tests/fft/fft.py`). It requires errors below $10^{-10}$, of the real and imaginary parts without MPI and of the real part with two and four processes, and the same real error without MPI and with one process, and it warns below $10^6$ zone-cycles per second.

#### 2.10.2 Multigrid: the hierarchy of grids and its operators

[Tomida and Stone](#tomida-stone-2023) (2023) build multigrid solvers on the hierarchy of AMR and the task lists of Athena++ and apply them to self-gravity, governed by the Poisson equation (abstract). The Laplacian is written in a conservative form that keeps level boundaries free of spurious accelerations. Boundaries may be periodic, fixed or of zero gradient, or open through a multipole expansion, and the solvers run in parallel with MPI and OpenMP together. On a uniform grid multigrid is much faster than FFT and takes only a small part of the time of the MHD solver, itself highly optimised, and a run of the collapse of a magnetised protostellar core on an adaptive mesh shows what the solvers can do.

The Poisson equation is elliptic and needs other methods than the explicit integration of the hyperbolic equations of motion (§1). Implicit integrators for diffusive processes, such as viscosity, conduction, resistivity and radiation in the optically thick or flux-limited regime, also invert matrices with global communication, so that solvers for such non-local processes must scale to many processes. FFT is robust and efficient on uniform Cartesian grids, but it does not suit non-uniform spacing, curvilinear coordinates or AMR, and it is most efficient with periodic boundaries. Solvers for sparse matrices such as conjugate gradients are hard to implement on complex grids, whose matrices are irregular, and may need robust preconditioners, while tree methods suit open boundaries but combine poorly with others. Geometric multigrid, which goes back to [Fedorenko](#fedorenko-1962) (1962) and is set out by [Trottenberg et al.](#trottenberg-et-al-2001) (2001), applies a classical iterative solver on grids of several resolutions, so that the error decays on all scales together. It suits AMR and many kinds of boundary, and when communication costs little it needs $O(N)$ operations for $N$ unknowns, against $O(N\log N)$ for FFT and tree methods, a gap that matters at large $N$, while the fast multipole method also reaches $O(N)$. The algorithms suit general coordinates, but the paper treats uniformly spaced Cartesian grids.

With $\phi$ and $\rho$ at cell centres, as for the hydrodynamic variables, and the same spacing $h$ along all axes, the seven-point Laplacian gives $\phi_{i+1,j,k} + \phi_{i-1,j,k} + \phi_{i,j+1,k} + \phi_{i,j-1,k} + \phi_{i,j,k+1} + \phi_{i,j,k-1} - 6\phi_{i,j,k} = 4\pi G\rho_{i,j,k}h^2$, written $L_h\phi_h = f_h$ with $f_h = 4\pi G\rho$ (§2.1, eqs. 1 to 4). As the blocks of the octree are uniform inside, refinement complicates only the communication at level boundaries and the refinement itself. The discretisation error $\delta_h = \phi^*_h - \phi_h$, measured from the converged discrete solution $\phi^*_h$, and the true error $\epsilon_h = \phi^* - \phi_h$, measured from the exact solution, differ, as the converged solution reduces $\epsilon_h$ only to the truncation error, of order $h^2$ (eqs. 5, 6). The defect $d_h = f_h - L_h\phi_h$ obeys the defect equation $L_h\delta_h = d_h$ (eqs. 7, 8). As $L_h$ is too costly to invert, an approximate inverse supplies corrections that iterate from an initial guess $\phi^0_h$ towards the solution.

The classical iterative solvers follow from the diffusion-like equation $\partial\phi/\partial\tau = \nabla^2\phi - 4\pi G\rho$ in a pseudo-time $\tau$, whose steady state solves the Poisson equation (§2.2, eq. 9). Forward differences in time with the largest stable step, $\Delta\tau = h^2/(2D)$ in $D$ dimensions, give the method of Jacobi, which replaces each value by the mean of its six neighbours less $4\pi G\rho h^2/6$ (eq. 10). Long wavelengths diffuse slowly, and convergence takes $O(N_x^2)$ iterations, with $N_x = L/h$ cells per direction, and so $O(N_x^5)$ operations in three dimensions, against $O(N_x^3)$ for the explicit MHD. Since only the steady state matters, the condition of Courant, Friedrichs and Lewy need not hold. Gauss–Seidel uses the values already updated, which halves the number of iterations and needs no second array, but makes the iteration anisotropic and sequential, which prevents vectorisation and parallelisation (eq. 11). Red–black Gauss–Seidel (RBGS) updates first the cells with $i + j + k$ even and then the odd ones from them (eqs. 12, 13). It reduces the anisotropy and parallelises, but still defeats vectorisation through strided access, and both variants still cost $O(N_x^5)$. Over-relaxation by a factor $\omega$, convergent for $0 < \omega < 2$ and best near $2/(1 + \pi/N_x)$ in a cubic domain, reduces the iterations to $O(N_x)$ and the cost to $O(N_x^4)$ (eqs. 14 to 16). Chebyshev acceleration, which raises $\omega$ from 1 towards its optimum, cures the convergence of over-relaxation where it is not monotonic, but the solvers do not use it.

On coarser grids long wavelengths behave like short ones and decay quickly under the same solver, so that multigrid combines coarser grids consistently to damp all wavelengths together, after [Trottenberg et al.](#trottenberg-et-al-2001) (2001) (§2.3). Multigrid iteration (MGI) repeats cycles on the finest level from an initial guess, while full multigrid (FMG) starts on the coarsest grid and builds the solution upwards, each finer level starting from the coarser one. Smoothing means applying a classical solver on a level, restriction averaging fine data onto a coarser grid, and prolongation interpolating coarse data onto a finer one. Level $l = 0$ is the coarsest, which cannot be restricted further, with $h_{l-1} = 2h_l$ and the Laplacian $L_l$ on level $l$. Restriction takes the volume-weighted mean of the eight fine cells of a coarse cell, for the potential, the defect or the source (§2.3.1, eq. 17). Prolongation of the potential or of a correction is trilinear, each fine cell taking the weights $3/4$ from its parent and $1/4$ from the neighbour on its own side along each axis (eq. 18; Figure 1). FMG prolongates the potential with an interpolation of third order, as [Trottenberg et al.](#trottenberg-et-al-2001) (2001) recommend, with the weights $5/32$, $30/32$ and $-3/32$ for the cells below, at and above the parent along each axis, reflected for fine cells in the upper half. These weights assume equal cubic cells in Cartesian coordinates.

The smoother is RBGS with over-relaxation, eqs. 14 and 15, with $\omega = 1.15$, which damps the short wavelengths faster than the optimum of eq. 16 would (§2.3.2; [Trottenberg et al.](#trottenberg-et-al-2001) 2001; [Yavneh](#yavneh-1996) 1996). Where the coarsest level is a single cell, eq. 3 gives its value exactly from the ghost cells that the boundary conditions fill, $u_0 = (\sum u_{\mathrm{ghost}} - h_0^2f_0)/6$ (eq. 19). Otherwise, for $N_{0,x} \times N_{0,y} \times N_{0,z}$ cells, the smoother runs $\max(N_{0,x}, N_{0,y}, N_{0,z})$ times, which suffices there.

Athena++ implements the V-cycle, the simplest of the V-, W- and F-cycles (§2.3.3). A cycle on level $l$ smooths $M$ times, computes the defect and restricts it. It obtains the correction on level $l - 1$ from the coarsest solver, or recursively from a guess of zero, prolongates and adds it, and smooths $N$ times (Algorithm 1). This V$(M, N)$ cycle, with $M$ and $N$ of 0, 1 or 2 and usually V(1,1), works on the coarser levels with the equation for the defect instead of the Poisson equation, which the linearity of the problem allows, and is called the correction scheme. Cycles repeat from the initial guess until the criterion of convergence holds (Figure 2a). FMG, introduced by [Brandt](#brandt-1977) (1977), restricts the source to all levels, solves the coarsest level, and on each finer level prolongates the solution with the operator of third order and applies one V-cycle (§2.3.4, Algorithm 2; Figure 2b). It needs no initial guess, as the geometry of the hierarchy builds one. One cycle per level brings the true error down to the truncation error, but the defect and the discretisation error stay large, so that further V-cycles are needed for a converged solution.

The correction scheme restricts only the defect, which works because the Poisson and defect equations are linear and of the same form (§2.3.5). It fails for nonlinear problems and on AMR, where defects of different levels cannot be combined. With refinement, and for nonlinear extensions, the full approximation scheme or storage (FAS) of [Brandt](#brandt-1977) (1977) also restricts the potential and solves eq. 4 on every level (Algorithm 3). After presmoothing and the defect, the defect and the potential $\bar{\phi}$ are restricted, and the coarse source becomes $f_{l-1} = d_{l-1} + L_{l-1}\bar{\phi}_{l-1}$. The coarse solution $\hat{\psi}_{l-1}$ follows from the coarsest solver or recursively, and the coarse correction $\hat{\psi}_{l-1} - \bar{\phi}_{l-1}$ is prolongated and added before postsmoothing. For linear problems on uniform grids FAS equals the correction scheme. The extra restriction of the potential is cheap, and the other extra work falls on the coarse levels, which hold few cells, so that FAS costs moderately more, and its FMG replaces the V-cycle of Algorithm 2 by that of FAS.

Every level needs boundary conditions for smoothing and prolongation (§2.3.6). Athena++ provides periodic, zero-gradient and zero-fixed boundaries, open boundaries from a multipole expansion (App. A) and an interface for boundaries of the user. In the correction scheme the physical boundaries apply on the finest level, while the defect equation on coarser levels takes zero-fixed boundaries unless they are periodic, and FAS needs consistent physical boundaries on all levels. The boundaries set the ghost cells around the active cells that the solvers update. Periodic and zero-gradient values sit at the centres of the ghost cells, while for fixed and open boundaries the ghost values are chosen so that the face of the domain takes the prescribed value, as the centres of the ghost cells move from level to level. The potential has no zero point of its own, and a fixed value or, for the multipole expansion, zero at infinity sets one. Where no boundary sets it, as with periodic or zero-gradient boundaries throughout, the solver works with the deviations $\delta f = f - \langle f\rangle$ and $\delta\phi = \phi - \langle\phi\rangle$ from the volume averages, $\nabla^2\delta\phi = \delta f$, with $\langle\delta\phi\rangle = 0$.

Athena++ offers two modes (§2.3.7). FMG applies full multigrid first and then V-cycles, while MGI repeats V-cycles from an initial guess, such as the potential of the previous step, which is usually worse than the result of one sweep of FMG, so that FMG is recommended. In both modes V-cycles repeat until a threshold is met or the reduction of the error stalls. As the errors are unknown, the criterion uses the defect on the finest level, through its root mean square weighted by volume, $[\sum_{i,j,k}|d_{i,j,k}|^2\Delta V_{i,j,k}/\sum_{i,j,k}\Delta V_{i,j,k}]^{1/2}$ over all cells, with $d_{i,j,k} = 4\pi G\rho_{i,j,k} - (\phi_{i-1,j,k} + \phi_{i+1,j,k} + \phi_{i,j-1,k} + \phi_{i,j+1,k} + \phi_{i,j,k-1} + \phi_{i,j,k+1} - 6\phi_{i,j,k})/h^2$ (eqs. 20, 21). The threshold must be chosen small enough with care, or set to zero to iterate until saturation, or a fixed number of iterations may replace it. No threshold fits all problems, and trials before production runs are advised.

The section on the implementation first reviews Athena++ (§3, §3.1). It recalls its physics in the public version and extensions developed in the community, and that C.-G. Kim developed the FFT solver for uniform grids of 2.10.1. Athena++ decomposes the domain into blocks of equal logical size, and MPI processes and OpenMP threads, nearly equivalent, each own one or more blocks with ghost cells, filled by messages or by copies. Its octree AMR splits a refined block into eight, refines neighbours so that touching blocks differ by at most one level, numbers the blocks along the Z-curve and distributes them evenly. Its task lists overlap computation with communication, and a uniform time step on all levels avoids complex scheduling and interpolation in time, an advantage for physics that couples distant regions, such as self-gravity or radiation transport integrated implicitly (3.2, 3.5).

The multigrid code, in C++11, needs no library except MPI (§3.2). It uses the blocks of the main integrator, and since each block is restricted and prolongated by itself, blocks must be logically cubic with $2^n$ cells along each side, while the number of blocks along each direction is free (§3.2.1). With $N_{0,x} \times N_{0,y} \times N_{0,z}$ cells on level 0, level $l$ has $2^l$ times as many along each direction. On some level $m$ the number of cells equals the number of blocks, and with $L = m + n$ levels in all, the mesh has $2^nN_{\mathrm{MB},x} = 2^{m+n}N_{0,x}$ cells along $x$, and likewise along $y$ and $z$. The potential, source and defect of levels $m$ to $m + n$ are kept by a `Multigrid` object in each block, and those of levels 0 to $m$ in a separate one, the root grid, with one layer of ghost cells for self-gravity and storage for the restricted potential with FAS. Level $m$, a single cell in each object of a block, exists in both for the exchange between them. Levels 0 to $m$ are called root-grid levels and $m + 1$ to $m + n$ block levels.

A V-cycle on a uniform grid starts on the finest level, with the source from the density of the blocks and the initial guess as potential (§3.2.2). It smooths by RBGS, computes and restricts the defect, and repeats down to level $m$. There the data of all objects of the blocks are gathered into the root grid, and the cycle continues to level 0, which the coarsest solver handles. The corrections then travel back up, each followed by postsmoothing, are handed back to the blocks at level $m$ and reach the finest level, which completes the cycle. After each cycle the defect of eq. 20 is compared with the threshold, and once it is met the potential returns to the MHD, which takes the acceleration from it. With FAS the potential is restricted as well, and the source and the coarse correction of Algorithm 3 are computed in addition.

In the code, the class `Multigrid` holds for each level the solution `u_`, the source `src_`, the defect `def_`, a copy `uold_` for FAS and coordinates, and for other solvers coefficients and matrices, and several variables may be solved together (`src/multigrid/multigrid.hpp`). Its constructor refuses blocks that are not logically cubic, whose size is not a power of two, or whose cells are not cubic (`Multigrid::Multigrid`). For the root grid, whose cells are the blocks of the root level, the number of levels follows from how often the numbers of blocks along all three directions can be halved together. A warning follows when the coarsest level cannot shrink to one cell, so that it is solved by iteration, or when it holds more than 100 cells. `Multigrid::Restrict` takes the mean of the eight fine cells, and `Multigrid::RestrictBlock` computes the defect and restricts it into the source of the coarser level. With FAS it restricts the solution as well, and otherwise it clears the coarser solution to zero. `Multigrid::ProlongateAndCorrect` adds the trilinear prolongation of the correction, with the weights 27, 9, 3 and 1 over 64, after `Multigrid::ProlongateAndCorrectBlock` has subtracted, with FAS, the stored restriction from the coarse solution. An option of the driver selects instead the interpolation of third order, whose weights are the products of 5, 30 and $-3$ over 32 along the axes, as the solver of cosmic-ray diffusion does (`src/crdiffusion/mg_crdiffusion.cpp`), and `Multigrid::FMGProlongate` always uses that interpolation. The smoother and the defect are virtual functions, which each solver implements, gravity by RBGS as described later in 2.10. Each object also loads the source and the initial guess, returns the potential with its ghost cells, sums the source or the solution and subtracts an average. It gives the defect summed over its cells as $|d|$ or $d^2$ times the cell volume, or its maximum, which the driver combines into eq. 20 (`Multigrid::CalculateDefectNorm`). With OpenMP the root grid divides its loops among threads, and the coordinates of each level are uniform and Cartesian (`MGCoordinates::CalculateMGCoordinates`).

#### 2.10.3 Multigrid: the driver, its boundaries and its parallelisation

FMG builds directly on the V-cycle ([Tomida and Stone](#tomida-stone-2023) 2023, §3.2.4). The source is first restricted from the finest to the coarsest level and kept on every level. The coarsest level is then solved, exactly or by a few sweeps of the smoother, the result is prolongated with the interpolation of third order of 2.10.2, and one V-cycle improves it as the initial guess of the next level, up to the finest (Figure 2b). There V-cycles repeat until the criterion of convergence holds.

Since FMG parallelises once the V-cycle does, only the latter is described (§3.2.5). As in the MHD part, MPI and OpenMP are used together, and every process, or thread, holds one or several blocks with their `Multigrid` objects, so that the block levels are distributed. Before each smoothing and prolongation, neighbouring objects exchange their boundary values through the ghost cells, for RBGS before the sweep of each colour, and the task lists overlap computation with communication. At level $m$, where every object has shrunk to one cell, the data of all of them are gathered on every process by `MPI_Allgather`. Each process then runs the root-grid levels by itself and hands the result back to its own objects at level $m$. This equals a gather on one process followed by a scatter, but one collective call needs less synchronisation than two.

Most of the cost lies in smoothing, restriction and prolongation, which grow with the number of cells (§3.4). As each coarser level has an eighth of the cells of the finer one, a V-cycle costs about $(8/7)N_x^3$ operations, and as multigrid damps all wavelengths together, the number of cycles needed for some accuracy is independent of the cell count. The total cost is thus $O(N_{\mathrm{tot}})$, against $O(N_{\mathrm{tot}}\log N_{\mathrm{tot}})$ for FFT and $O(N_x^4)$ for SOR. Weak scaling, with the work per process held fixed, should then be good as long as communication stays cheap and the root-grid and octet levels, which are not fully parallel, cost little. Their unknowns number only as many as the objects, but in very large runs, with AMR above all, the root grid can carry a heavy load on a single core, and a further layer of parallelism is left for later work. With uniform grids the root grid costs $O(N_{\mathrm{MB}}^3)$ and the block levels $O(n^3)$, with $n$ cells per block along each direction, so that large blocks favour parallel performance, which becomes hard to reach when $N_{\mathrm{MB}}$ exceeds $n$. With AMR the size of blocks must balance performance against the flexibility of the grid.

Isolated boundaries use the multipole expansion of the potential, which describes it well far from the centre of mass, as the higher multipoles fall off steeply with distance, and Athena++ carries it up to hexadecapoles (App. A). Outside the mass, $\phi(\mathbf{r}) = -G\sum_{l=0}^{l_{\max}}\sum_{m=-l}^{l}(Q_{l,m}/r^{l+1})[4\pi/(2l + 1)]^{1/2}Y_{l,m}(\mathbf{r})$, with the moments $Q_{l,m} = \sum_V\rho(\mathbf{r}')\Delta V[4\pi/(2l + 1)]^{1/2}r'^lY_{l,m}(\mathbf{r}')$ summed over the domain and the real spherical harmonics $Y_{l,m}$, which the paper lists in Cartesian form up to $l = 4$ (eqs. A1, A2). With the centre of mass as origin the dipole moments vanish and the higher moments stay small, so that Athena++ computes the centre of mass at every step by default, while a fixed origin chosen by the user suits a centre of mass that does not move. The accuracy depends on the order of the expansion: it is good for a centrally condensed mass far from the boundaries and worse when the mass extends close to them. A more general treatment of isolated boundaries, for instance the James algorithm ([Moon, Kim and Ostriker](#moon-et-al-2019) 2019), is planned.

The class `MultigridDriver` runs the solvers of all blocks (`src/multigrid/multigrid_driver.cpp`). It refuses meshes that are not three-dimensional or not uniformly spaced, selects FMG followed by V-cycles or V-cycles alone, and uses FAS by default when the mesh is refined (`MultigridDriver::MultigridDriver`). The boundary flags of the mesh for multigrid may be periodic, of zero gradient, zero-fixed, multipole or of the user, whose functions must be enrolled, and periodic flags must agree with those of the main mesh (`MultigridDriver::CheckBoundaryFunctions`). Averages are subtracted unless a boundary is zero-fixed, multipole or of the user, and the multipole expansion switches FAS on, as its boundaries must hold on every level. Before solving, `MultigridDriver::SetupMultigrid` applies the masks of the user to the source, subtracts its average where needed, computes the multipole moments, and for FMG restricts the source through the block objects, transfers it to the root grid and restricts it there. `MultigridDriver::TransferFromBlocksToRoot` gathers the single coarsest cell of each block, and with FAS its solution, by `MPI_Allgather`, or `MPI_Allgatherv` when the processes hold different numbers of blocks, into the root grid or into the octets of refined levels. `MultigridDriver::TransferFromRootToBlocks` hands each block its cell and the neighbours around it. `MultigridDriver::SubtractAverage` likewise gathers the totals of the blocks, divides by the volume of the mesh and subtracts the result.

`MultigridDriver::SolveVCycle` reverses the order of the two colours at every cycle and steps down by `MultigridDriver::OneStepToCoarser` and up by `MultigridDriver::OneStepToFiner`, through task lists on the block levels and by direct calls on the root grid. `MultigridDriver::SolveFMGCycle` applies one V-cycle on every level, with the prolongation of FMG between levels, subtracts the average of the potential where needed, and continues with `MultigridDriver::SolveIterative` when a threshold is given, or else with a fixed number of cycles. `MultigridDriver::SolveIterative` repeats V-cycles while the L2 norm of the defect, summed over the variables, exceeds the threshold. When a cycle reduces it by less than a factor 0.9 it stops for a threshold of zero, and warns of slow convergence otherwise; it stops with a warning when the norm grows, and gives up with a warning after more than 100 cycles. `MultigridDriver::SolveCoarsestGrid` sets the potential to zero when the coarsest level is one cell and averages are subtracted. Otherwise it runs as many red–black sweeps as the largest number of coarsest cells along a direction, with the over-relaxation of the smoother, between subtractions of the average where needed. Unlike the exact solution of eq. 19 that [Tomida and Stone](#tomida-stone-2023) (2023, §2.3.2) describe for a single coarsest cell, the code thus applies one over-relaxed sweep to such a cell under fixed, multipole or user boundaries, whose ghost values depend on the cell itself.

The boundary functions of multigrid set the ghost cells of an object (`src/multigrid/mgbval_zerofixed.cpp`, `mgbval_zerograd.cpp`, `mgbval_periodic.cpp`, `mgbval_multipole.cpp`). A zero-fixed boundary gives each ghost cell the opposite of its active neighbour, so that the face takes the value zero, a boundary of zero gradient copies the neighbour, and a periodic one copies the opposite side of the root grid. The multipole boundary evaluates the expansion at the centre of each face and sets the ghost value to twice it less the active value, for $l_{\max} = 2$ or 4, chosen by the parameter `mporder`. The moments are summed over the blocks, threads and processes, and their factors, $-1/(4\pi)$ for the monopole and the like, turn the moments of the source $4\pi G\rho$ into the coefficients of the potential (`Multigrid::CalculateMultipoleCoefficients`, `MultigridDriver::ScaleMultipoleCoefficients`). By default the origin follows the centre of mass from the dipole moments at every solve, and an option drops the dipole terms (`MultigridDriver::CalculateCenterOfMass`). The class `MGBoundaryValues` exchanges the ghost cells between objects and dispatches the boundary functions on the physical faces (`src/bvals/cc/mg/bvals_mg.cpp`; `MGBoundaryValues::ApplyPhysicalBoundaries`). It applies the same kind of boundary on every level, whereas [Tomida and Stone](#tomida-stone-2023) (2023, §2.3.6) describe zero-fixed boundaries for the equation of the defect on coarser levels in the correction scheme, unless the boundaries are periodic. The class `MultigridTaskList` orders the work on the block levels (`src/task_list/mg_task_list.cpp`). Going down, it exchanges boundaries, applies the physical ones, computes the right-hand side of FAS where needed, smooths red, exchanges again, smooths black, exchanges once more and restricts. Going up, it prolongates and corrects, or prolongates for FMG, and smooths in the same pattern, for zero, one or two smoothing sweeps.

#### 2.10.4 Multigrid on refined meshes and self-gravity by multigrid

The main integrator, VL2 or the Runge–Kutta methods of second or third order, advances the MHD through its task list, while multigrid runs a task list of its own ([Tomida and Stone](#tomida-stone-2023) 2023, §3.2.3). In every stage the density of the integrator is loaded into multigrid, the solver runs, and the potential returns to the MHD before the stage is integrated. A solve per stage is not the cheapest choice, but it is simple and suits any integrator. The source terms of gravity follow the cell-centred scheme of [Mullen, Hanawa and Gammie](#mullen-et-al-2021) (2021). The accelerations on the faces are differences of the potential, $g_{x,i\pm1/2} = \mp(\phi_{i\pm1} - \phi_i)/h$, the acceleration of the cell is their mean, and the momentum gains $\rho_ig_{x,i}$. The energy gains half the sum, over the six faces, of the mass flux of the Riemann solver times the acceleration on that face (eq. 22). This form is free of curl, unlike forms built on the gravitational flux such as that of [Jiang et al.](#jiang-et-al-2013) (2013), for which [Mullen, Hanawa and Gammie](#mullen-et-al-2021) (2021) give a curl-free variant. The conservative energy term of the latter is not used, so that energy is conserved only as well as the scheme is accurate, and neither momentum nor energy to round-off, as the potential keeps a residual. Merging the two task lists would overlap more communication with computation. They are kept apart for simplicity and for a future parallelisation in which some nodes compute self-gravity while others treat other physics, which could pay off as multigrid scales less well than the MHD.

On refined meshes, the creation, removal and balancing of blocks belong to the framework of AMR and do not change (§3.3; 3.4). Multigrid changes in two ways: refined objects must be handled on the coarse levels, and neighbours on different levels must exchange boundaries. As octree refinement always splits a block into $2 \times 2 \times 2$ finer ones, the levels between the block levels and the root grid hold such sets, octets, each with $2 \times 2 \times 2$ cells, ghost cells and its own potential, source and defect, and the relations between octets and blocks follow from the tree of blocks (§3.3.1; Figure 3). With AMR, FAS is used. Going down, the block levels proceed as on uniform grids until every block is one cell. Blocks that are not refined then pass their cell to the root grid, and refined ones to their cell in an octet. The octets of the finest level are smoothed and restricted, level by level, with boundaries towards octets of the same level updated and those towards coarser levels held at values prolongated from there, until the data reach the root grid and level 0. Going up, the coarsest level is solved as on uniform grids, the first octet level is created by prolongating cells of the root grid and smoothed, and so on through the octet levels, until the data return to the objects of the blocks, which are prolongated and smoothed to the finest level. Solvers that treat each refined level by itself, with boundaries from the coarser one, are not consistent, as the coarse solutions miss the fine structure and give the fine levels wrong boundaries. The iteration here instead solves the Poisson equation over all levels together (App. B). Unlike the iteration that [Trottenberg et al.](#trottenberg-et-al-2001) (2001) propose, which removes locally refined grids first, all blocks are restricted to one cell before any is removed, which keeps the load balanced, as the number of objects stays the same until the octet levels. FMG and parallelisation follow as on uniform grids.

The boundaries between levels decide whether the solver is consistent, and their ghost cells are computed by a method like that of [Feng et al.](#feng-et-al-2018) (2018), with one layer of ghost cells, which the seven-point Laplacian needs, although the MHD uses two or more (§3.3.2). A conservative form serves the exchanges before smoothing and before the forces are computed. For the Laplacian to be accurate at a level boundary, the acceleration on a coarse face must equal the mean of those on the four fine faces it covers, $(\phi_{I_s,J,K} - \phi_{I_s-1,J,K})/(2h) = \tfrac{1}{4}\sum_{a,b}(\phi_{i_s,j+a,k+b} - \phi_{i_s-1,j+a,k+b})/h$, with $h$ the fine spacing (eq. 23). This keeps the truncation error of the gradient free of divergence, so that no spurious mass appears at the boundary. While the MHD needs corrections of fluxes at such boundaries to conserve mass and momentum, multigrid satisfies conservation by the construction of its differences. The fine block sends the area average of its first layer of cells to the coarse neighbour, and the coarse block sends its first layer, with the cells of its edge and corner neighbours, restricted where those lie on the fine level, to the fine one. The fine block interpolates the coarse values to the transverse positions of its cells, $\phi_{I_s-1,J,K} \pm (\phi_{I_s-1,J+1,K} - \phi_{I_s-1,J-1,K})/8 \pm (\phi_{I_s-1,J,K+1} - \phi_{I_s-1,J,K-1})/8$, whose four values average to the coarse one (eqs. 24, 25). It then sets each ghost cell to one third of its active neighbour plus two thirds of the interpolated value (eq. 26), while the coarse block extrapolates its ghost value as $\phi_{I_s,J,K} = \tfrac{4}{3}\phi_{i_s,J,K} - \tfrac{1}{3}\phi_{I_s-1,J,K}$ (eq. 27). The mean fine gradient across each coarse face then equals the coarse gradient, as eq. 23 requires, with one set of independent messages. A coarse ghost cell that faces two or three fine blocks takes several values, which does no harm to smoothing or forces but not to prolongation, which needs one value. The red–black pattern cannot hold at a level boundary, where one coarse cell faces two red and two black fine cells, and the ghost cells there are updated after each smoothing. [Feng et al.](#feng-et-al-2018) (2018) extrapolate the transverse gradients of the fine block instead, their stencil $\triangle$, while the scheme here matches their stencil $\Pi$, which in some cases behaves better, as extrapolated gradients can differ much from the true ones. Prolongation, which needs ghost cells from edge and corner neighbours as well for its stencil of $3 \times 3 \times 3$ cells, uses a normal interpolation instead. The fine first layer is restricted by eq. 17 into the ghost cells of the coarse block, the coarse first layer, with neighbours restricted where needed, goes to the fine block, and trilinear prolongation by eq. 18 fills its ghost cells, again with one set of independent messages. Trilinear prolongation serves the boundaries of FMG as well, as it needs no further ghost cells and keeps the method of second order overall.

A rough comparison with solvers that treat one level at a time uses the binary problem of 2.10.5 with $h = 1/64$ on the root level (App. B). The error, normalised by eq. 31, in the region outside the refined blocks is a few $10^{-4}$ for the converged solution on the adaptive mesh, but about 1 per cent for a solution on the root level alone, which is what such solvers obtain on their coarsest level (Figure 13). As that solution gives the boundaries of the next level, its error passes to the finer levels, so that their solutions are expected to be over ten times less accurate, with errors of about a per cent, depending on the problem, the grid and the number of levels. Here the binary is poorly resolved on the root level, and better resolution of the density on coarse levels would reduce the error, but solvers of this kind should always suffer from it. The error is systematic rather than random and may matter in long runs, for instance in the torque of a binary that the coarse levels resolve poorly, so that adaptive grids for such solvers must be built with more care. The comparison indicates that the solver of Athena++ is more accurate and consistent.

`MGGravityDriver` and `MGGravity` implement self-gravity by multigrid (`src/gravity/mg_gravity.cpp`). The block `<gravity>` sets `mgmode`, which must be `FMG` or `MGI`, and either `threshold` for the defect or `niteration` for a fixed number of cycles, the latter ignored when both are given and a threshold of zero iterating until convergence stalls. It also sets `fas`, `npresmooth`, `npostsmooth`, `omega` with the default 1.15, `show_defect`, the boundary flags `ix1_bc` to `ox3_bc`, and for the multipole expansion `mporder`, which must be 2 or 4, `auto_mporigin`, true by default and not allowed with `nodipole`, or the origin `mporigin_x1` to `mporigin_x3`. Unlike the FFT solver, the driver refuses a gravitational constant left unset (`MGGravityDriver::MGGravityDriver`). `MGGravityDriver::Solve` loads $4\pi G\rho$ as source, and in MGI the previous potential as initial guess, sets up the hierarchy, runs FMG or MGI, and returns the potential with its ghost cells and, if `output_defect` is set, the defect. `MGGravity::Smooth` is RBGS with the over-relaxation `omega`, and `MGGravity::CalculateDefect` and `MGGravity::CalculateFASRHS` apply the seven-point Laplacian. The octets of the driver are rebuilt from the tree of blocks whenever the mesh changes, and smoothed, restricted, prolongated and given boundaries by functions of their own, with threads but without MPI, as every process holds them all (`MultigridDriver::SetupMultigrid`, `MultigridDriver::SetBoundariesOctets`). `MGBoundaryValues` exchanges boundaries with coarser and finer neighbours as well, and `MGGravityBoundaryValues::ProlongateMultigridBoundariesFluxCons` and its counterpart for octets, `MGGravityDriver::ProlongateOctetBoundariesFluxCons`, fill the ghost cells by eqs. 25 to 27 (`src/bvals/cc/mg/bvals_mg_gravity.cpp`). With `fill_ghost` set on refined meshes, the task list of 2.10.1 refills the ghost cells of the potential after the solve. It keeps the values of multigrid on faces towards coarser neighbours and prolongates the others (`Gravity::SaveFaceBoundaries`, `BoundaryValues::ProlongateBoundariesPostMG` in `src/bvals/bvals_refine_postmg.cpp`). Otherwise the ghost values of multigrid are used as they are. `HydroSourceTerms::SelfGravity` adds the source terms of eq. 22, as described later in 2.10.

#### 2.10.5 Tests of multigrid

The tests cover static problems on uniform and refined grids, the Jeans wave, in which hydrodynamics and gravity interact, and the collapse of a magnetised cloud on an adaptive mesh ([Tomida and Stone](#tomida-stone-2023) 2023, §4). The timings come from the Cray XC50 of the Center for Computational Astrophysics of the National Astronomical Observatory of Japan, with two Intel Xeon 6148 processors, 40 cores, per node, the Aries network, the Intel compiler of 2018 and Cray MPI 7.7.0, in pure MPI.

On a periodic uniform grid, the density $\rho = \rho_0 + A\sin(2\pi x/L_x)\sin(2\pi y/L_y)\sin(2\pi z/L_z)$ has the potential $-4\pi GA[(2\pi/L_x)^2 + (2\pi/L_y)^2 + (2\pi/L_z)^2]^{-1}$ times the same product of sines, with the zero point $\phi_0 = 0$, and the test takes $A = 1$, $\rho_0 = 2$ and $G = 1$ (§4.1, eqs. 28, 29). In a unit box at $h = 1/16$ to $1/1024$, with zero as the initial guess of MGI, the errors against the analytic solution, $\epsilon$, and against the converged discrete solution, $\delta$, taken after 20 cycles, and the defect are followed through the iterations (§4.1.1; Figure 5). All variants soon stop improving against the analytic solution, as they converge to the discrete solution. Each V-cycle reduces $\delta$ and the defect by a factor of about 0.125, as found elsewhere ([Yavneh](#yavneh-1996) 1996), so that 10 to 15 cycles reach convergence. Converged solutions of FMG and MGI are of second order, and the first sweep of FMG alone already reaches the truncation error, here an error even below that of the converged solution. As it still carries noise of short wavelength from the pattern of RBGS, a few further V-cycles, which damp that noise quickly, are advised, while iterating until the defect stalls is unnecessary, and $\delta \sim 10^{-6}$ suffices. The finest level dominates, so that one FMG sweep is worth about two V-cycles. MGI costs more unless its guess is very good, FMG gains with resolution, and the potential of the previous step proved usually too poor a guess, so that FMG almost always does better. In weak scaling, with fixed blocks and domains, often cuboids, that do not suit multigrid or FFT ideally but resemble practical runs, the performance falls within a node, as multigrid is limited by the bandwidth of memory (§4.1.2; Figure 6). From 2 to 200 nodes the efficiency is 89.6, 73.2 and 45.7 per cent for one sweep of FMG with blocks of $128^3$, $64^3$ and $32^3$ cells, and 92.9, 78.8 and 56.3 per cent with 10 further V-cycles. Eight blocks of $32^3$ per process perform like blocks of $32^3$. The many small messages make the solver sensitive to the latency and bandwidth of the network, so that the figures are means of the ten best of 100 runs, and crowded machines were up to about 30 per cent slower. Ten further V-cycles make FMG 5 to 6 times dearer than a single sweep. FFT does not scale as well, its cost growing as $O(N\log N)$ by nature rather than by fault of its implementation, so that multigrid wins in practice, above all in massively parallel runs. With blocks of $64^3$, a single FMG sweep takes about 15 per cent of the time of the MHD solver and FMG with 10 V-cycles about 70 per cent, while the second-order integrators solve twice per step, so that with the few cycles needed in practice gravity adds little to the cost of the MHD.

On a refined grid, the binary test places stars of masses $M_1 = 2$ at $(6/1024, 0, 0)$ and $M_2 = 1$ at $(-12/1024, 0, 0)$, each a uniform sphere of radius $R = 6/1024$, with cell densities averaged over $10^3$ subcells to smooth the surface (§4.2). All boundaries take the multipole expansion to the hexadecapole. The potential is the sum of $-GM/\Delta r$ outside each sphere and $-GM(3R^2 - \Delta r^2)/(2R^3)$ inside, with $\Delta r$ the distance from its centre. The domain $[-0.5, 0.5]^3$ is refined self-similarly, with $2^l$ times finer cells on $[-0.5^l, 0.5^l]^3$ up to $l = 4$. With $h = 1/64$ to $1/1024$ on the root grid and 16 times finer cells at the deepest level, the binary is not resolved on the root grid at low resolution, and MGI starts from the poor guess of zero (§4.2.1; Figure 7). The errors are normalised by the potential, $\delta = (\phi^*_h - \phi^n)/\phi^*_h$ and $\epsilon = (\phi^* - \phi^n)/\phi^*$, with the converged solution after 40 cycles (eqs. 30, 31). Both variants are of second order, but FMG reaches the analytic solution within a few cycles while MGI needs many more. FMG first converges with a factor of about 0.2 per cycle and later with about 0.5, as errors persist at the level boundaries, while MGI starts from a worse guess and converges more slowly. The defect shows the checkerboard of RBGS, and $\epsilon$ is small after the first sweep (Figure 8), so that again a few further V-cycles suffice. Strong and weak scaling, with three V-cycles after the first sweep, blocks of $16^3$ or $32^3$ cells and 36 cores per node, use root grids of $256^3$ or $512^3$ cells with four further levels, 18,432 blocks in all, from 36 processes with 512 blocks each to 2,304 with 8, and for weak scaling 8 blocks per process (§4.2.2; Figure 9). Performance degrades at large numbers of processes, the more so for small blocks, and with 2,304 processes multigrid is 2 to 6 times slower than the MHD on the same blocks and scales worse, mainly as the root-grid and octet levels are not parallel, comparably to the gravity solvers of other codes with AMR. The configuration of the grid matters, and blocks of $16^3$ cells are a reasonable start.

The Jeans wave uses a periodic box of $1 \times 0.5 \times 0.5$ with $\rho = \rho_0 + A\sin(\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{x})$, $A = 10^{-6}$ and $\mathbf{k} = (2\pi/L_x, 2\pi/L_y, 2\pi/L_z)$, so that $\lambda = 1/3$, an adiabatic gas with $\Gamma = 5/3$, $\rho_0 = 1$ and $p_0 = 1$, and the dispersion relation $\omega^2 = c_s^2k^2 - 4\pi G\rho_0$ with $c_s^2 = \Gamma p_0/\rho_0$ (§4.3, eqs. 32, 33). The ratio $n_{\mathrm{Jeans}}$ of $\lambda$ to the Jeans length $(\pi c_s^2/(G\rho_0))^{1/2}$ sets $G$. Stable waves start at rest, and growing ones with the momentum $(\mathbf{k}/k)(|\omega|/k)\rho_0A\cos(\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{x})$ (eq. 34), with PLM, VL2 and HLLC. For $n_{\mathrm{Jeans}} = 0.5$ the RMS over the five conserved variables of their L1 errors converges at about second order, both with converged potentials and with one sweep of FMG (Figure 10). The latter error is exactly of second order and dominated by the potential, while with converged potentials the hydrodynamics dominates and falls slightly short of second order through the limiter. At $t = 1.5$ the stable wave looks the same with both, but for $n_{\mathrm{Jeans}} = 1.1$ the growing wave keeps its symmetry with converged potentials and fragments early with one sweep of FMG, as the residual noise of the potential speeds up the instability and breaks the symmetry (Figure 11). Where a system is sensitive to noise, the error of the solver must be controlled. The problem generator `jeans` of 2.10.1 sets up this wave with $p_0 = 1/\gamma$, so that $c_s = 1$, and an adiabatic perturbation of the pressure, which the dispersion relation requires.

The collapse of a magnetised core shows self-gravity in MHD on an adaptive mesh (§4.4). A critical Bonnor–Ebert sphere at $T = 10$ K, with its density raised by $f = 1.2$ and a perturbation $m = 2$ of 10 per cent, has a mass of $1\,M_\odot$, a radius of 8,400 au, a central density $\rho_0 = 1.13 \times 10^{-18}$ g cm$^{-3}$ before the enhancement and $c_s = 0.19$ km s$^{-1}$. It rotates rigidly about $z$ at $\omega = 5.55 \times 10^{-14}$ s$^{-1}$, so that $\omega t_{\mathrm{ff}} = 0.1$ with the central free-fall time $(3\pi/(32Gf\rho_0))^{1/2} = 5.71 \times 10^4$ yr. Its uniform field $B_z = 27.4\,\mu$G gives the mass-to-flux ratio $\mu = 3$ in units of the critical value $0.53(5/G)^{1/2}/(3\pi)$ of a spherical cloud. The domain $[-10{,}400, 10{,}400]^3$ au has a root grid of $128^3$ cells and blocks of $16^3$, refined to resolve the Jeans length by at least 32 cells. The ambient pressure confines the cloud, the velocity outside its initial radius stays zero, and the multipole expansion, to the hexadecapole, ignores the mass outside that radius, to suppress high multipoles from gas near the edges. The MHD uses VL2, PLM and HLLD, gravity FMG with 10 further V-cycles, and the barotropic relation $P = \rho c_s^2[1 + (\rho/\rho_{\mathrm{crit}})^{2(\Gamma - 1)}]^{1/2}$ with $\rho_{\mathrm{crit}} = 10^{-13}$ g cm$^{-3}$ and $\Gamma = 5/3$ makes the gas isothermal at low and adiabatic at high density (eq. 35). At $t = 1.96 \times 10^5$ yr the centre reaches $10^{-8}$ g cm$^{-3}$ and 1,000 K, with 9 levels of refinement and a finest cell of 0.326 au (Figure 12). A first hydrostatic core has formed, nearly spherical as the field removes angular momentum efficiently, inside an infalling pseudo-disc not supported by rotation. Slow, wide bipolar outflows of about 1 km s$^{-1}$ leave the pseudo-disc by the magneto-centrifugal mechanism, and the magnetic interchange instability disturbs it near the core, where the collapse concentrates the field, in agreement with earlier simulations of ideal MHD. The run took about 4,900 core-hours, 30.7 hours on 160 cores, of which gravity took 85 to 90 per cent, depending on the grid.

[Tomida and Stone](#tomida-stone-2023) (2023, §5) conclude that their geometric multigrid solvers, built on the task lists of Athena++, with MGI and FMG in V(1,1) cycles, reach the analytic solution quickly at second order, FMG converging faster than MGI and needing no initial guess. One sweep of FMG alone gives a good solution, but its defect and its error against the converged solution stay large, with noise from RBGS and from level boundaries, so that 3 to 10 further V-cycles are advised, an amount that depends on the problem and should be checked. Uniform grids scale very well, but with AMR it depends strongly on the grid, as many small blocks make the serial part significant, which a further layer of parallelism for the root-grid and octet levels, or a split of processes between gravity and hydrodynamics, may improve. Solvers that treat one level at a time often serve individual time steps, but gravity acts instantaneously and needs solutions consistent across the whole mesh, hence the uniform time step and the global solver of Athena++. Multigrid suits octree blocks better than cell-based or patch-based AMR. The solver resembles that of SFUMATO, which also uses FMG on octree blocks with a uniform step but corrects fluxes at level boundaries, where Athena++ uses the formula of mass conservation of [Feng et al.](#feng-et-al-2018) (2018) without extra work or messages. Planned extensions include implicit moment methods for radiation and cosmic rays, the pressure of incompressible flow, Hodge–Helmholtz decompositions, particle-mesh gravity with AMR, curvilinear coordinates and a performance-portable version of Athena++ for GPUs. The code is public under the BSD licence.

In the code, `poisson` loads a static density for three tests: the sines above with $\rho_0 = 2$, a Plummer sphere $\rho = 3M/(4\pi a_0^3)(1 + r^2/a_0^2)^{-5/2}$ with $\phi = -GM/(r^2 + a_0^2)^{1/2}$, and $\rho = (4a_0^2r^2 - 6a_0)e^{-a_0r^2}$ with $\phi = 4\pi Ge^{-a_0r^2}$ (`src/pgen/poisson.cpp`). Without time steps it stores the analytic potential, times `ncycle` solves, by FFT with the continuous kernel or by multigrid, and reports the mean absolute error. Its input file has $64^3$ cells in one block over $[-0.5, 0.5]^3$, periodic, FMG with a threshold of zero, the defect as output, $4\pi G = 4\pi$ and 100 solves (`inputs/hydro/athinput.poisson`). `binary_gravity` sets up the binary of §4.2 with $10^3$ subcells per cell and reports the RMS of the absolute and normalised errors, their maxima, the maximum defect and timings (`src/pgen/binary_gravity.cpp`). Its input file refines statically, with blocks of $16^3$, four levels on $[-0.5/2^l, 0.5/2^l]^3$, FMG with a threshold of zero and multipole boundaries, on a root grid of $64^3$ (`inputs/hydro/athinput.binary_gravity`). `collapse` sets up the core of §4.4 in units with $4\pi G = 1$ and $c_s(10\,\mathrm{K}) = 1$, with the profile $f(1 + r^2/r_c^2)^{-3/2}$ that approximates a Bonnor–Ebert sphere, after the thesis of Tomida, and the perturbation $1 + A(r/r_c)^2\cos 2\varphi$ (`src/pgen/collapse.cpp`). Its rotation follows from `omegatff` and its field from `mu`, with the critical ratio above. A source term of the user zeroes the momentum outside the cloud and resets the energy to the barotropic relation, a mask removes the source of gravity outside the cloud, and refinement follows the number of cells per Jeans length `njeans`. Its input files, for hydrodynamics and MHD, use 15 levels, a root grid of $128^3$ over $[-16, 16]^3$, blocks of $16^3$, FMG with 10 cycles and multipole boundaries of order 4. The MHD one sets $A = 0.1$, $\mu = 3$, $\omega t_{\mathrm{ff}} = 0.03$ and `njeans` = 16, against the 0.1 and 32 cells of the paper (`inputs/hydro/athinput.collapse`, `inputs/mhd/athinput.collapse`). The regression test `unstable_jeans_3d_mg` runs the growing Jeans mode with multigrid as `unstable_jeans_3d_fft` does with FFT (2.10.1), to $t = 0.04$ (`tst/regression/scripts/tests/grav/unstable_jeans_3d_mg.py`).

#### 2.10.6 Conservative source terms of self-gravity

[Mullen, Hanawa and Gammie](#mullen-et-al-2021) (2021) implement in Athena++ a scheme for self-gravitating flows whose source terms conserve total momentum and total energy (abstract). Source terms evaluated properly are conservative when they equal the divergence of a gravitational flux, a stress tensor for the momentum or an energy flux for the energy. Their tests show second-order convergence and conservation of total momentum and energy to round-off, and the scheme suppresses the spurious accelerations that a common discretisation of the gravitational stress tensor causes, as it does not guarantee a gravity free of curl. Self-gravity matters in core-collapse supernovae, giant impacts, the formation of planets and stars and mergers of white dwarfs, often together with fields, radiation and complex equations of state (§1). Beside fast solvers of the Poisson equation, by FFT or multigrid, the accelerations and the release of gravitational energy need care. [Jiang et al.](#jiang-et-al-2013) (2013) conserve momentum and energy through the divergence of the stress tensor of gravity and that of an energy flux, a scheme of gravitational fluxes that keeps self-gravitating equilibria in shape under advection. Earlier source-term schemes, which the authors follow, avoid the large errors that such fluxes cause where the density implied by the discrete Poisson equation differs from the local one. A common discretisation of the stress tensor, used by Athena ([Stone et al.](#stone-et-al-2008) 2008) as by Athena++ up to version 19.0 ([Stone et al.](#stone-et-al-2020) 2020), also yields accelerations with curl, spurious near a body of mass $M$ and size $L$ wherever the surrounding density falls below about $(M/L^3)(\Delta x/L)^2$.

The momentum and energy equations of self-gravitating hydrodynamics, with $\nabla^2\phi = 4\pi G\rho$, carry the source terms $\rho\mathbf{g}$ and $\rho\mathbf{v}\cdot\mathbf{g}$, with $\mathbf{g} = -\nabla\phi$ free of curl (§2.1, eqs. 1 to 7). [Jiang et al.](#jiang-et-al-2013) (2013) write them in conservative form, with the stress tensor $\mathsf{T}_g = [\nabla\phi\nabla\phi - \tfrac{1}{2}(\nabla\phi\cdot\nabla\phi)\mathsf{I}]/(4\pi G)$, the energy density $E_g = \rho\phi/2$ and the energy flux $\mathbf{F}_g = (\phi\nabla\dot{\phi} - \dot{\phi}\nabla\phi)/(8\pi G) + \rho\mathbf{v}\phi$ (eqs. 8 to 12). A further conservative form, $\partial_t(E - \mathbf{g}\cdot\mathbf{g}/(8\pi G)) + \nabla\cdot[(E + P)\mathbf{v} + \mathbf{F}'_g] = 0$ with $\mathbf{F}'_g = -\phi\,\partial_t\mathbf{g}/(4\pi G) + \rho\mathbf{v}\phi$, has the energy density $E'_g = -\mathbf{g}\cdot\mathbf{g}/(8\pi G)$, whose integral equals that of $E_g$ when the surface term vanishes, with periodic boundaries or mass far from them (eqs. 13 to 20). Source terms conserve exactly when $\rho\mathbf{g} = -\nabla\cdot\mathsf{T}_g$ and $\rho\mathbf{v}\cdot\mathbf{g} = -\nabla\cdot\mathbf{F}'_g + \partial_t(\mathbf{g}\cdot\mathbf{g})/(8\pi G)$ hold cell by cell (§2.2, eqs. 21 to 24).

On a uniform Cartesian grid the Poisson equation is discretised by centred differences, and the gravity sits on the faces as differences of the potential, so that the Poisson equation becomes the divergence of the face gravities (§2.3, eqs. 25 to 30). Multiplied by $\phi$, it shows that the discrete energies $\sum\phi\rho\Delta V/2$ and $-\sum\Delta V$ times the mean of the six squared face gravities over $8\pi G$ agree up to surface terms (eqs. 31 to 33). As $-\nabla\cdot\mathsf{T}_g = -(\nabla\cdot\mathbf{g})\mathbf{g}/(4\pi G) - (\nabla\times\mathbf{g})\times\mathbf{g}/(4\pi G)$, only the normal face gravities, which the Poisson equation contains, should enter the stress tensor (§2.3.1, eq. 34). The discretisation of Athena and Athena++ takes tangential gravities as means of four neighbouring normal ones and squares them in the diagonal components, which is of second order but does not keep the gravity free of curl (eqs. 35 to 38). The authors instead average the products of neighbouring tangential gravities in the diagonal components, keeping the others (eqs. 39 to 42). The divergence of this tensor reduces to $\rho_{i,j,k}g_{x,i,j,k}$ and likewise along $y$ and $z$, with the cell gravity the mean of the gravities on the two faces, $g_{x,i,j,k} = (g_{x,i+1/2,j,k} + g_{x,i-1/2,j,k})/2$, and this gravity has no curl by central differences (eqs. 43 to 51; App. A). For the energy, the equivalence becomes $\rho\mathbf{v}\cdot\mathbf{g} = -\nabla\cdot(\rho\mathbf{v}\phi) - \phi\,\partial_t\rho$, with the density change from the continuity equation, and with the face potentials $\phi_{i+1/2} = (\phi_i + \phi_{i+1})/2$ the source becomes half the sum, over the six faces, of the mass flux times the face gravity (§2.3.2, eqs. 52 to 57). The mass flux must be the one that updates the density, and the gravity must be averaged over the step, $\tfrac{1}{2}[\mathbf{g}(t_0) + \mathbf{g}(t_0 + \Delta t)]$, as the time derivative of $\mathbf{g}\cdot\mathbf{g}$ requires (eqs. 58, 59).

With VL2 the algorithm needs the potential $\phi^{(0)}$ at the start (§3.1).
