The half step advances the conserved variables by their fluxes, the Poisson equation is solved for the new density $\rho^{(1)}$, and the source terms are added with the momentum term $\rho^{(0)}$ times the mean face gravity of $\phi^{(0)}$ and the energy term from the mass fluxes of the half step and $\mathbf{g}^{(0,1)} = \tfrac{1}{2}(\mathbf{g}^{(0)} + \mathbf{g}^{(1)})$ (eqs. 60 to 64). The full step repeats this with the fluxes and density of the half step and $\mathbf{g}^{(0,2)}$, after a solve for $\rho^{(2)}$ (eqs. 65 to 68). The scheme is of second order in space and time and needs two Poisson solves per step, but it must store $\phi^{(0)}$ and solve the Poisson equation after the update of the density and before the energy source, and App. B extends it to RK2 and RK3 ([Gottlieb, Ketcheson and Shu](#gottlieb-et-al-2009) 2009; [Ketcheson](#ketcheson-2010) 2010), with effective mass fluxes and the release of the previous stage removed before that of the next. Total momentum and energy are conserved to round-off only if the Poisson equation is solved to round-off, as FFT does and multigrid, with its residual, does not (§3.2). Energy needs exact potentials only at the start and end of a step, not at intermediate stages. Momentum needs the density implied by the discrete Poisson equation to match $\rho_{i,j,k}$, but a uniform corrective acceleration $\mathbf{g}_{\mathrm{corr}} = \sum\Delta\rho\,\mathbf{g}/\sum\rho$ restores its conservation without stresses or tides (eqs. 69 to 71). Outside a body of mass $M$ and radius $R$ with an atmosphere of mass $M_a$, Taylor expansion shows both schemes to be of second order, but the error of the source terms stays finite as $M_a \to 0$ while that of the fluxes with the common tensor diverges, reaching the true acceleration near $r \sim R$ when $M_a/M$ falls to about $\tfrac{3}{4}(R/\Delta x)^{-2}$, about $10^{-2}$ for $R/\Delta x = 10$ (§3.3, eqs. 72 to 79; Figure 1).

The tests use the FFT solver of 2.10.1, HLLC, a $\gamma$-law gas, VL2 and PLM on uniform Cartesian grids (§4). Spitzer sheets, polytropic equilibria of the one-dimensional Poisson equation with $G = K = 1$, $\Gamma = \gamma = 1.2$, mean density 0.3 and $L_z = 4$, with density contrasts of about $10^4$, are advected once across the periodic domain at $v_z = 1$ on 16 to 2,048 cells (§4.1, eqs. 80 to 83). The L1 errors of the density converge at second order and total momentum is conserved to round-off, with source terms as with gravitational fluxes, both with the energy term of eq. 57 (Figure 2). Jeans waves in three dimensions, of wavelength $\lambda = 1$ on $2N \times N \times N$ cells over $3 \times 1.5 \times 1.5$, with the wavevector $(2\pi/\lambda)(1/3, 2/3, 2/3)$, $\rho_0 = 1$, $P_0 = 1/\gamma$, $\gamma = 5/3$ and $A = 10^{-6}$, start from travelling perturbations of density, pressure and velocity (§4.2, eqs. 84 to 88). Stable waves with $\lambda/\lambda_J = 1/2$ converge at second order after one period on $N = 8$ to 256 for VL2, RK2 and RK3 with PLM and PPM, the gravity limiting the order, while higher-order methods lower the errors (§4.2.1, eq. 89; Figure 3). An unstable wave with $\lambda/\lambda_J = 3/2$ collapses to a sheet at $t \sim 3\lambda_J/c_{s,0}$ and to filaments at $t \sim 7\lambda_J/c_{s,0}$, and total energy, with the gravitational part $\sum\rho\phi\Delta V/2$, and momentum stay conserved to round-off to $t \sim 10\lambda_J/c_{s,0}$ (§4.2.2, eq. 90; Figure 4). A polytrope with $\Gamma = \gamma = 2$, $\rho = \rho_c\sin(\alpha r)/(\alpha r)$ with $\alpha = (2\pi G\rho_c/p_c)^{1/2}$, inside the atmosphere of §3.3 with $M_a = 10^{-2}M$, $G = M = R = 1$ and $80^3$ cells over $(8R)^3$ with periodic boundaries, is followed for ten dynamical times $R/v_{\mathrm{esc}}$ (§4.3, eqs. 91, 92). Gravitational fluxes with the common tensor destroy the atmosphere, through inflow along the axes and outflow along the diagonals that leave an $m = 4$ pattern, related to the curl of their gravity, while source terms, and fluxes with the new tensor, keep the equilibrium, all conserving momentum and energy to round-off (§4.3.1; Figures 5, 6). With white noise of amplitude $10^{-4}GM/R$ added to the potential as a residual, fluxes still conserve momentum but give spurious velocities in low-density regions, while source terms conserve momentum only with $\mathbf{g}_{\mathrm{corr}}$ and show no spurious motion (§4.3.2; Figure 7). An energy source $\rho\mathbf{v}\cdot\mathbf{g}$ from cell values, without the average in time, heats the polytrope steadily, which expands and loses about 30 per cent of its central density by 100 dynamical times, while the conservative scheme settles into equilibrium after about 20 (§4.3.3, eq. 93; Figure 8).

The constraint $\nabla\times\mathbf{g} = 0$ mirrors $\nabla\cdot\mathbf{B} = 0$ of CT, with curl and divergence exchanged (§5). The diagonal components of the new tensor average geometric means of tangential gravities, which can be negative near faces where the gravity changes sign. The spurious accelerations first appeared in models of giant impacts with low-mass atmospheres and debris discs and may affect any problem of large contrast in density. The scheme adds modest complexity, storage for the initial gravity or for earlier mass fluxes, no extra Poisson solves for second-order integrators and no elliptic solve for $\dot{\phi}$, but a Poisson solve between the update of the density and the energy source, the largest hurdle for existing codes. [Mullen, Hanawa and Gammie](#mullen-et-al-2021) (2021, §6) conclude that properly evaluated source terms of self-gravity are fully conservative, of second order in space and time and compatible with many integrators, Riemann solvers and reconstructions. Spitzer sheets give nearly the errors of the flux scheme, Jeans waves show second order with all integrators and conservation of energy to round-off, and polytropes keep their equilibria for over 100 dynamical times, free of the spurious accelerations of the common tensor and of the heating of non-conservative sources. Energy conservation needs exact potentials only at the ends of a step, which may allow looser thresholds at intermediate stages of iterative solvers, while residual errors may matter less than floors and ceilings. AMR is not yet included, but extends straightforwardly and was to follow multigrid on refined meshes.

`HydroSourceTerms::SelfGravity` of Athena++ adds, in each stage, the momentum term $\rho_{i,j,k}$ times the mean of the two face gravities along each axis, with the density of the stage and the potential solved from it, as in eqs. 43 to 48 (`src/hydro/srcterms/self_gravity.cpp`). For a non-barotropic gas the energy gains half the sum, over the faces, of the Riemann mass flux of the stage times the face gravity of the same potential. Unlike eq. 57 with eqs. 63 and 67, the gravity in the energy term is not averaged over the step, and the Poisson equation is solved after each stage rather than between the update of the density and the energy source, so that, as the comment of the function states, the energy term is not conservative.

### 2.11 Diffusion and super time-stepping

#### 2.11.1 Viscosity, thermal conduction and non-ideal MHD

The terms of 2.1.2 for viscosity, conduction, resistivity and ambipolar diffusion can be integrated explicitly in every stage of the integrator, unsplit, which evolves them at the temporal order of the main scheme and is the most accurate choice ([Stone et al.](#stone-et-al-2020) 2020, §3.2.5). They enter as divergences of fluxes, which conserves momentum, energy and magnetic flux, with the viscous stress, the heat flux and the electric field from differences of second order, while higher-order methods might need higher-order differences. Explicit diffusion limits the step to a multiple of the square of the cell size, which can cripple runs at low Reynolds number or high resolution. A module of super time-stepping by the Runge–Kutta–Legendre methods of [Meyer, Balsara and Aslam](#meyer-et-al-2012) (2012, [2014](#meyer-et-al-2014)), RKL1 of first and RKL2 of second order in time, therefore advances the diffusion in a separate, operator-split super step of $s$ stages, worth $O(s^2)$ explicit steps (2.11.2). The super step equals the full step of the MHD for RKL1 and half of it for RKL2, which takes two such steps per update. Both converge at the expected rates for smooth flows, give speed-ups of about $s$, and run in parallel through the task lists.

A two-dimensional variant of the test of linear waves (2.16) checks viscosity, resistivity and conduction (§3.3.2). On a domain of $(2/\sqrt{5}) \times (1/\sqrt{5})$, a linearised fast wave of $\lambda = 1$ at $\theta = \arctan 2 \approx 63.43^\circ$ to the $x_1$ axis, with amplitude $A = 10^{-4}$ and speed $c_f = 2$, runs to $t = 0.75$ at a Courant number of 0.4 with the explicit unsplit diffusion. Kinematic viscosities from $10^{-2}$ to $10^{-4}$ are used with Prandtl and magnetic Prandtl numbers of 1/2, that is $\kappa = \eta = \nu/2$, and the decay rate follows from a weighted least-squares fit to $\ln\max|v_2|$ in time. After [Ryu, Jones and Frank](#ryu-et-al-1995) (1995, eq. 3.13), the fast wave decays at $\Gamma_f = [19\nu/4 + 3\eta + 3\kappa(\gamma - 1)^2/(4\gamma)]\,2k^2/15$ with $k = 2\pi$, to first order in the coefficients and for $\nu k$ and $\eta k$ small compared with the wave speeds, which defines the Reynolds number $R_f = 4\pi^2c_f/(\lambda\Gamma_f) = 8\pi^2/\Gamma_f$ (eqs. 22 to 24). For Reynolds numbers from 134 to 13,452, the measured rates converge to the analytic ones as the mesh is refined, those of RK3 with PPM much closer at high resolution than those of VL2 with PLM (Figure 12). At the largest Reynolds number, the error of the decay rate converges at second order, RK3 with PPM approaching the analytic rate much faster at first, until below about $10^{-4}$ the initial data, an eigenmode of ideal rather than non-ideal MHD, limit it (Figure 13).

Ambipolar diffusion is tested by the oblique C-shock of [Masson et al.](#masson-et-al-2012) (2012), after [Wardle and Ng](#wardle-ng-1999) (1999), with an adiabatic gas so that its heating and energy flux are tested too (§3.3.4). The left state is $(\rho, v_x, v_y, B_x, B_y, P) = (0.5, 5, 0, \sqrt{2}, \sqrt{2}, 0.125)$ and the right one $(0.9880, 2.5303, 1.1415, \sqrt{2}, 3.4327, 1.4075)$, with $\eta_{\mathrm{AD}} = 1/(75\rho)$. To reduce the symmetry, the domain $[-0.5, 0.5] \times [-0.0078125, 0.0078125]$ at a resolution of 1/128 has the interface rotated by $\arctan(3/4)$, with shifted periodic boundaries along $y$ ([Tomida, Okuzumi and Machida](#tomida-et-al-2015) 2015, App. A.6) and outflow along $x$. By $t = 10$ the shock is steady, and its profile along the direction of propagation matches the analytic solution even at this low resolution (Figure 16).

In the code, each `Hydro` holds a `HydroDiffusion` and each `Field` a `FieldDiffusion` (`src/hydro/hydro_diffusion/`, `src/field/field_diffusion/`). The parameters `nu_iso` and `kappa_iso` of the block `<problem>` switch on isotropic viscosity and, for a non-barotropic gas, conduction, and `eta_ohm` and `eta_ad` Ohmic resistivity and ambipolar diffusion, while relativity refuses all of them. A problem generator may replace the constant coefficients by functions enrolled with `Mesh::EnrollViscosityCoefficient`, `Mesh::EnrollConductionCoefficient` and `Mesh::EnrollFieldDiffusivity`. `HydroDiffusion::ViscousFluxIso` builds the viscous stress of eq. 7 on each face from differences of the velocity, with the coefficient and the density averaged to the face, and adds to the energy flux the stress times the mean velocity of the two cells (`src/hydro/hydro_diffusion/viscosity.cpp`). `HydroDiffusion::ThermalFluxIso` adds $-\rho\kappa\nabla T$ with $T = p/\rho$ and the density averaged to the face, so that `kappa_iso` is a thermal diffusivity and $\rho\kappa$ the conductivity of the heat flux of 2.1.2 (`src/hydro/hydro_diffusion/conduction.cpp`). The parameters `nu_aniso` and `kappa_aniso` are read and set coefficients, but `HydroDiffusion::ViscousFluxAniso` and `HydroDiffusion::ThermalFluxAniso` are empty, so that they have no effect. The fluxes are added to those of the integrator before the update, and for the stages of super time-stepping in place of them (2.11.2). `HydroDiffusion::NewDiffusionDt` limits the step to $\Delta x^2/\nu$ and $\Delta x^2/\kappa$ times 1/2, 1/4 or 1/6 for one, two or three dimensions (`src/hydro/hydro_diffusion/hydro_diffusion.cpp`). `FieldDiffusion` computes the current on the edges and the electric fields $\eta\mathbf{J}$ and $\eta_{\mathrm{AD}}\mathbf{J}_\perp$, with $\mathbf{J}_\perp$ the current perpendicular to $\mathbf{B}$, which equals the form of 2.1.2, adds them to those of CT, and adds the Poynting flux of these fields to the energy flux (`src/field/field_diffusion/diffusivity.cpp`). By default $\eta = $ `eta_ohm` and $\eta_{\mathrm{AD}} = $ `eta_ad`$\,|\mathbf{B}|^2$, whereas [Stone et al.](#stone-et-al-2020) (2020, §3.1) provide no default form for $\eta_{\mathrm{AD}}$ (2.1.2). A Hall diffusivity `eta_hall`$\,|\mathbf{B}|/\rho$ is set from `eta_hall` but enters no electric field.

The problem generators `visc` and `resist` diffuse a Gaussian of $v_2$ or of $B_2$ in one dimension, or of $v_3$ or $B_3$ in two, from the analytic profile at $t_0$, with the amplitude, $t_0$, the coefficient and the centre as parameters, and compare with the analytic profile at the end (`src/pgen/visc.cpp`, `src/pgen/resist.cpp`). The regression tests `viscous_diffusion` and `resistive_diffusion` run the one-dimensional Gaussians with $A = 10^{-6}$, $\nu$ or $\eta = 0.25$ and $t_0 = 0.5$ to $t = 2$ on 256 and 512 cells over a length of 12, explicitly with a Courant number of 0.8, and require an L1 error that falls faster than the resolution to the power $-1.99$ (`tst/regression/scripts/tests/diffusion/`). `thermal_attenuation` runs a decaying linear wave of `linear_wave` with $\kappa = 0.04$ to $t = 3$ on $32 \times 16 \times 16$ and $64 \times 32 \times 32$ cells, and `linear_wave3d` a decaying slow wave with $\nu = 0.01$ and $\kappa = \eta = 0.02$. They require relative errors of the decay rate below 0.38 and 0.10, or 0.22 and 0.05, and for the latter a convergence rate of at least 2.

#### 2.11.2 Super time-stepping

Super time-stepping is compiled in with the option `-sts` of `configure.py` and implemented by the task list `SuperTimeStepTaskList` (`src/task_list/sts_task_list.cpp`). The parameter `sts_integrator` of the block `<time>` selects RKL1 or RKL2, the latter by default, and the task list refuses other values and runs that set no diffusive process. With super time-stepping the time step of the integrator ignores the limit of diffusion, unless `sts_max_dt_ratio` caps the ratio of the step to that limit (`src/hydro/new_blockdt.cpp`; `Mesh::NewTimeStep`). The main loop then splits the diffusion off (`src/main.cpp`). RKL1 advances it by the whole step before the main integrator, while RKL2 advances it by half the step before and half after. The number of stages $s$ follows from the ratio of the step $\Delta\tau$ of diffusion to the explicit limit $\Delta t_{\mathrm{par}}$ as the integer part of $\tfrac{1}{2}[-1 + (1 + 8\Delta\tau/\Delta t_{\mathrm{par}})^{1/2}]$ plus one for RKL1 and of $\tfrac{1}{2}[-1 + (9 + 16\Delta\tau/\Delta t_{\mathrm{par}})^{1/2}]$ plus one for RKL2, raised to the next odd number when even. These invert the extended limits $\Delta\tau \le \Delta t_{\mathrm{par}}(s^2 + s)/2$ and $\Delta\tau \le \Delta t_{\mathrm{par}}(s^2 + s - 2)/4$ of the methods of [Meyer, Balsara and Aslam](#meyer-et-al-2012) (2012, [2014](#meyer-et-al-2014)).

Each stage $j$ computes only the diffusive fluxes of viscosity, conduction, resistivity and the passive scalars, and updates the affected variables as $Y_j = \mu_jY_{j-1} + \nu_jY_{j-2} + \tilde{\mu}_j\Delta\tau\,M(Y_{j-1})$ for RKL1, with $\mu_j = (2j - 1)/j$, $\nu_j = (1 - j)/j$ and $\tilde{\mu}_j = 2\mu_j/(s^2 + s)$ (`SuperTimeStepTaskList::StartupTaskList`). For RKL2 the update adds $(1 - \mu_j - \nu_j)Y_0 + \tilde{\gamma}_j\Delta\tau\,M(Y_0)$, with $b_j = (j^2 + j - 2)/[2j(j + 1)]$ and $b_0 = b_1 = b_2 = 1/3$, $\mu_j = (2j - 1)b_j/(jb_{j-1})$, $\nu_j = -(j - 1)b_j/(jb_{j-2})$, $\tilde{\mu}_j = 4\mu_j/(s^2 + s - 2)$, $\tilde{\mu}_1 = 4b_1/(s^2 + s - 2)$ and $\tilde{\gamma}_j = -(1 - b_{j-1})\tilde{\mu}_j$. The update covers the momenta and energy for viscosity, the energy for conduction and for resistivity with a non-barotropic gas, the field through the electric fields of diffusion and CT, and the passive scalars. Each stage exchanges boundaries and recomputes the primitive variables as the main task list does, with the times of the shearing boundaries set to the start or the end of the step.

The regression tests `viscous_diffusion_sts`, `resistive_diffusion_sts` and `scalar_diffusion_sts` repeat the Gaussians of 2.11.1 and 2.12 with RKL1 and RKL2 and require convergence of the L1 error at first and second order, faster than the resolution to the powers $-0.99$ and $-1.99$ (`tst/regression/scripts/tests/diffusion/`). `thermal_attenuation_sts` and `linear_wave3d_sts` repeat the decaying waves with RKL2, the latter requiring a convergence rate of at least 1.

### 2.12 Passive scalars

#### 2.12.1 Advection

The solver of MHD can carry any number of passive scalars along with the flow ([Stone et al.](#stone-et-al-2020) 2020, §3.2.6). Each satisfies a transport equation of its own in conservative form, $\partial(\rho C_i)/\partial t + \nabla\cdot(\rho\mathbf{v}C_i) = 0$ (eq. 20), where the specific density $C_i$ of scalar $i$ is its primitive variable and the mass density $\rho C_i$ of the species its conserved one. Scalars trace transport and mixing in a flow, and they are needed when chemical or nuclear reactions are coupled to the dynamics. The net rates of the reactions then enter the right-hand sides of the transport equations as source terms, as a rule by operator splitting. A later paper is to describe the chemistry of Athena++ (2.15).

The option `--nscalars` of `configure.py` sets the number `NSCALARS` of scalars, zero by default. Each block holds a `PassiveScalars` with the conserved scalars `s`, the concentrations `r` as primitive variables and the fluxes `s_flux[3]`, indexed like the arrays of `Hydro` with the scalar first (`src/scalars/scalars.hpp`; 2.1.4). The registers `s1` and, where needed, `s2`, `s0` and `s_fl_div` serve the integrators as `u1`, `u2`, `u0` and `fl_div` serve the gas. The constructor registers `s` for restart files, for mesh refinement with a coarse buffer, and as a variable at cell centres for the exchange of boundaries, in the stages of super time-stepping as well when the scalars diffuse (`src/scalars/scalars.cpp`; 3.3, 3.4). Outputs name the conserved scalars `s0`, `s1` and so on and the concentrations `r0`, `r1` and so on, which the variables `cons` and `prim` include, and the history adds the integral of each conserved scalar over the volume (`src/outputs/outputs.cpp`, `src/outputs/history.cpp`; 3.7). With chemistry, the first `NSPECIES` scalars, set by `--nspecies`, hold the abundances of the network and take the names of its species (2.15).

`PassiveScalars::CalculateFluxes` runs after the fluxes of the gas, whose mass fluxes it needs (`src/scalars/calculate_scalar_fluxes.cpp`). Along each direction it reconstructs the concentrations by the method that `xorder` selects for the gas, in its variants for arrays of any number of variables (2.4.1, 2.4.6), and by donor cell in the first stage of VL2, as the gas does (2.6.5). On each face `PassiveScalars::ComputeUpwindFlux` multiplies the mass flux $F_\rho$ of the Riemann solver by the state on its upwind side, $F_\rho C_L$ for $F_\rho \ge 0$ and $F_\rho C_R$ otherwise, so that the scalars need no Riemann solver of their own. After PPM or WENO, but not after donor cell or PLM, the states on the faces are raised to the floor `sfloor`, one for all scalars (2.2.1). For the fourth order the face averages of the states become values at the centres of the faces, the upwind rule with the pointwise mass fluxes that `Hydro::CalculateFluxes` keeps in `mass_flux_fc` gives pointwise fluxes, and the transverse Laplacian of the fluxes from the face averages corrects these to face averages, as for the gas (2.7.1). The fluxes of diffusion are added at the end, unless super time-stepping treats them (2.12.2). **[GK]** With $C_L = C_R = C$ on every face, the scalar fluxes are $C$ times the mass fluxes, so that an update by fluxes turns $\rho C$ into $C$ times the new density, and a uniform concentration stays uniform.

In each stage `TimeIntegratorTaskList::IntegrateScalars` updates `s` with the same weighted averages of the registers and the same flux divergence as the gas, the extra step of `ssprk5_4` included (`src/task_list/time_integrator.cpp`; `PassiveScalars::AddFluxDivergence` in `src/scalars/add_scalar_flux_divergence.cpp`; 2.6.5). The source terms of the stage follow the updates of gas and scalars. After the exchange of boundaries, `EquationOfState::PassiveScalarConservedToPrimitive` raises each $\rho C_i$ to at least `sfloor` times the density, since fluxes in several dimensions can drive it lower, and divides by the conserved density `u(IDN)` of the gas, for every EOS and in relativity as well (`src/eos/eos_scalars.cpp`). A note in the code warns that this floor, which adds scalar mass, can violate global conservation strongly, for instance in the first cycles of the slotted cylinder (2.8.7). `EquationOfState::ApplyPassiveScalarFloors` raises only primitive values and leaves `s` as it is. For the fourth order, `EquationOfState::PassiveScalarConservedToPrimitiveCellAverage` repeats the inversion of 2.7.1 with the values of the density at the cell centres and floors the averages of $C_i$ that result, again without changing $\rho C_i$. `EquationOfState::PassiveScalarPrimitiveToConserved` sets $\rho C_i$ from $C_i$, for problem generators such as the relativistic shock tube.

Between blocks, across levels of refinement and across shearing-periodic boundaries the conserved scalars are exchanged, with the corrections of the fluxes at the boundaries of levels and of the shearing box (2.9.3), and the conversion to concentrations covers the ghost cells that neighbours fill. The physical boundaries act on the concentrations, as those of the gas act on its primitive variables (`TimeIntegratorTaskList::PhysicalBoundary`). Orbital advection remaps the conserved scalars like the conserved variables of the gas (`src/orbital_advection/calculate_orbital_advection.cpp`; 2.9.4). The source function of a problem generator receives the concentrations and the conserved scalars with the variables of the gas and can add the rates of reactions (`HydroSourceTerms::AddSourceTerms`; 2.9.1). With chemistry, a solver of ordinary differential equations integrates the network over the whole step in the last stage (`TimeIntegratorTaskList::IntegrateChemistry`; 2.15). With the parameter `active` of the block `<hydro>` set to `background` or `disabled`, the gas keeps its initial state while its mass fluxes still carry the scalars, and the step follows from the widths of the cells and the velocity alone, without the sound speed (`src/hydro/new_blockdt.cpp`), as in the tests of [Mignone](#mignone-2014) (2014) in 2.8.7. With `fixed` the scalars stay fixed too, and the step is `tlim`/`nlim` (`Mesh::NewTimeStep`).

#### 2.12.2 Diffusion and tests

A positive `nu_scalar_iso` of the block `<problem>` lets all scalars diffuse with one constant coefficient $\nu$, through the flux $-\nu\bar{\rho}\nabla C_i$ (`src/scalars/scalar_diffusion.cpp`). `PassiveScalars::DiffusiveFluxIso` takes $\bar{\rho}$ as the mean density of the two cells of a face and the gradient as the difference of the concentrations over the distance of their centres, divided by the scale factors of curvilinear coordinates. Unlike the coefficients of 2.11.1, $\nu$ cannot be made a function of position or of the variables. In each stage `TimeIntegratorTaskList::DiffuseScalars` computes these fluxes from the concentrations and the density of the gas, and `PassiveScalars::AddDiffusionFluxes` adds them to the advective ones, while with super time-stepping they enter only its stages (2.11.2). `PassiveScalars::NewDiffusionDt` joins the limits of diffusion with $\Delta x^2/\nu$, $\Delta x$ being the smallest width of a cell, times the factor of 2.11.1, from 1/2 in one dimension to 1/6 in three (`src/hydro/new_blockdt.cpp`).

The problem generator `scalar_diff` sets a gas of unit density at rest and every concentration to the Gaussian $A(4\pi\nu t_0)^{-d/2}\exp[-|\mathbf{x} - \mathbf{x}_0|^2/(4\nu t_0)]$ in $d = 1$, 2 or 3 dimensions, for the parameter `iprob` equal to 0, 1 or 2 (`src/pgen/scalar_diff.cpp`). The distance is Cartesian, also with cylindrical coordinates in two and three dimensions and with spherical-polar ones in three, so that diffusion is tested in curvilinear coordinates too. The amplitude $A$, the time $t_0$, $\nu$ and the centre $\mathbf{x}_0$ are parameters. With adaptive refinement a block refines where the largest magnitude of the centred differences of the first concentration exceeds the parameter `thr` and derefines where it stays below a quarter of it. The input file runs the one-dimensional case on 128 cells over $[-6, 6]$ to $t = 2$ with VL2 and PLM (`inputs/hydro/athinput.scalar_diff`). The regression test `scalar_diffusion` runs it with one scalar, an isothermal gas and the solver of Roe, with the parameters of `viscous_diffusion` (2.11.1): $A = 10^{-6}$, $\nu = 0.25$ and $t_0 = 0.5$ to $t = 2$ on 256 and 512 cells over a length of 12 with outflow boundaries, explicitly at a Courant number of 0.8 (`tst/regression/scripts/tests/diffusion/scalar_diffusion.py`). It requires an $L_1$ error against the Gaussian of time $t_0 + t$ that falls faster than the resolution to the power $-1.99$, and `scalar_diffusion_sts` repeats it with super time-stepping (2.11.2).

The advection of scalars is tested by the problems of [Mignone](#mignone-2014) (2014) and the slotted cylinder (2.7.2, 2.8.7). `sr_hydro_scalars` runs the four shock tubes of special relativity of `inputs/hydro_sr/athinput.mb_1` to `inputs/hydro_sr/athinput.mb_4` (2.14) with HLLC and one scalar, 0 to the left of the interface and 1 to its right, and requires these values at the end outside a band of width 0.1 set for each problem (`tst/regression/scripts/tests/scalars/sr_hydro_scalars.py`; `src/pgen/gr_shock_tube.cpp`). `restart` builds the problem generator `shock_tube` with two scalars, runs the problem of Sod to $t = 0$ with a restart file as output, restarts from that file, and passes when both runs complete (`tst/regression/scripts/tests/scalars/restart.py`).

### 2.13 Particles

#### 2.13.1 Particles coupled to the gas by drag

[Bai and Stone](#bai-stone-2010) (2010) extend Athena by particles that exchange momentum with the gas through aerodynamic drag, to study gas and solids in protoplanetary discs and the formation of planetesimals (abstract). The copy of Athena++ has no module of particles (1.2), so that this subject describes the method of Athena alone. Its hybrid scheme is a predictor-corrector of second order whose feedback of momentum onto the gas conserves momentum exactly, and it remains stable and converges in most of the regimes that matter for discs. A semi-implicit integrator, generalised from the leapfrog, keeps the geometric properties of orbits without drag, and a fully implicit one is unconditionally stable for any strength of the coupling. Once the streaming instability saturates nonlinearly, the properties of the gas converge at modest resolution, while the clumping of particles converges only at higher resolution, and the measured transport of particles is uncertain by about 20 per cent (2.13.4).

In protoplanetary discs the gas, partly supported by the radial gradient of pressure, rotates below the Keplerian speed, while solids feel no pressure and tend to orbit at the Keplerian speed, so that they move relative to the gas and feel its drag (§1). Without other forces the velocity of a particle relative to the gas decays as $\exp(-t/t_{\mathrm{stop}})$, and the dimensionless stopping time $\tau_s = \Omega t_{\mathrm{stop}}$, with the Keplerian angular frequency $\Omega$, measures the coupling. For particles smaller than the mean free path of the gas, the regime of Epstein, $\tau_s = \rho_sa\Omega/(\rho_gc_s)$, with the material density $\rho_s$ and radius $a$ of a spherical particle and the density $\rho_g$ and sound speed $c_s$ of the gas, and $\tau_s = 1$ means bodies of about a metre at 1 AU in the minimum-mass solar nebula. Grains settled to the midplane can drive the Kelvin–Helmholtz and streaming instabilities, whose nonlinear evolution needs simulations of gas and solids together. They need many superparticles, each a swarm of real particles that share their properties, which a fluid of dust cannot replace for a distribution of properties or largely collisionless solids, and the feedback of momentum onto the gas, on which both instabilities depend. Earlier hybrid codes use finite differences ([Youdin and Johansen](#youdin-johansen-2007) 2007; [Johansen and Youdin](#johansen-youdin-2007) 2007) or Godunov methods similar to those of Athena ([Miniati](#miniati-2010) 2010; [Balsara et al.](#balsara-et-al-2009) 2009). The scheme of Athena differs from all four. Its integrators, one semi-implicit and one fully implicit, handle the stiff coupling of small grains with $\tau_s \ll 1$, and tests compare it with the four codes, using the growing modes of the streaming instability from [Youdin and Johansen](#youdin-johansen-2007) (2007) and the nonlinear runs of [Johansen and Youdin](#johansen-youdin-2007) (2007), two of which are repeated in a systematic study of convergence (2.13.3, 2.13.4).

The equations are written for the local shearing sheet ([Goldreich and Lynden-Bell](#goldreich-lynden-bell-1965) 1965), in Cartesian coordinates of a frame that rotates at $\Omega$ at a fiducial radius, with $\hat{\mathbf{x}}$, $\hat{\mathbf{y}}$ and $\hat{\mathbf{z}}$ radial, azimuthal and vertical; without the source terms of the sheet they serve other problems (§2.1). Particle $i$ obeys $d\mathbf{v}_i/dt = 2\mathbf{v}_i\times\mathbf{\Omega} + 2q\Omega^2x\hat{\mathbf{x}} - \Omega^2z\hat{\mathbf{z}} - (\mathbf{v}_i - \mathbf{u})/t_{\mathrm{stop}}$ (eq. 1), and the gas the equation of continuity and $\partial(\rho_g\mathbf{u})/\partial t + \nabla\cdot(\rho_g\mathbf{u}\mathbf{u} + P_g\mathbf{I}) = \rho_g[2\mathbf{u}\times\mathbf{\Omega} + 2q\Omega^2x\hat{\mathbf{x}} - \Omega^2z\hat{\mathbf{z}} + \epsilon(\bar{\mathbf{v}} - \mathbf{u})/t_{\mathrm{stop}}]$ (eqs. 2, 3). Here $q$, the shear parameter, is 3/2 in Keplerian flow; $\mathbf{u}$ and $\mathbf{v}$ are the velocities of gas and particles in the rotating frame, $\bar{\mathbf{v}}$ is the mean velocity of the particles in a fluid element, weighted by their masses, and $\epsilon = \rho_p/\rho_g$ is the local ratio of the densities of particles and gas. The code admits any number of species, each with its own stopping time, but the paper takes one species with a constant one. The last term of eq. 3 writes the feedback of the particles as for a fluid, and its form for particles, given below, conserves the total momentum. The paper neglects the vertical gravity, the heat dissipated by drag, as its gas is isothermal, $P_g = \rho_gc_s^2$, and the field, which the integrators of MHD handle ([Stone et al.](#stone-et-al-2008) 2008). **[GK]** The paper defines $q$ as $d\ln\Omega/d\ln r$, which is $-3/2$ for Keplerian flow; the minus sign of 2.9.2 is missing.

In the shearing box, whose radial boundaries are periodic but shifted by the shear ([Hawley, Gammie and Balbus](#hawley-et-al-1995) 1995; 2.9.3), a radial gradient of pressure is inconsistent with periodicity. It may be replaced by a constant outward force $2\eta v_K\Omega\hat{\mathbf{x}}$ on the gas, where $\eta v_K$ is the amount by which the gradient lowers the azimuthal speed of the gas below the Keplerian speed $v_K$. Athena instead applies a constant inward force to the particles, which leaves the integrator of the gas unchanged, is mathematically equivalent, and raises the azimuthal velocities of particles and gas by $\eta v_K$. Orbital advection ([Masset](#masset-2000) 2000; [Johnson, Guan and Gammie](#johnson-et-al-2008) 2008; [Stone and Gardiner](#stone-gardiner-2010) 2010; 2.9.4) applies to the particles as well. For the velocities relative to the shear, $\mathbf{v}'$ and $\mathbf{u}'$, eq. 1 becomes $d\mathbf{v}'_i/dt = 2(v'_{iy} - \eta v_K)\Omega\hat{\mathbf{x}} - (2 - q)v'_{ix}\Omega\hat{\mathbf{y}} - \Omega^2z\hat{\mathbf{z}} - (\mathbf{v}'_i - \mathbf{u}')/t_{\mathrm{stop}}$, whose first term includes the gradient of pressure, and a step of advection of the particles accompanies the orbital advection of the gas (eq. 4). The simulations of the paper, in the radial-vertical plane, do without orbital advection, which serves those in three dimensions. **[GK]** By eq. 1 the uniform orbital motion is $-q\Omega x\hat{\mathbf{y}}$, as the shift of positions in 2.13.2 assumes, so that eq. 4 holds for $\mathbf{v}' = \mathbf{v} + q\Omega x\hat{\mathbf{y}}$ and $\mathbf{u}' = \mathbf{u} + q\Omega x\hat{\mathbf{y}}$, whereas the paper writes $\mathbf{v}' = \mathbf{v} - (q\Omega x)\hat{\mathbf{y}}$ and calls $(q\Omega x)\hat{\mathbf{y}}$ the background flow.

Of the integrators CTU and VL2 (2.6), the particles join CTU, the more accurate and less diffusive of the two by [Stone and Gardiner](#stone-gardiner-2009) (2009) (§2.2). The feedback of momentum, and of energy where it applies, enters the gas as source terms, unlike in [Miniati](#miniati-2010) (2010), while the transverse gradients of the fluxes in CTU and the equation of continuity stay unchanged. With $\mathbf{f}$ and $\mathbf{g}$ the accelerations of particles and gas by forces other than drag, such as the Coriolis and tidal forces and the global gradient of pressure, the equations read $d\mathbf{v}_i/dt = \mathbf{f}_i - (\mathbf{v}_i - \mathbf{u})/t_{\mathrm{stop}}$ and $d(\rho_g\mathbf{u})/dt = \rho_g\mathbf{g} + \rho_p(\bar{\mathbf{v}} - \mathbf{u})/t_{\mathrm{stop}}$, the latter with a Lagrangian derivative as the fluxes are unchanged (eqs. 5, 6). A step of length $h$ from $t^{(n)}$ to $t^{(n+1)}$ consists of a predictor, $(\rho_g\mathbf{u})^{(n+1/2)} = (\rho_g\mathbf{u})^{(n)} + (h/2)(\rho_g\mathbf{g})^{(n)} + \sum_iW(\mathbf{x} - \mathbf{x}_i^{(n)})\Delta\mathbf{p}_i^{\mathrm{pred}}$, the particle step $\mathbf{v}_i^{(n+1)} = \mathbf{v}_i^{(n)} + h[\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{v}_i, \mathbf{u}^{(n+1/2)}(\mathbf{x}_i)) - (\mathbf{v}_i - \mathbf{u}^{(n+1/2)}(\mathbf{x}_i))/t_{\mathrm{stop}}]$, in whose bracket $\mathbf{x}_i$ and $\mathbf{v}_i$ may combine values of both steps (2.13.2), and a corrector, $(\rho_g\mathbf{u})^{(n+1)} = (\rho_g\mathbf{u})^{(n)} + h(\rho_g\mathbf{g})^{(n+1/2)} + \sum_iW(\mathbf{x} - \mathbf{x}_i^{(n+1/2)})\Delta\mathbf{p}_i^{\mathrm{corr}}$ (eqs. 7a to 7c).

The weight function $W$ interpolates the gas to a particle for the drag and, for consistency, distributes the feedback of the particle to the grid. It should be continuous, non-negative to reduce noise ([Youdin and Johansen](#youdin-johansen-2007) 2007), not much less accurate than the reconstruction of the gas, PPM of third order here, and cheap. Of the interpolations by cloud in cell, by triangular-shaped cloud and by a quadratic polynomial, the first is simple, though noisy and inaccurate, and the last the most accurate but discontinuous, so that the triangular-shaped cloud is used, as by [Johansen and Youdin](#johansen-youdin-2007) (2007).

The predictor evaluates the drag of each particle directly over half a step. As the drag diverges for small stopping times and amplifies the errors of velocity and interpolation, each particle contributes $\Delta\mathbf{p}_i^{\mathrm{pred}} = m_i(\mathbf{v}_i - \mathbf{u})h/[2\max(t_{\mathrm{stop}}, h)]$, with $m_i$ its mass (eq. 8). For $t_{\mathrm{stop}} \le h$ this is of first order, like the exponential decay, which assumes drag alone, and for $t_{\mathrm{stop}} \ge h$ it balances the forces of an equilibrium exactly, such as the drifting equilibrium from which the linear tests start (2.13.3). Divided by the density of the gas, the feedback corrects the velocity of both interface states between the first and second steps of CTU (2.6.3). The corrector takes the change of momentum of each particle over the full step, less the impulse of the other forces, $\Delta\mathbf{p}_i^{\mathrm{corr}} = \mathbf{p}_i^{(n+1)} - \mathbf{p}_i^{(n)} - \mathbf{F}_{c,i}^{(n+1/2)}h$, with these forces, which are not stiff, evaluated at the midpoint $\mathbf{x}^{(n+1/2)}$ of the old and new positions for second order (eq. 9). This conserves momentum exactly and avoids evaluating the stiff drag, and the feedback, distributed from $\mathbf{x}^{(n+1/2)}$, is added at the end of the integrator of the gas. **[GK]** As $\Delta\mathbf{p}_i^{\mathrm{corr}}$ is the impulse of drag on the particle, the gas must receive $-\Delta\mathbf{p}_i^{\mathrm{corr}}$ for momentum to be conserved, the sign that the feedback of eq. 8 carries, whereas eq. 7c adds $\Delta\mathbf{p}_i^{\mathrm{corr}}$.

With thermodynamics, which the paper does not treat, the heat of friction, $\dot{\mathcal{E}} = \rho_p(\bar{\mathbf{v}} - \mathbf{u})^2/t_{\mathrm{stop}}$ per volume, would enter the energy of the gas (eq. 10). It comes from the work of the gradient of pressure and is small against the thermal energy of a disc. To avoid the stiffness of $1/t_{\mathrm{stop}}$, particle $i$ deposits $(\Delta p_i)^2t_{\mathrm{stop}}/(m_ih^2)$, with $\Delta p_i$ of the predictor or corrector from eq. 8 or 9. The scheme is of second order, below the finite differences of higher order of the Pencil Code in smooth flows, but fully conservative, without artificial hyperviscosity, and easier to combine with implicit integrators of particles. The similar predictor-corrector of [Balsara et al.](#balsara-et-al-2009) (2009, eq. 16) approximates the corrector and loses accuracy where dust dominates the density, and the fully implicit scheme of [Miniati](#miniati-2010) (2010) assumes one species, whereas that of Athena is simpler and admits any number.

Being explicit, the predictor-corrector can lose stability when the mass loading $\epsilon$ makes the equations stiff: the integrators couple the particles strictly to the gas for $t_{\mathrm{stop}} \lesssim h$, but for $\epsilon \gg 1$ the gas should follow the particles, and both velocities can grow without physical cause. With the stiffness parameter $\chi = \sum_k\epsilon_kh/\max(t_{\mathrm{stop},k}, h)$ over the species $k$ (eq. 11), the scheme may turn unstable once $\chi$ exceeds about 3 to 5. An implicit hybrid scheme would cure this for a single size, much like two fluids ([Miniati](#miniati-2010) 2010), but $N$ sizes, coupled to each other through the gas, need a non-diagonal matrix of rank $N + 1$ to be inverted in every cell. Instead, the stopping times of all particles in a cell may be raised where $\chi$ exceeds a threshold, as in the Pencil Code, so that particles in dense clumps move more freely while the gas still follows them. Athena can enforce $\chi < 3$ in this way, but the feature is switched off in practice. Reducing the feedback of the predictor by $\max(\chi, 1)$, but not that of the corrector, which would break conservation, also gains stability, at the price of first order. In discs the dust holds about one per cent of the mass, so that $\chi$ is much less than one on average. Even weak turbulence, with $\alpha = 10^{-5}$, keeps the mean of $\chi$ in the midplane well below one. Clumping by the streaming instability, most efficient for $\tau_s \sim 1$, raises $\chi$ only slowly, and $\chi$ stays below 3 in the runs of 2.13.4. It limits stability, not the density of particles, and stratified simulations with five to seven times the solar abundance of solids did become unstable and needed shorter steps.

#### 2.13.2 The integrators of particles

The integrator of particles solves $d\mathbf{x}/dt = \mathbf{v}$ and $d\mathbf{v}/dt = \mathbf{a}[\mathbf{v}, \mathbf{x}, \mathbf{u}^{(n+1/2)}(\mathbf{x})]$, with all forces including drag in $\mathbf{a}$ and the gas at the half step, which spares following the gas and keeps second order ([Bai and Stone](#bai-stone-2010) 2010, §3, eq. 12). Two implicit integrators of second order meet the stiffness of drag on strongly coupled particles, and different species may use different ones, so that any stopping time can be integrated while orbits keep their geometric properties.

The semi-implicit integrator follows Crank and Nicolson (§3.1). It drifts to $\mathbf{x}' = \mathbf{x}^{(n)} + h\mathbf{v}^{(n)}/2$, kicks with $\mathbf{v}^{(n+1)} = \mathbf{v}^{(n)} + h\mathbf{a}[(\mathbf{v}^{(n)} + \mathbf{v}^{(n+1)})/2, \mathbf{x}', \mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x}')]$ and drifts to $\mathbf{x}^{(n+1)} = \mathbf{x}' + h\mathbf{v}^{(n+1)}/2$, taking the stopping time and the velocity of the gas at $\mathbf{x}'$ (eq. 13). In explicit form $\mathbf{v}^{(n+1)} - \mathbf{v}^{(n)} = h\Lambda^{-1}\mathbf{a}(\mathbf{v}^{(n)}, \mathbf{x}', \mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x}'))$ with $\Lambda = 1 - (h/2)\,\partial\mathbf{a}/\partial\mathbf{v}$ (eq. 14). The Jacobian at $\mathbf{x}'$ has $-1/t_{\mathrm{stop}}$ on its diagonal, $2\Omega$ as its element $xy$ and $-(2 - q)\Omega$ as its element $yx$, the factor $2 - q$ coming from orbital advection, which is always used (eq. 15). After the step the positions shift by $-q\Omega xh\hat{\mathbf{y}}$, with $x$ the mean of the old and new radial positions; this advection improves the accuracy and keeps the geometry of orbits. The integrator resembles the Boris integrator, as the Coriolis force resembles the Lorentz force, and is a leapfrog of the form drift-kick-drift that keeps the geometric properties of orbits exactly for $t_{\mathrm{stop}} = \infty$ (2.13.3). The symplectic integrator of [Quinn et al.](#quinn-et-al-2010) (2010) for Hill's equations, of the form kick-drift-kick, lacks the advection of particles and so has slightly more complicated formulae. For $t_{\mathrm{stop}} \ll h$ the position tends to $\mathbf{x}^{(n)} + h\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x}')$, still of second order, but the velocity to $\mathbf{v}^{(n)} + \mathbf{v}^{(n+1)} = 2\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x}')$, so that a large initial difference of the velocities of particle and gas oscillates without damping, and the evolving gas can even amplify it into a runaway. Experience shows the integrator to be safe for $t_{\mathrm{stop}} \gtrsim 0.2h$. The only other limit that a semi-implicit scheme of this type can reach, $\mathbf{v}^{(n)} + \mathbf{v}^{(n+1)} = \mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x}^{(n)}) + \mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x}^{(n+1)})$, shares the weakness and loses the geometry of orbits.

An absolutely stable integrator must be fully implicit, the new velocity depending only on itself (§3.2). For $dy/dt = f(y)$ one such scheme of second order is $y^{(n+1)} = y^{(n)} + hf(y^{(n+1)})/2 + hf[y^{(n+1)} - hf(y^{(n+1)})]/2$ (eq. 16). For the particles it reads $\mathbf{x}' = \mathbf{x}^{(n)} + h\mathbf{v}^{(n)}$, $\mathbf{v}^{(n+1)} = \mathbf{v}^{(n)} + (h/2)\mathbf{a}(\mathbf{v}^{(n+1)}, \mathbf{x}') + (h/2)\mathbf{a}(\mathbf{v}^{(n+1)} - h\mathbf{a}[\mathbf{v}^{(n+1)}, \mathbf{x}'], \mathbf{x}^{(n)})$ and $\mathbf{x}^{(n+1)} = \mathbf{x}^{(n)} + (h/2)(\mathbf{v}^{(n)} + \mathbf{v}^{(n+1)})$, with the gas taken where $\mathbf{a}$ is evaluated (eq. 17). For $t_{\mathrm{stop}} = 0$ it demands $\mathbf{v}^{(n+1)} = \mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x}')$, which is not of second order, but the position is. To second order $\mathbf{v}^{(n+1)} - \mathbf{v}^{(n)} = (h/2)\Lambda^{-1}[\mathbf{a}(\mathbf{v}^{(n)}, \mathbf{x}^{(n)}) + (1 - hJ_0)\,\mathbf{a}(\mathbf{v}^{(n)}, \mathbf{x}')]$ with $\Lambda = 1 - (h/2)(J_1 + J_0 - hJ_0J_1)$, where $J_0$ and $J_1$ are the Jacobians at $\mathbf{x}^{(n)}$ and $\mathbf{x}'$, which differ where the stopping time depends on position (eq. 18). Only a matrix of $2 \times 2$ needs inverting. To first order in $h$, but to all orders in the terms with $1/t_{\mathrm{stop}}$, $\Lambda^{-1}$ is $(1 + b)^{-2}$ times the matrix with $1 + b$ on its diagonal, $2h\Omega$ as its element $xy$ and $-(2 - q)h\Omega$ as its element $yx$, where $b = \tfrac{1}{2}[h/t_{\mathrm{stop}0} + h/t_{\mathrm{stop}1} + h^2/(t_{\mathrm{stop}0}t_{\mathrm{stop}1})]$ (eq. 19). As $t_{\mathrm{stop}} \to 0$, $\Lambda^{-1}$ tends to $b^{-1} \sim 2t_{\mathrm{stop}}^2/h^2$ and so to zero, which makes the integrator stable for any $t_{\mathrm{stop}} > 0$. Like the explicit method and any fully implicit one, it fails to keep the geometry of orbits as $t_{\mathrm{stop}} \to \infty$, so that the semi-implicit integrator serves most particles and the fully implicit one those with $t_{\mathrm{stop}} \lesssim h$.

The approximation of short friction times of [Johansen and Klahr](#johansen-klahr-2005) (2005) is an alternative for strongly coupled particles. They move with the gas at the terminal velocity $\mathbf{v}_{\mathrm{term}}(\mathbf{x}) \approx \mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x}) + t_{\mathrm{stop}}(\mathbf{F}_p - \mathbf{F}_g)$, set by the external forces on particles and gas, which differ as particles have no support by pressure and in general feel no field (eq. 20). The leading truncation error is of the order of $\max[(t_{\mathrm{stop}}|\nabla\mathbf{u}|)^2, t_{\mathrm{stop}}^2|\nabla\mathbf{F}|, t_{\mathrm{stop}}^2\Omega|\nabla\mathbf{u}|]$ with $\mathbf{F} = \mathbf{F}_p - \mathbf{F}_g$, so that the approximation holds for $t_{\mathrm{stop}} \ll \min(\Omega^{-1}, |\nabla\mathbf{u}|^{-1})$, and an integrator of second order adds errors of the order of $(\Omega h)^2$. It could therefore match the fully implicit integrator only for $t_{\mathrm{stop}} < h$. In its original form it lacks the feedback, needed for the streaming instability, though the feedback of 2.13.1 could be added, and Athena does not implement it.

#### 2.13.3 Tests of the particle method

Without drag, $t_{\mathrm{stop}} = \infty$, eq. 1 gives an epicycle about the centre of the domain, $x = A\cos\omega t$ and $y = -(2\Omega/\omega)A\sin\omega t$ with $\omega = [2(2 - q)]^{1/2}\Omega$, which conserves the energy $E = \tfrac{1}{2}(\dot{x}^2 + \dot{y}^2) - q\Omega^2x^2 = (2 - q)\Omega^2A^2$ ([Bai and Stone](#bai-stone-2010) 2010, §4.1, eqs. 21, 22). A test particle with $A = 0.4$, for $\Omega = 1$ and $q = 3/2$ and with a step $h = 0.4\Omega^{-1}$ large enough to make the errors visible, is followed by the two integrators of 2.13.2 and, for comparison, by an explicit one of second order after the modified Euler method (Figure 1). The semi-implicit integrator conserves the energy exactly, though only with orbital advection: without it, as for the leapfrog, the numerical Hamiltonian depends on time, and the energy oscillates about its true value or, with varying steps, may drift. The orbit closes, its error is a shift of phase that falls as $h^2$, the explicit integrator gains energy and moves off its guiding centre, and the fully implicit one loses energy and spirals into it, a quite general result. As the semi-implicit integrator keeps orbits geometric and evaluates the drag, with its interpolation, only once per step, it is preferred in most applications.

Particles spread uniformly in a uniform gas, with drag and feedback, travel in the frame of zero total momentum the distance $S(t) = [w_0t_{\mathrm{stop}}/(1 + \epsilon)][1 - e^{-(1 + \epsilon)t/t_{\mathrm{stop}}}]$, with $w_0$ their initial velocity and $\epsilon$ the overall ratio of the masses of particles and gas (§4.2, eq. 23). For $\epsilon = 1$, $t_{\mathrm{stop}} = 2$, $w_0 = 1$ and $\rho_g = 1$, run with fixed steps to $t_e = 1$, the error of position falls as $h^2$ for both integrators, which shows that the hybrid scheme is of second order (Figure 2). The total momentum stays at the level of round-off, $10^{-16}$, and grows as smaller steps accumulate more rounding errors, slightly faster than the $h^{-1/2}$ of uncorrelated errors. In the stiff regime, at $h = 1$ with $t_{\mathrm{stop}} = 0.2$ or 0.02 to $t_e = 10$, the fully implicit integrator damps the velocity of the particles, and through conservation that of the gas, by a factor of about $2(t_{\mathrm{stop}}/h)^2$ per step (Figure 3). This is slower than the unresolved physical rate, but still several orders of magnitude per step. The semi-implicit integrator lets the velocity oscillate with a factor of about $-(1 - 4t_{\mathrm{stop}}/h)$ per step, damping the more slowly the shorter the stopping time and never for $t_{\mathrm{stop}} = 0$. It then acts as a damped harmonic oscillator, which a gas oscillating at a similar frequency could amplify by numerical resonance, so that particles with $t_{\mathrm{stop}} \lesssim h$ use the fully implicit integrator.

The most stringent test is the growth of the unstable modes that [Youdin and Johansen](#youdin-johansen-2007) (2007, eq. 10, Table 1) give for the streaming instability, built on the equilibrium of [Nakagawa, Sekiya and Hayashi](#nakagawa-et-al-1986) (1986) between solids and gas in an unstratified Keplerian disc, where the gas is partly supported by a radial gradient of pressure (§4.3). With the advection of particles and the careful feedback of predictor and corrector, the code holds this equilibrium exactly, the net forces on gas and particles vanishing to machine precision. The modes have the dimensionless wavenumbers $K_x = k_x\eta v_K/\Omega$ and $K_z = k_z\eta v_K/\Omega$, and with $\eta v_K = 0.05c_s$ and a box of $L_x = L_z = 2$, one wavelength, they set the sound speed. One particle per cell starts at the centre of its cell, and radial shifts in proportion to $\cos k_zz$ give the density of particles $1 + A\cos k_xx\cos k_zz$ with $A = 10^{-6}$. Both tests have $\tau_s = 0.1$. The mode linA, with $\epsilon = 3$ and $K_x = K_z = 30$, grows at $s = 0.4190204\Omega$, and linB, with $\epsilon = 0.2$ and $K_x = K_z = 6$, only at $s = 0.015476\Omega$, which makes it harder, though its smaller sound speed allows larger steps. On 16 to 128 cells per wavelength, with a Courant number of 0.8 or with the fixed step that this number gives on 128 cells, linA approaches the predicted rate closely, more so with smaller steps, and linB more slowly, not growing at all on 32 cells at a Courant number of 0.8 (Figure 4). Both integrators give almost the same results, as the steps, $\Omega h = 2.6 \times 10^{-4}$ for linA and $1.3 \times 10^{-3}$ for linB on the coarsest grid, lie far below the stopping time. At a Courant number of 0.8, linB needs about 64 cells to grow, while 16 suffice for the fast mode of linA. The rates resemble the results of [Miniati](#miniati-2010) (2010) and [Balsara et al.](#balsara-et-al-2009) (2009), whose codes of MHD are similar but whose hybrid schemes differ, and lie even closer to the semi-analytic values, but they are less accurate than those of [Youdin and Johansen](#youdin-johansen-2007) (2007), whose Pencil Code is of sixth order in space and third order in time for smooth flows such as these.

As later simulations reach $\tau_s = 10^{-3}$, two more modes test smaller stopping times (App. A). The mode linC, with $\tau_s = 0.01$, $\epsilon = 2$ and $K_x = K_z = 1500$, grows at $s = 0.5981\Omega$, and linD, with $\tau_s = 10^{-3}$, $\epsilon = 2$ and $K_x = K_z = 2000$, at $s = 0.3154\Omega$, both near the fastest growth and costly for their large $K$. Their eigenvectors, provided by Andrew Youdin, follow the form of Table 1 of [Youdin and Johansen](#youdin-johansen-2007) (2007) (Table 2). At a Courant number of 0.4 the growth rates converge for all quantities except the gas, whose perturbations are too small to measure reliably, but need about 128 cells per wavelength (Figure 7).

#### 2.13.4 Convergence of the streaming instability

As an application, simulations in two dimensions, in the radial-vertical plane, test the convergence of the streaming instability once saturated, which [Johansen and Youdin](#johansen-youdin-2007) (2007) analysed without a systematic study of convergence (§5). Two dimensions allow a wider range of resolutions than three, and the two give similar results. With an isothermal gas and no vertical gravity, the instability depends mainly on $\tau_s$ and the overall ratio $\epsilon$ (§5.1). The drift $\eta v_K$ only sets the length $\eta r = \eta v_K/\Omega$ that measures all lengths, and the sound speed matters little, the gas moving almost incompressibly; the paper takes $\eta v_K/c_s = 0.05$, about the value at 1 AU in the minimum-mass solar nebula. Marginally coupled or larger particles, $\tau_s \gtrsim 1$, become unstable over a wide range of $\epsilon$ around one and saturate in long stripes, nearly vertical and slightly tilted radially. Tightly coupled ones, $\tau_s \ll 1$, become strongly unstable only for $\epsilon \gtrsim 1$, and saturate in large voids with narrow streams and much weaker turbulence. Over a wide range of parameters the code reproduces all results of [Johansen and Youdin](#johansen-youdin-2007) (2007). For more strongly coupled particles finer grids are needed as $\tau_s$ decreases, and clumping weakens to modest overdensities for $\tau_s \le 0.01$ and $\epsilon \ge 1$, in qualitative agreement with linear analysis.

The two problems repeat the runs AB, with $\tau_s = 0.1$, $\epsilon = 1$ and $L_x = L_z = 2\eta r$, and BA, with $\tau_s = 1$, $\epsilon = 0.2$ and $L_x = L_z = 40\eta r$, of [Johansen and Youdin](#johansen-youdin-2007) (2007), with the semi-implicit integrator, as the steps are at least ten times shorter than $t_{\mathrm{stop}}$. The standard grid has $256^2$ cells with $N_{pc} = 9$ particles per cell, and the step, $h \sim \mathrm{CFL}\,(L_x/N)/c_s$ for $N$ cells along a side, is $\Omega h \approx 3 \times 10^{-4}$ for AB and $6 \times 10^{-3}$ for BA at a Courant number of 0.8. One series varies the grid from $64^2$ to $2048^2$ cells at nine particles per cell, refining the step with it, and another varies $N_{pc}$ from 1 to 25 on $256^2$ cells, with the particles at random positions and the velocities of the equilibrium (Table 1). The parameters of the runs AB9 and BA9 are exactly those of [Johansen and Youdin](#johansen-youdin-2007) (2007). The AB runs saturate after about $16\Omega^{-1}$ and the BA runs after about $160\Omega^{-1}$, and averages run from $T_s = 30\Omega^{-1}$ to $T_e = 80\Omega^{-1}$ for AB and from $300\Omega^{-1}$ to $1500\Omega^{-1}$ for BA. At saturation the patterns of BA hardly change with the grid, which only adds detail, while in AB all grids show cavities and streams but the clumps shrink as the grid is refined, and $256^2$ cells are needed to capture the main features (Figure 5).

Concentration into clumps matters most among the properties of the instability, as clumps up to $10^3$ times the mean density of particles can become gravitationally bound and form planetesimals (§5.2). It is measured by the cumulative distribution of the density of particles around each particle, normalised by the mean (Figure 6). The stiffness parameter of eq. 11 stays small: its maximum falls from 0.2 on $64^2$ cells to 0.004 on $2048^2$ cells in AB, and stays below 0.5 in BA except for transients up to 2.0 on the finest grid, too brief to affect the distribution. The distributions agree quantitatively with those of [Johansen and Youdin](#johansen-youdin-2007) (2007, Figure 11). Those of AB do not change with longer averaging or other random seeds, whereas those of BA, dominated by a few dense clumps whose slow stochastic evolution makes them depend on the period of averaging, are averaged over nearly 200 orbits, about 170 correlation times of the clumps. Finer grids give stronger clumping, with higher maximum densities and more particles in dense clumps; AB converges on $512^2$ cells and BA apparently on $1024^2$ cells. The distributions depend only weakly on the number of particles, and the scatter of BA shrinks with longer averaging, so that one particle per cell suffices to capture the distribution of densities of the solids.

The turbulence that the instability drives may affect the settling and radial transport of solids (§5.3). After [Johansen and Youdin](#johansen-youdin-2007) (2007, §5.3), the runs measure the Mach numbers of the fluctuations of the gas along the three directions, the mean radial drift of the particles relative to that of the equilibrium, the diffusion coefficients of the particles, and the Reynolds stress $\rho_gu_x(u_y - v_K)$ relative to that of the equilibrium (Table 1). The coefficients follow from fits of $\sigma_{x,z}^2(\Delta t) = 2D_{x,z}\Delta t$ to the spread of tracer particles over intervals up to $32\Omega^{-1}$ for AB and $320\Omega^{-1}$ for BA, and the uncertainties of one standard deviation from the time series or the regression. The properties of the gas and the radial drift depend only weakly on the grid and the number of particles. In AB they agree from $256^2$ cells on, the stripes on $1024^2$ cells being smaller but the statistics of the gas indistinguishable, and in BA even $64^2$ cells agree with finer grids, unlike the concentration. The diffusion coefficients vary more, with the grid and with the number of particles, at times beyond their uncertainties and without a systematic trend, except a decrease with resolution in AB. Their spread on grids of at least $256^2$ cells, about 20 per cent for both series, is therefore taken as the uncertainty of such simulations.

The scheme couples particles of a distribution of sizes to the gas by drag and could be extended to their gravity (§6). In discs the drag matters for solids from millimetres to tens or hundreds of metres, the range relevant to the formation of planetesimals. The predictor-corrector differs from those of other codes, is simple and robust, and conserves momentum exactly. Thanks to its two implicit integrators, it works where the coupling is not stiff and where it is stiff without strong mass loading, that is with $\chi$ of order one at most. In the tests, among them the linear growth and the nonlinear saturation of the streaming instability, it performs as well as the Pencil Code of higher order. Convergence in the nonlinear regime differs from and is more complicated than in the linear one. The resolution needed depends strongly on $\tau_s$, about 128 cells per $\eta r$ for $\tau_s = 0.1$, and as many particles as cells suffice. The gas converges at modest resolution, insensitive to the number of particles, the concentration of particles only at very high resolution, growing stronger with it, and the diffusion of particles depends subtly on the setup, uncertain by about 20 per cent. These criteria guide the choice of grid and number of particles for later work, may carry over to stratified discs, and suggest one particle per cell for each species of a distribution. [Rein, Lesur and Leinhardt](#rein-et-al-2010) (2010) argue that superparticles converge only if the scaled system keeps the timescales of the real one. Without self-gravity or collisions, the only timescales here, the stopping time and the orbital period, combine into $\tau_s$, and convergence with drag supports their conclusions.

### 2.14 Relativistic MHD

#### 2.14.1 The equations of relativistic MHD

[Beckwith and Stone](#beckwith-stone-2011) (2011) describe a Godunov method for relativistic MHD in Athena that joins a simple unsplit integrator of second order to CT, with several approximate Riemann solvers for the fluxes (abstract). A broad suite of multidimensional tests led to a hierarchy of corrections for the unphysical states that errors of the fluxes, or of converting conserved into primitive variables, can produce; used exceedingly rarely, they improve the stability greatly. In preliminary applications to supersonic magnetised jets and to turbulence from the relativistic Kelvin–Helmholtz instability, solvers that include contact and rotational discontinuities raise the field in the cocoon of a jet tenfold and double the spectral resolution of the turbulence, gains that far outweigh their cost. The scheme is public as part of Athena.

Relativistic magnetised flows matter near black holes, in gamma-ray bursts and in blazar jets, and their nonlinear, multidimensional nature calls for numerical methods (§1). Godunov methods, extended to relativistic MHD over the preceding decade, capture shocks without artificial viscosity and, in conservative form, keep the coupling of energy and momentum. Two ingredients set the method apart. CT with fields on faces and the electric fields of [Gardiner and Stone](#gardiner-stone-2005) (2005, [2008](#gardiner-stone-2008)) keeps the divergence zero to machine precision, which suits the tight coupling of the variables better than divergence cleaning. The unsplit VL+CT of [Stone and Gardiner](#stone-gardiner-2009) (2009; 2.6.5) needs neither the characteristic decomposition in primitive variables, very complex in relativity, nor the multidimensional source terms of CTU+CT, and proved more robust in supersonic turbulence at a small cost in diffusion ([Lemaster and Stone](#lemaster-stone-2009) 2009). The inversion uses the method 1DW of [Noble et al.](#noble-et-al-2006) (2006) as implemented by [Mignone and McKinney](#mignone-mckinney-2007) (2007). Relativistic schemes are less robust than Newtonian ones and more complex, owing to the nonlinear coupling of conserved and primitive variables and to unphysical fluxes or superluminal velocities from approximate solvers, hence the corrections, needed in fewer than one in $10^9$ updates. Tests in several dimensions, which probe the coupling of the components of four-vectors and the solenoidal constraint, prove far more exacting than those in one, and the code inherits the static mesh refinement of Athena.

The equations express the conservation of particle number, $\nabla_\mu(\rho U^\mu) = 0$, and of energy and momentum, $\nabla_\mu T^\mu_\nu = 0$, together with Maxwell's equations and the conservation of charge (§2.1, eqs. 1 to 4). Here $\rho$ is the comoving density, $U^\mu$ the four-velocity, with $U^\mu U_\mu = -1$, and $T^\mu_\nu$ the stress-energy tensor; units have $c = 1$, the fields are in Lorentz–Heaviside form, and the metric is flat and Cartesian, $g_{\alpha\beta} = \mathrm{diag}(-1, 1, 1, 1)$ (eq. 6). In ideal MHD, where the Lorentz force vanishes in the comoving frame, Maxwell's equations reduce to $\nabla\cdot\mathbf{B} = 0$, $\partial_t\mathbf{B} + \nabla\times\mathbf{E} = 0$ and $\mathbf{E} = -\mathbf{V}\times\mathbf{B}$, as in Newtonian MHD, with the transport velocity $V^i = U^i/\Gamma$ and the Lorentz factor $\Gamma = (1 - |\mathbf{V}|^2)^{-1/2}$ (eqs. 8 to 10). The fluid contributes $\rho hu^\mu u_\nu + P_g\delta^\mu_\nu$ to the stress-energy tensor, with the enthalpy $h = 1 + [\gamma/(\gamma - 1)]P_g/\rho$ of an ideal gas of constant $\gamma$ (eqs. 11, 13). With the magnetic four-vector $b^\mu = \Gamma[\mathbf{V}\cdot\mathbf{B}, B^i/\Gamma^2 + V^i(\mathbf{V}\cdot\mathbf{B})]$, of square $|b|^2 = |\mathbf{B}|^2/\Gamma^2 + (\mathbf{V}\cdot\mathbf{B})^2$, the field contributes $|b|^2u^\mu u_\nu + \tfrac{1}{2}|b|^2\delta^\mu_\nu - b^\mu b_\nu$ (eqs. 14 to 16). In flat space the covariant divergences become partial derivatives (eq. 7), which gives the conservative form of eqs. 17 and 18. **[GK]** Eq. 18 writes $\partial_t(\rho U^i)$ for the flux term of the continuity equation, which is $\partial_i(\rho U^i)$ by eq. 7, and eqs. 3 and 12 keep the factors $4\pi$ of Gaussian units, which the Lorentz–Heaviside units of §2.1 and eq. 16 drop.

The conserved variables are the lab-frame density $D = \rho\Gamma$, the momentum $\mathbf{M} = (\rho h\Gamma^2 + |\mathbf{B}|^2)\mathbf{V} - (\mathbf{V}\cdot\mathbf{B})\mathbf{B}$, the total energy $E = \rho h\Gamma^2 - P_g + \tfrac{1}{2}|\mathbf{B}|^2 + \tfrac{1}{2}|\mathbf{V}|^2|\mathbf{B}|^2 - \tfrac{1}{2}(\mathbf{V}\cdot\mathbf{B})^2$ and the field, and the primitive ones $\rho$, $\mathbf{V}$, $P_g$ and $\mathbf{B}$, which follow from the conserved ones only numerically (eqs. 19, 20; 2.14.2). The flux along $x$ has the components $DV^x$, $M_xV^x - \Gamma^{-1}B^xb_x + P_g + \tfrac{1}{2}|b|^2$, $M_yV^x - \Gamma^{-1}B^xb_y$, $M_zV^x - \Gamma^{-1}B^xb_z$, $M_x$, 0, $B^yV^x - B^xV^y$ and $B^zV^x - B^xV^z$, and the fluxes along $y$ and $z$ follow by cyclic permutation (eqs. 21, 22). The grid is uniform and Cartesian, and the step lets light cross a fraction $C$ of a cell, $\Delta t = C\min(\delta x, \delta y, \delta z)$, with $C \le 0.5$ for VL+CT (§2.2, eq. 23). As in 2.1.3 and 2.3.2, $D$, $\mathbf{M}$ and $E$ are averages over cells updated by fluxes averaged over faces and the step, the field consists of averages over faces updated by CT from electric fields averaged along edges, and the field at the centre of a cell, the mean of its two face values, is secondary (eqs. 24 to 30). **[GK]** Eq. 23 prints the minimum of the inverse widths, $\min(\delta x^{-1}, \delta y^{-1}, \delta z^{-1})$, where the crossing of a fraction $C$ of a cell by light requires the minimum of the widths.

[Stone et al.](#stone-et-al-2020) (2020, §4) summarise the methods of [White, Stone and Gammie](#white-et-al-2016) (2016) for special and general relativity in Athena++, with $c = 1$ and a notation that swaps that of [Beckwith and Stone](#beckwith-stone-2011) (2011): $\gamma$ is the Lorentz factor and $\Gamma$ the constant adiabatic index. In special relativity the primitive variables are the fluid-frame density $\rho$ and gas pressure $p_g$, the spatial part $\mathbf{u}$ of the four-velocity in the lab frame and the lab-frame field $\mathbf{B}$, with $\gamma = (1 + u^2)^{1/2}$ and $\mathbf{v} = \mathbf{u}/\gamma$ (§4.1). With the magnetic pressure $p_m = \tfrac{1}{2}[B^2/\gamma^2 + (\mathbf{v}\cdot\mathbf{B})^2]$ and the total enthalpy $w = \rho + [\Gamma/(\Gamma - 1)]p_g + 2p_m$, the equations read $\partial_tD + \nabla\cdot(D\mathbf{v}) = 0$, $\partial_t\mathbf{M} + \nabla\cdot\mathsf{S} = 0$, $\partial_tE + \nabla\cdot\mathbf{M} = 0$ and $\partial_t\mathbf{B} - \nabla\times(\mathbf{v}\times\mathbf{B}) = 0$ (eqs. 31 to 33). Here $D = \gamma\rho$, $E = \gamma^2w - \gamma^2(\mathbf{v}\cdot\mathbf{B})^2 - (p_g + p_m)$, $\mathbf{M} = (E + p_g + p_m)\mathbf{v} - (\mathbf{v}\cdot\mathbf{B})\mathbf{B}$ and $\mathsf{S} = \gamma^2w\mathbf{v}\mathbf{v} - \mathbf{B}\mathbf{B}/\gamma^2 - (\mathbf{v}\cdot\mathbf{B})(\mathbf{v}\mathbf{B} + \mathbf{B}\mathbf{v}) - \gamma^2(\mathbf{v}\cdot\mathbf{B})^2\mathbf{v}\mathbf{v} + (p_g + p_m)\mathsf{I}$ (eqs. 34, 35). As the equations keep the Newtonian form, the same discretisation applies.

In general relativity the primitive variables are $\rho$, $p_g$, the spatial velocities $u^{i'}$ in the normal frame, whose time direction is orthogonal to the surfaces of constant time, and the coordinate-frame field $B^i$. In coordinates $u^0 = \gamma/\alpha$ and $u^i = u^{i'} - \beta^i\gamma/\alpha$, with the lapse $\alpha = (-g^{00})^{-1/2}$, the shift $\beta^i = \alpha^2g^{0i}$ and the normal-frame Lorentz factor $\gamma = (1 + g_{ij}u^{i'}u^{j'})^{1/2}$ (eq. 36). The magnetic four-vector $b^\mu = u_\nu(^*F)^{\nu\mu}$ has $b^0 = u_iB^i$ and $b^i = (B^i + b^0u^i)/u^0$, which gives $p_m = \tfrac{1}{2}b_\mu b^\mu$ and $w$ as before (eqs. 37 to 39). The equations $\nabla_\mu(\rho u^\mu) = 0$, $\nabla_\mu T^\mu_\nu = 0$ and $\nabla_\mu(^*F)^{\nu\mu} = 0$, with $T^\mu_\nu = wu^\mu u_\nu - b^\mu b_\nu + (p_g + p_m)\delta^\mu_\nu$ and $(^*F)^{\mu\nu} = b^\mu u^\nu - b^\nu u^\mu$, are solved as $\partial_t(\sqrt{-g}\rho u^0) + \partial_j(\sqrt{-g}\rho u^j) = 0$, $\partial_t(\sqrt{-g}T^0_\mu) + \partial_j(\sqrt{-g}T^j_\mu) = \tfrac{1}{2}\sqrt{-g}(\partial_\mu g_{\alpha\beta})T^{\alpha\beta}$ and $\partial_t(\sqrt{-g}B^i) + \partial_j(\sqrt{-g}(^*F)^{ij}) = 0$, where $g$ is the determinant of the metric (eqs. 40 to 42). The conserved variables are $\rho u^0$, $T^0_\mu$ and $B^i$, discretised again in the same way, with volumes, areas and lengths that include $\sqrt{-g}$. The source term of eq. 42b, which also arises in the Newtonian momentum equation in non-Cartesian coordinates, vanishes for ignorable coordinates when the free index is lowered ([Gammie, McKinney and Tóth](#gammie-et-al-2003) 2003), so that the energy and the angular momentum about $z$ are often conserved to machine precision.

#### 2.14.2 Primitive variables, reconstruction and corrections

The inversion of the conserved variables lies at the heart of a relativistic scheme, since no algebraic expression gives $\mathbf{W}(\mathbf{U})$ ([Beckwith and Stone](#beckwith-stone-2011) 2011, §3.1). Of the methods that [Noble et al.](#noble-et-al-2006) (2006) examine, Athena uses 1DW, implemented as by [Mignone and McKinney](#mignone-mckinney-2007) (2007) but with the total energy $E$ as conserved variable instead of $E - D$, for simplicity and for compatibility with static mesh refinement. It assumes an ideal gas, though other equations of state, such as the Synge gas of [Mignone and McKinney](#mignone-mckinney-2007) (2007), could be added. The primitive variables follow from the root in $Q = \rho h\Gamma^2$ of $f(Q) = Q - P_g + \tfrac{1}{2}(1 + |\mathbf{V}|^2)|\mathbf{B}|^2 - S^2/(2Q^2) - E$, the definition of $E$ with $S = M_iB^i$, where $|\mathbf{V}|^2 = [|\mathbf{M}|^2 + (S^2/Q^2)(2Q + |\mathbf{B}|^2)]/(Q + |\mathbf{B}|^2)^2$, $\Gamma = (1 - |\mathbf{V}|^2)^{-1/2}$ and $P_g = [(\gamma - 1)/\gamma](Q - D\Gamma)(1 - |\mathbf{V}|^2)$ (eqs. 31, 32). Newton–Raphson iterations use $df/dQ = 1 - dP_g/dQ + \tfrac{1}{2}|\mathbf{B}|^2d|\mathbf{V}|^2/dQ + S^2/Q^3$, with both derivatives in closed form (eqs. 33, 34). They start from the positive solution of the quadratic $|\mathbf{M}|^2 - Q^2 + (2Q + |\mathbf{B}|^2)(2Q + |\mathbf{B}|^2 - 2E) = 0$, which guarantees a positive initial pressure and spares storing the primitives of the previous step, a simplification notably for mesh refinement (eq. 35). Once $f(Q)$ vanishes to the tolerance, typically $10^{-10}$, the velocity is $\mathbf{V} = (\mathbf{M} + S\mathbf{B}/Q)/(Q + |\mathbf{B}|^2)$ (eq. 36).

The states at the faces come from the PLM of [Stone and Gardiner](#stone-gardiner-2009) (2009, §4.2), limited in the primitive rather than the characteristic variables, and with the four-velocity in place of the three-velocity, whose values at the faces follow from the reconstructed four-velocity (§3.2). This prevents unphysical states with $|\mathbf{V}|^2 > 1$, which matters for strongly relativistic shocks such as the colliding shocks with $\Gamma = 30$ of [Mignone and Bodo](#mignone-bodo-2006) (2006), and where such a state occurs nonetheless the reconstruction falls back to first order. PPM ([Colella and Woodward](#colella-woodward-1984) 1984) could raise the spatial order to three, though raising the order of reconstruction does not always improve the accuracy most ([Stone et al.](#stone-et-al-2008) 2008).

A conserved state without a physical primitive counterpart arises from non-real fluxes of the Riemann solver, an inversion that does not converge, a negative density or pressure, or a superluminal velocity (§3.4). Non-real fluxes can be replaced by more diffusive ones. For the other failures, a first-order update can add the dissipation of the Riemann solver in the affected cells while keeping conservation ([Lemaster and Stone](#lemaster-stone-2009) 2009), the pressure can come from the entropy, which keeps it positive at the expense of conservation ([Noble, Krolik and Hawley](#noble-et-al-2009) 2009), or density and pressure can be set to floors with $|\mathbf{V}|^2$ estimated below one ([Mignone et al.](#mignone-et-al-2007) 2007). Athena applies all three in turn. In the most demanding application, the jet at high resolution with HLLD, they were needed about once in $10^9$, $10^{10}$ and $10^{11}$ updates.

HLLC and HLLD can return non-real fluxes, which the solver detects and replaces by those of HLLE, real for any physical left and right states (§3.4.1). The primary fix of unphysical states, the first-order flux correction, reverts the affected cell to an update of first order, conservatively, as in the supersonic turbulence of [Lemaster and Stone](#lemaster-stone-2009) (2009), and cures most failures of the inversion (§3.4.2). With the differences between the fluxes of the corrector of VL+CT and the first-order fluxes of its predictor on the faces of the cell (eq. 44), it updates the cell with the first-order fluxes and corrects its six neighbours on the shared faces (eq. 45). The differences of the electric fields on the edges of the cell likewise correct its face fields and the faces of neighbours that share those edges, after which the centred fields of all corrected cells are computed anew (eqs. 46 to 49). **[GK]** With the differences defined as second-order minus first-order values (eqs. 44, 46), replacing the fluxes and electric fields gives the corrections of the neighbouring cells and of eq. 47, whereas eqs. 45, 48 and 49 carry the opposite signs, which would break conservation and the solenoidal constraint.

Where the inversion does not converge to a physical state, the plasma may be treated as locally ideal, without shocks or dissipation, so that the conservation of energy becomes that of entropy, $\nabla_\mu(\rho su^\mu) = 0$, as in [Del Zanna et al.](#del-zanna-et-al-2007) (2007), for the entropy $s = P_g/\rho^\gamma$ of the ideal gas (§3.4.3, eq. 50). The conserved entropy $\mathcal{S} = \rho s\Gamma$ is integrated like $D$, with the flux $\rho s\Gamma V^x$, and the inversion then finds the root of $f(Q) = DP_g/\rho^\gamma - \mathcal{S}$, using $d\rho/dQ = -(D\Gamma/2)\,d|\mathbf{V}|^2/dQ$ (eqs. 51, 52). This keeps $P_g$ positive, but $|\mathbf{V}|^2 > 1$ or a failure to converge remains possible. In that still rarer case density and pressure are set to floors, $|\mathbf{V}|^2$ starts from $1 - \eta$ with typically $\eta = 10^{-8}$, and eq. 32 is solved for $|\mathbf{V}|^2$ with $Q$ from the floors and the current $|\mathbf{V}|^2$ (§3.4.4, eq. 53; [Mignone et al.](#mignone-et-al-2007) 2007). **[GK]** The derivative in eq. 51 is $(D/\rho^\gamma)\,dP_g/dQ - \gamma DP_g\rho^{-\gamma-1}\,d\rho/dQ$; the paper's second term lacks the factor $D$ and has the power $\rho^{\gamma+1}$.

Athena++ reconstructs only primitive variables in relativity, which avoids the cost of characteristic reconstruction and the failures of inversion that it risks ([Stone et al.](#stone-et-al-2020) 2020, §4.2.1). Its primitive velocities, $u^i$ in special and $u^{i'}$ in general relativity, give a unique subluminal state for any finite real values, which $v^i$ or the coordinate components $u^i$ would not. The code of [White, Stone and Gammie](#white-et-al-2016) (2016) first used three-velocities in special relativity, and the spatial components of the four-velocity made it more robust.

[White, Stone and Gammie](#white-et-al-2016) (2016, §3.1) base the inversion largely on 1DW. The field at the volume centre of a cell is interpolated linearly between the faces, $B^1_i = (1 - \lambda)B^1_{i-1/2} + \lambda B^1_{i+1/2}$ with $\lambda = (x^1_i - x^1_{i-1/2})/(x^1_{i+1/2} - x^1_{i-1/2})$ (eq. 16). In general relativity the primitive variables are $\rho$, $p_{\mathrm{gas}}$ and $\tilde{u}^i$, and the conserved ones $\rho u^0$ and $T^0_\mu$, with the velocities $\tilde{u}^\mu = (\delta^\mu_\nu + n^\mu n_\nu)u^\nu$ of [Noble et al.](#noble-et-al-2006) (2006), projected orthogonally to the unit normal $n_\mu = -\alpha\delta^0_\mu$ of the surfaces of constant $x^0$. These describe subluminal motion for any values, with $\tilde{u}^0 = 0$ and the normal-frame Lorentz factor $\gamma = (1 + g_{ij}\tilde{u}^i\tilde{u}^j)^{1/2}$, and Newton–Raphson solves for $W = \gamma^2\rho h$, for hydrodynamics with the field set to zero. In special relativity the primitive variables are $\rho$, $p_{\mathrm{gas}}$ and $v^i$ and the conserved ones $D = \gamma\rho$, $E$ and $M^i$, the components $T^{0\mu}$; MHD follows [Mignone and McKinney](#mignone-mckinney-2007) (2007), but iterates on the residual of $E$ as a function of $W$ rather than of $E - D$ as a function of $W - D$. For hydrodynamics the speed solves a quartic instead ([Schneider et al.](#schneider-et-al-1993) 1993), $a_4|v|^4 + a_3|v|^3 + a_2|v|^2 + a_1|v| + a_0 = 0$ with $a_4 = (\Gamma - 1)^2(D^2 + M^2)$, $a_3 = -2\Gamma(\Gamma - 1)|M|E$, $a_2 = \Gamma^2E^2 + 2(\Gamma - 1)M^2 - (\Gamma - 1)^2D^2$, $a_1 = -2\Gamma|M|E$ and $a_0 = M^2$, whose root gives $\rho = D(1 - |v|^2)^{1/2}$, $v^i = |v|M^i/|M|$ and $p_{\mathrm{gas}} = (\Gamma - 1)(E - \eta_{ij}M^iv^j - \rho)$ (eqs. 17 to 19). The recovered variables must satisfy $\rho > \rho_{\min}$ and $p_{\mathrm{gas}} > p_{\mathrm{gas}}^{\min}$, set by the problem, and $1 \le \gamma < \gamma_{\max}$, often 100. Values outside these ranges move to the nearest admissible ones, a failed iteration sets the floors, and the conserved variables of the cell are then recomputed.

The inversion is expensive, with iterative solvers that vectorise poorly, and prone to failure where a conserved state has no subluminal primitives ([Stone et al.](#stone-et-al-2020) 2020, §4.2.3). [Noble et al.](#noble-et-al-2006) (2006) catalogue six root-finding procedures, four in one dimension, one in two and one in five, solving for a variable like the enthalpy, one like the velocity, or both, some only for particular equations of state, and modern codes differ in their choice: RAMSES, for instance, uses a modified method of [Mignone and McKinney](#mignone-mckinney-2007) (2007) and PLUTO that of [Mignone and Bodo](#mignone-bodo-2006) (2006). Early versions of Athena++ used the method of [White, Stone and Gammie](#white-et-al-2016) (2016), but the adaptation of [Newman and Hamlin](#newman-hamlin-2014) (2014), a root-find in one dimension that finds a solution whenever one exists, proved more robust. Robustness also relies on floors on $\rho$ and $p_g$, optionally depending on position, and on ceilings on $\gamma$, on $\beta^{-1} = p_m/p_g$ and on $\sigma = 2p_m/\rho$, the last two acting as further floors on $\rho$ and $p_g$ without altering the field. A first-order flux correction after [Lemaster and Stone](#lemaster-stone-2009) (2009) was being explored.

#### 2.14.3 The relativistic equations of state in the code

The option `-s` of `configure.py` switches on special and `-g` general relativity, which includes the former, and both refuse an isothermal or general EOS. They select the file `adiabatic_hydro_sr.cpp`, `adiabatic_mhd_sr.cpp`, `adiabatic_hydro_gr.cpp` or `adiabatic_mhd_gr.cpp` of `src/eos/` and relativistic Riemann solvers. All four files take the spatial components of the four-velocity as primitive velocities, $u^i$ in special and the normal-frame $u^{i'}$ in general relativity. `EquationOfState::ConservedToPrimitive` first raises the conserved density to `dfloor` and the energy to `dfloor` plus `pfloor`$/(\Gamma - 1)$, plus $B^2/2$ in MHD, and limits $M^2$ to $(1 - 10^{-12})E^2$ (`src/eos/adiabatic_hydro_sr.cpp`, `src/eos/adiabatic_mhd_sr.cpp`). The inversion then follows [Newman and Hamlin](#newman-hamlin-2014) (2014), iterating on the pressure from its previous value (`ConservedToPrimitiveNormal`). Each iterate sets $a = E + p_g$, or $E + p_g + B^2/2$ in MHD, finds $v^2 = M^2/a^2$, or in MHD the velocity from a root of a cubic, and gives the enthalpy $w$, $\rho = D/\gamma$ and the new $p_g = [(\Gamma - 1)/\Gamma](w - \rho)$. Every third iterate is an Aitken extrapolation, and the iteration stops at a change below $10^{-12}$ or fails after 15 iterates.
