Where the density or pressure lie below their floors, which in MHD the parameters `sigma_max` and `beta_min` of the block `<hydro>` raise to $2p_m/\sigma_{\max}$ and $\beta_{\min}p_m$, the deficit is added to $D$ and $E$ and the inversion repeated. The velocity is then limited to $\gamma \le$ `gamma_max`, 1000 by default, and the floors applied again in the fluid frame. A cell whose inversion fails takes the floors at rest, and any changed cell has its conserved variables recomputed. In general relativity the conserved variables are first transformed to the frame of the normal observer, with the projected momentum $Q_\mu = -n_\nu T^\nu_\mu$ and the field $\alpha B^i$ after [Noble et al.](#noble-et-al-2006) (2006), and the inversion uses $g_{ij}$ in place of $\eta_{ij}$ (`src/eos/adiabatic_hydro_gr.cpp`, `src/eos/adiabatic_mhd_gr.cpp`). There the parameters `rho_min`, `rho_pow`, `pgas_min` and `pgas_pow` add floors that vary as powers of the first coordinate, such as $\rho_{\min}r^{\rho_{\mathrm{pow}}}$, and the conserved variables are $\rho u^0$ and $T^0_\mu$, the factor $\sqrt{-g}$ residing in the volumes and areas of the coordinates. `EquationOfState::ApplyPrimitiveFloors` applies `dfloor` and `pfloor` to the reconstructed states, and the files provide the relativistic sound and fast magnetosonic speeds for the Riemann solvers. The time step takes the speed of light, 1, as the largest signal speed in special relativity, and the coordinate speed of light in general relativity (`src/hydro/new_blockdt.cpp`).

#### 2.14.4 Relativistic Riemann solvers

A Riemann solver gives the fluxes from the two states that the reconstruction yields on either side of a face ([Beckwith and Stone](#beckwith-stone-2011) 2011, §3.3, eq. 37). Athena has three solvers of the HLL family, which need the outermost speeds $\lambda_L$ and $\lambda_R$ of the Riemann fan, those of the fast magnetosonic waves. Accurate values are roots of the quartic $\rho h(1 - c_s^2)\Gamma^4(\lambda - V^x)^4 - (1 - \lambda^2)[(|b|^2 + \rho hc_s^2)\Gamma^2(\lambda - V^x)^2 - c_s^2(b^x - \lambda b^t)^2] = 0$, with the sound speed $c_s^2 = \gamma P_g/(\rho h)$, found numerically as by [Mignone and Bodo](#mignone-bodo-2006) (2006) (eq. 38). The smallest and largest roots of both states give $\lambda_L = \min[\lambda^-(\mathbf{U}_L), \lambda^-(\mathbf{U}_R)]$ and $\lambda_R = \max[\lambda^+(\mathbf{U}_L), \lambda^+(\mathbf{U}_R)]$, after [Davis](#davis-1988) (1988) (eq. 39). These accurate speeds make the code much more robust than quadratic approximations such as that of [Gammie, McKinney and Tóth](#gammie-et-al-2003) (2003). HLLE ([Harten, Lax and van Leer](#harten-et-al-1983) 1983) takes the states and fluxes of 2.5.2 in the three regions of its fan (eqs. 40 to 43), and with $|\lambda_L| = |\lambda_R| = 1$ it becomes the flux of Lax and Friedrichs, with the largest dissipation, which the speed of light sets. **[GK]** Eq. 38 prints $(\lambda^4 - V^x)$ in its first term; only $(\lambda - V^x)^4$ reduces the speeds for $\mathbf{B} = 0$ to the relativistic sound speeds.

HLLE diffuses contact and rotational discontinuities even in a fluid at rest (§3.3.2). The HLLC of [Mignone and Bodo](#mignone-bodo-2006) (2006) restores the contact by the jump conditions across the outer waves and an intermediate contact, whose speed solves a quadratic, and Athena implements it after the public code PLUTO ([Mignone et al.](#mignone-et-al-2007) 2007). It costs little more than HLLE and gives far more accurate fluxes, but the tests of 2.14.6 show pathologies from the separate treatment of $B^x = 0$ and $B^x \neq 0$, and it is singular as $B^x \to 0$ with $B^z \neq 0$ or $V^z \neq 0$, that is in truly three-dimensional flows. The HLLD of [Mignone, Ugliano and Bodo](#mignone-et-al-2009) (2009), after [Miyoshi and Kusano](#miyoshi-kusano-2005) (2005), restores the rotational discontinuities as well as the contact (§3.3.3). It matches the jump conditions across the fast and the Alfvén waves across the contact, where, unlike in Newtonian MHD, relativistic aberration allows jumps of the normal velocity in the intermediate states. Matching the normal velocity across the contact leaves one nonlinear equation in the total pressure, solved to about $10^{-7}$. HLLD, also implemented after PLUTO, removes the singularity of HLLC and suits truly multidimensional problems better.

Athena++ has relativistic HLLE for hydrodynamics and MHD, HLLC for hydrodynamics ([Mignone and Bodo](#mignone-bodo-2005) 2005) and HLLD for MHD ([Mignone, Ugliano and Bodo](#mignone-et-al-2009) 2009), the last two designed for special relativity but usable in general relativity through the local transformations of frame of [White, Stone and Gammie](#white-et-al-2016) (2016) ([Stone et al.](#stone-et-al-2020) 2020, §4.2.2). Rather than solve the Riemann problem in general coordinates, these take the two states of a face to a locally Minkowski frame, where solvers of special relativity apply, as [Antón et al.](#anton-et-al-2006) (2006) and [Pons et al.](#pons-et-al-1998) (1998) suggested ([White, Stone and Gammie](#white-et-al-2016) 2016, §3.3). The scalars $\rho$ and $p_{\mathrm{gas}}$ stay as they are, while vectors are expressed in a basis $\vec{e}_{(\hat{\mu})}$ with $\vec{e}_{(\hat{\mu})}\cdot\vec{e}_{(\hat{\nu})} = \eta_{\hat{\mu}\hat{\nu}}$. For a face of constant $x^i$ its vectors are mutually orthogonal and normalised to $\pm1$, with $\vec{e}_{(\hat{t})}$ timelike, $\vec{e}_{(\hat{t})}$ is orthogonal to the surfaces of constant $x^0$, and within such a surface the projection of $\vec{e}_{(\hat{x})}$ stands normal to the surface of constant $x^i$. The third property makes the fluxes describe evolution in $x^0$, and the fourth lets the $x$-fluxes of a solver in one dimension alone give the fluxes through the face.

The appendix builds the basis for a face of constant $x^1$ (App. A, eqs. A1 to A17). $\vec{e}_{(\hat{t})}$ is the unit normal of the foliation into surfaces of constant time, with $e^\mu_{(\hat{t})} = Ag^{0\mu}$ and $A = -(-g^{00})^{-1/2}$, and $e^\mu_{(\hat{x})} = B(g^{01}g^{0\mu} - g^{00}g^{1\mu})$ with $B = [g^{00}(g^{00}g^{11} - g^{01}g^{01})]^{-1/2}$. A vector $\vec{e}_{(\hat{x})}$ parallel to $\vec{\partial}_{(1)}$ would mix $\hat{y}$- and $\hat{z}$-fluxes into the fluxes through the face, and one normal to the surface of constant $x^1$ is orthogonal to $\vec{e}_{(\hat{t})}$ only if $g^{01}$ vanishes. The other two vectors lie in the surface of constant $x^0$ without a component along $x^1$, $e^\mu_{(\hat{z})} = C(0, 0, 0, 1)$ with $C = g_{33}^{-1/2}$ and $e^\mu_{(\hat{y})} = D(0, 0, g_{33}, -g_{23})$ with $D = [g_{33}(g_{22}g_{33} - g_{23}g_{23})]^{-1/2}$, so that the solver need return no $\hat{y}$- or $\hat{z}$-fluxes. These vectors form the matrix $M^\mu{}_{\hat{\nu}}$ that takes contravariant tensors from the orthonormal to the coordinate basis, so that $T^1{}_\mu = g_{\mu\nu}M^1{}_{\hat{\mu}}M^\nu{}_{\hat{\nu}}T^{\hat{\mu}\hat{\nu}}$ are the fluxes of four-momentum through the face, and its inverse $M^{\hat{\mu}}{}_\nu$ gives for instance $u^{\hat{\mu}} = M^{\hat{\mu}}{}_\nu u^\nu$ (eqs. 23 to 26). Faces of constant $x^2$ and $x^3$ follow by cyclic permutation.

In the orthonormal frame a face of constant $x^1$ generally moves, with the velocity $v^{\hat{x}} = g^{01}/(g^{01}g^{01} - g^{00}g^{11})^{1/2}$, which in terms of the 3+1 split is $\beta^1/(\alpha\sqrt{\gamma^{11}})$, as [Pons et al.](#pons-et-al-1998) (1998) found: a shift along the normal moves the interface (eqs. 27, 28). As the normal observer exists everywhere, the transformation works inside the ergosphere and, with coordinates that penetrate the horizon, inside the horizon, which a transformation to a stationary observer would not, though it may fail where the coordinates are singular, as on a polar axis.

HLL solvers take the left and right states and leftgoing and rightgoing signal speeds, the speeds of sound for hydrodynamics or of fast magnetosonic waves for MHD, and keep intermediate states physical for physical inputs (§3.4). LLF takes the largest linear wave speed in magnitude, the largest eigenvalue of the matrix of [Roe](#roe-1981) (1981), on both sides, cheap but diffusive for flows slower than that speed (2.14.7). HLLE lets the two speeds differ, taking the fastest leftgoing and rightgoing waves of both states ([Einfeldt](#einfeldt-1988) 1988), and gives results very close to LLF. HLLC adds the contact, with the solver of [Mignone and Bodo](#mignone-bodo-2005) (2005) for hydrodynamics, while the MHD version of [Mignone and Bodo](#mignone-bodo-2006) (2006) is forgone in favour of HLLD ([Mignone, Ugliano and Bodo](#mignone-et-al-2009) 2009). HLLD resolves the contact and the Alfvén waves at the price of nonlinear root-finding: the secant method finds the total pressure at the contact, started, for a strong longitudinal field, from the total pressure of the HLLE state, which Newton–Raphson inverts (Figure 1). Through the transformation the same solvers serve special and general relativity, with a moving interface: the fan is built as usual and its region chosen where the interface velocity of eq. 28 falls among the wave speeds. A moving interface makes $M^1{}_{\hat{t}}$ nonzero, so that the conserved state of that region is needed besides its fluxes; even the complex solvers find the intermediate states through primitive variables, from which both follow. The fluxes thus come from transforming states, speeds and interface velocity to the local frame, solving the fan, selecting the region and transforming its fluxes and conserved variables back to the coordinates.

In the code the files `hlle_rel.cpp`, `llf_rel.cpp` and `hllc_rel.cpp` of `src/hydro/rsolvers/hydro/` and `hlle_mhd_rel.cpp`, `llf_mhd_rel.cpp` and `hlld_rel.cpp` of `src/hydro/rsolvers/mhd/` implement these solvers with the transformations, which the option `-t` of `configure.py` switches on in general relativity. Without it general relativity accepts only LLF and HLLE, its default, in versions that compute the fluxes in the coordinates directly, after the routine `fluxcalc` of HARM (`hlle_rel_no_transform.cpp`, `llf_rel_no_transform.cpp`, `hlle_mhd_rel_no_transform.cpp`, `llf_mhd_rel_no_transform.cpp`). The transforming solvers take the states to the local frame with `Coordinates::PrimToLocal1` to `Coordinates::PrimToLocal3` and return the fluxes with `Coordinates::FluxToGlobal1` to `Coordinates::FluxToGlobal3`, the interface velocity being that of eq. 28 in general and zero in special relativity (`src/hydro/rsolvers/hydro/hlle_rel.cpp`). On the faces of a polar axis in general relativity every solver falls back to the non-transforming HLLE. The speeds are the extremal ones of the two states, combined as by [Davis](#davis-1988) (1988), and LLF takes the larger of their magnitudes on both sides rather than an eigenvalue of a Roe matrix (`src/hydro/rsolvers/hydro/llf_rel.cpp`). The sound speeds are exact, after [Mignone and Bodo](#mignone-bodo-2005) (2005, eq. 23) (`EquationOfState::SoundSpeedsSR`), whereas the fast magnetosonic speeds follow the routine `vchar` of HARM, with the isotropic comoving speed $c_{ms}^2 = c_s^2 + v_A^2 - c_s^2v_A^2$, $v_A^2 = b^2/(b^2 + w)$, and a quadratic for the speeds in the coordinates (`EquationOfState::FastMagnetosonicSpeedsGR`). The relativistic HLLD follows [Mignone, Ugliano and Bodo](#mignone-et-al-2009) (2009) and the solver of Athena 4.2 in its variables and constants (`src/hydro/rsolvers/mhd/hlld_rel.cpp`). It starts the total pressure from a quadratic where $(B^x)^2$ is below 0.01 of the total pressure of the HLLE state, and from that pressure otherwise, takes four secant iterations with an initial offset of $10^{-5}$ and a tolerance of $10^{-12}$, and falls back to HLLE where pressures, speeds, states or fluxes are not finite or the pressure not positive. The MHD solvers also return the electric fields at the faces and the weights for CT of `Hydro::GetWeightForCT` (2.3.5).

#### 2.14.5 General relativity

[White, Stone and Gammie](#white-et-al-2016) (2016) present a code for GRMHD within the framework of Athena++ (abstract). Unlike most codes for GRMHD it can use the less diffusive Riemann solvers HLLC and HLLD, and it keeps the field free of divergence by staggered CT suited to curvilinear coordinates. It works in any stationary spacetime in one to three dimensions, passes a range of tests, and performs and scales well.

Accretion onto black holes, collapsars as models of long gamma-ray bursts and merging neutron stars involve gravity too strong to treat without general relativity, and so need fluids evolved in curved spacetimes (§1). Finite volumes capture shocks with superior accuracy and stability, and Godunov schemes take the fluxes from Riemann problems at the faces. Godunov codes for GRMHD on stationary spacetimes include HARM, a code of Komissarov, the code of the Valencia group and ECHO, and further codes couple GRMHD to the equations of Einstein. The accuracy depends critically on the Riemann solver: exact solvers are costly, and the simplest approximate ones much more diffusive in subsonic flow. Of ten such codes, two describe solvers of Roe type, two central schemes that are not of Godunov type, and the others only LLF and HLLE. Approximate solvers that capture part of the inner fan exist for relativistic fluids ([Mignone and Bodo](#mignone-bodo-2005) 2005; [Mignone, Ugliano and Bodo](#mignone-et-al-2009) 2009), and transformations of frame bring them into a code of general relativity (2.14.4).

The solenoidal constraint can be kept by divergence cleaning, with auxiliary elliptic, parabolic or hyperbolic equations, of which only the last naturally respect causality in relativity; by evolving a staggered vector potential, which needs care with the gauge, especially with mesh refinement; by staggered CT, which [Evans and Hawley](#evans-hawley-1988) (1988) introduced in general relativity; or by flux-CT after [Tóth](#toth-2000) (2000), which smooths the fluxes to keep a discrete divergence of the centred fields zero. Among the ten codes one cleans hyperbolically, one evolves a staggered potential, two use flux-CT and six staggered CT. The code adopts CT, extending the algorithm of [Gardiner and Stone](#gardiner-stone-2005) (2005), which places only the field on faces, to arbitrary stationary coordinates (2.3.5). Athena++ is a complete rewrite of Athena ([Stone et al.](#stone-et-al-2008) 2008) whose flexible design lets Newtonian dynamics and both special and general relativity share one code, and the code is presented as the only three-dimensional finite-volume Godunov code for GRMHD known to its authors with both advanced approximate solvers and staggered CT. Units have $G = c = 1$, a factor $\sqrt{4\pi}$ is absorbed into the field, and the metric has the signature $(-, +, +, +)$.

For a perfect fluid with comoving density $\rho$, pressure $p_{\mathrm{gas}}$, enthalpy $h = 1 + [\Gamma/(\Gamma - 1)]p_{\mathrm{gas}}/\rho$ and coordinate-frame four-velocity $u^\mu$, and the coordinate-frame field $B^i$, the field four-vector has $b^0 = g_{i\mu}B^iu^\mu$ and $b^i = (B^i + b^0u^i)/u^0$, and the stress-energy tensor is $T^{\mu\nu} = (\rho h + b_\lambda b^\lambda)u^\mu u^\nu + (p_{\mathrm{gas}} + \tfrac{1}{2}b_\lambda b^\lambda)g^{\mu\nu} - b^\mu b^\nu$ (§2, eqs. 1, 2). The equations $\nabla_\mu(\rho u^\mu) = 0$, $\nabla_\mu T^{\mu\nu} = 0$ and $\nabla_\mu{}^*F^{\mu\nu} = 0$, with ${}^*F^{\mu\nu} = b^\mu u^\nu - b^\nu u^\mu$, follow [Gammie, McKinney and Tóth](#gammie-et-al-2003) (2003, eqs. 1, 3, 14). In the sign convention of the paper the dual tensor holds the magnetic field in its time row and column and the electric field in its spatial block, and in ideal MHD the electric field follows from eq. 4 (eqs. 3 to 5). With partial derivatives the equations take the flux-conservative form of 2.14.1, with the source term $\sqrt{-g}\,\Gamma^\sigma{}_{\rho\mu}T^\rho{}_\sigma$ in the equation for $T^0{}_\mu$ (eq. 6). For a stationary metric each cell has the volume $\Delta V = \int\sqrt{-g}\,dx^1dx^2dx^3$ and each face the integral of $\sqrt{-g}$ over its two coordinates as area (eqs. 7, 8). The conserved $\rho u^0$ and $T^0{}_\mu$ are volume averages weighted by $\sqrt{-g}$, updated by area averages of the fluxes $\rho u^i$ and $T^i{}_\mu$ and by the source terms $\Gamma^\sigma{}_{\rho\mu}T^\rho{}_\sigma$, evaluated from the primitives of the averaged conserved variables with the geometry of the cell centre (eqs. 9 to 12). The components of the field are area averages, as in the CT of [Evans and Hawley](#evans-hawley-1988) (1988), placed as in Figure 1 of [Stone et al.](#stone-et-al-2008) (2008); electric fields along edges of length $\int\sqrt{-g}$ along the edge update them (eqs. 13 to 15). **[GK]** For a symmetric tensor $\Gamma^\sigma{}_{\rho\mu}T^\rho{}_\sigma = \tfrac{1}{2}(\partial_\mu g_{\alpha\beta})T^{\alpha\beta}$, so that the source term equals that of 2.14.1.

In non-Cartesian coordinates the momentum equations have geometric source terms, while the continuity and induction equations never do (§3.6). With the lower index on $T^0{}_\mu$ the source term vanishes for every coordinate on which the metric does not depend ([Gammie, McKinney and Tóth](#gammie-et-al-2003) 2003), so that a stationary metric never gives a source of energy. The momentum sources contract the connection coefficients with the stress-energy of the primitives of the stage and the metric of the cell centre (eq. 11), and the sources of further physics, such as heating and cooling, can be added with them. Each cell stores five primitive and five conserved variables per half step of the van Leer integrator, and in MHD in three dimensions the face and centred components of the field and the electric fields on edges, faces and centres, in all 20 values per cell for hydrodynamics and 50 for MHD (§3.8). General relativity also needs $g_{\mu\nu}$, $g^{\mu\nu}$, $\Gamma^\sigma{}_{\mu\nu}$ and the matrices of eqs. 23 and 25 of 2.14.4. A stationary metric lets them be precomputed, and its symmetries, with faces of constant coordinates, often make them separable. In Kerr–Schild coordinates a cell between $r_\pm$, $\theta_\pm$ and $\phi_\pm$ has the volume $\Delta V = \tfrac{1}{3}(r_+ - r_-)(\cos\theta_- - \cos\theta_+)(\phi_+ - \phi_-)[f(r_-, r_+) + a^2f(\cos\theta_-, \cos\theta_+)]$ with $f(x, y) = x^2 + xy + y^2$, assembled from arrays in one dimension (eq. 34). No metric of the paper needs arrays of more than two dimensions: Kerr–Schild stores $22N_r + 32N_\theta + 9N_\phi + 20N_rN_\theta$ values on $N_r \times N_\theta \times N_\phi$ cells, against $4N_x + 4N_y + 4N_z$ for the Minkowski metric. This is little beside the 20 to 50 evolved values per cell, and much less than codes need that store every geometric factor without regard for symmetry.

The option `--coord` of `configure.py` selects with `-g` one of the files `minkowski.cpp`, `schwarzschild.cpp`, `kerr-schild.cpp` and `gr_user.cpp` of `src/coordinates/`, which general relativity requires in place of the coordinates of 2.8.1. Minkowski uses Cartesian coordinates in flat spacetime, and Schwarzschild and Kerr–Schild the spherical coordinates $(t, r, \theta, \phi)$, with the mass `m` of the block `<coord>` and, for Kerr–Schild, the spin `a`, with $|a| < M$ (`src/coordinates/schwarzschild.cpp`, `src/coordinates/kerr-schild.cpp`). These files give the class `Coordinates` the metric at the centres and faces of cells (`Coordinates::CellMetric`, `Coordinates::Face1Metric` to `Coordinates::Face3Metric`), the transformations of 2.14.4, the raising and lowering of indices (`Coordinates::RaiseVectorCell`, `Coordinates::LowerVectorCell`), and volumes, areas, lengths and widths that include $\sqrt{-g}$. `Coordinates::Initialize` precomputes the geometric factors as arrays in one dimension, for Kerr–Schild the volumes by eq. 34. `Coordinates::AddCoordTermsDivergence` adds the source terms in the form $\tfrac{1}{2}(\partial_\mu g_{\alpha\beta})T^{\alpha\beta}$ with analytic derivatives of the metric, which in Kerr–Schild coordinates change only the momenta along $r$ and $\theta$. `gr_user.cpp` serves any stationary metric that a problem generator enrols with `Mesh::EnrollUserMetric`, a function that returns $g_{\mu\nu}$, $g^{\mu\nu}$ and the derivatives of $g_{\mu\nu}$ at a point. It takes volumes and areas as $\sqrt{-g}$ at the centre times the coordinate widths and widths as $\sqrt{g_{ii}}$ times the coordinate width, and stores all geometric quantities in arrays of three dimensions (`src/coordinates/gr_user.cpp`).

#### 2.14.6 Tests of special relativistic MHD

[Beckwith and Stone](#beckwith-stone-2011) (2011, §4) test the method in several dimensions with quantitative diagnostics, all with HLLD, a Courant number of 0.4 and $\gamma = 5/3$ unless stated otherwise, and none needs an ad hoc fix. The circularly polarised Alfvén wave of [Stone and Gardiner](#stone-gardiner-2009) (2009) remains an exact solution in relativity, though inertia from kinetic and electromagnetic energy and the electric field in the momentum equation change its speed ([Del Zanna et al.](#del-zanna-et-al-2007) 2007; §4.1). It sets $B^y = A_0B^x\cos(kx)$, $B^z = A_0B^x\sin(kx)$, $V^y = -v_AA_0\cos(kx)$ and $V^z = -v_AA_0\sin(kx)$ with $k = 2\pi/L_x$ and the Alfvén speed given by $v_A^2 = (B^x)^2/[\rho h + (B^x)^2(1 + A_0^2)]\cdot\{\tfrac{1}{2}[1 + (1 - \{2A_0(B^x)^2/[\rho h + (B^x)^2(1 + A_0^2)]\}^2)^{1/2}]\}^{-1}$ (eqs. 56, 57). Placed obliquely to the grid as by [Gardiner and Stone](#gardiner-stone-2005) (2005, [2008](#gardiner-stone-2008)), with $\rho = 1$, $P_g = 1$, $A_0 = 1$, $B^x = 1$ and $L_x = 1$, it runs for one crossing of the grid, and the mean $L_1$ error against the initial state measures the result (eq. 58). HLLE and HLLD converge at second order in every number of dimensions from one to three, HLLD 1.65 times more accurate at the finest grid in three dimensions for 1.32 times the cost, the best compromise (Figure 1). HLLC fails in several dimensions for these parameters, where cells with a vanishing normal field and nonzero transverse fields and velocities meet its singularity; it passes for $B^x \le 0.1$ or a wave aligned with the grid, which shows why tests in several dimensions are needed.

Advecting a loop of field tests whether the divergence vanishes on the stencil of CT, since with $B^z = 0$ and a uniform $V^z$ the equation $\partial_tB^z = V^z(\partial_xB^x + \partial_yB^y)$ keeps $B^z$ zero ([Gardiner and Stone](#gardiner-stone-2005) 2005, [2008](#gardiner-stone-2008); §4.2, eq. 59). In relativity it also tests whether the inversion keeps $V^z$ uniform, as $V^z = M_z/(Q + |\mathbf{B}|^2)$ with $Q$ known only to the tolerance $\delta = 10^{-10}$, which gives errors $\delta V^z/V^z \sim \delta/(Q + |\mathbf{B}|^2)$ that can drive $B^z$ even with a vanishing divergence (eq. 60). A loop with $A_z = A_0(R - r)$ inside $r = R$, $A_0 = 10^{-3}$ and $R = 0.3$, in gas with $\rho = 1$ and $P_g = 3$, crosses $256 \times 128$ cells of width $7.8125 \times 10^{-3}$ with $\mathbf{V} = (0.2, 0.1, 0)/\sqrt{6}$ or $(0.2, 0.1, 0.1)/\sqrt{6}$ (eq. 61). A thousand inversions over the grid without evolution give $\delta V^z/V^z = 5 \times 10^{-14}$, and after one and two crossings $A_z$ and $P_m$ agree for both velocities, with $\delta V^z/V^z$ at most $10^{-7}$ (Figures 2, 3). The errors stay in the loop, positive at its leading and negative at its trailing edge, so that the kinetic energy along $z$ is conserved, and they scale as $1/Q$, falling to about $10^{-10}$ with $\rho = 10^3$, $P = 3 \times 10^3$ or $\Gamma^2 = 10^3$. With $V^z = 0$, $|b^z|$ stays exactly zero; with $V^z \neq 0$, $|b^z|/|b|_0$ falls with HLLD from $3.54 \times 10^{-3}$ to $3.29 \times 10^{-3}$ after two crossings, about 7 per cent, while $|b|$ falls from $7.45 \times 10^{-3}$ to $7.01 \times 10^{-3}$, and $\partial_iB^i$ stays zero to machine accuracy. HLLC loses about 16 per cent of $|b^z|$ and 15 per cent of $|b|$, so that HLLD is 3.75 times more accurate for 1.65 times the cost. A column of loops oblique to a periodic grid of $128^3$ cells, after [Gardiner and Stone](#gardiner-stone-2008) (2008), loses 14 per cent of $|b|$ with HLLC and 9 per cent with HLLD in one crossing (Figure 4).

Current sheets, without an analytic solution, test robustness in strongly magnetised gas ([Hawley and Stone](#hawley-stone-1995) 1995; §4.3). On $200 \times 200$ cells over $[0, 2]^2$, with $\rho = 1$, $P_g = \beta/2$ for $\beta$ from $10^{-1}$ to $10^{-3}$, $\mathbf{V} = [A\cos(\pi y), 0, 0]$ with $A = 0.2$, and $B^y = -1$ for $0.5 \le x \le 1.5$ and $+1$ elsewhere, the sheets at $x = 0.5$ and 1.5 reconnect at the grid scale by an instability like tearing, into islands that merge until a stable state forms (Figure 5). Reaching $t \ge 10$ robustly, HLLE manages $\beta \sim 10^{-3}$, HLLC $10^{-2}$ and HLLD $10^{-1}$, and $A \ge 0.5$ breaks every solver for $\beta < 1$. The vortex of [Orszag and Tang](#orszag-tang-1979) (1979), on $192^2$ cells over the unit square with $\rho = 25/(36\pi)$, $P_g = 5/(12\pi)$, $\mathbf{V} = [0.5\sin(2\pi y), 0.5\sin(2\pi x)]$ and $A_z = (B_0/4\pi)\cos(4\pi x) + (B_0/2\pi)\cos(2\pi y)$ with $B_0 = 1/\sqrt{4\pi}$, stays symmetric to $t = 1$ with all three solvers, HLLE being more diffusive (§4.4; Figures 6, 7). With $c = 100$, effectively non-relativistic as in [Gammie, McKinney and Tóth](#gammie-et-al-2003) (2003), it reproduces [Stone and Gardiner](#stone-gardiner-2009) (2009), and it is the first published version in ideal relativistic MHD known to the authors.

The one-dimensional shock tubes of [Komissarov](#komissarov-1999) (1999), [Balsara](#balsara-2001) (2001), [De Villiers and Hawley](#de-villiers-hawley-2003) (2003) and [Mignone and Bodo](#mignone-bodo-2006) (2006) give results comparable to the literature, but in one dimension they cannot reveal errors of the divergence, which make the normal field jump (§4.5). Rotated obliquely to a grid of $768 \times 8 \times 8$ cells over $-0.75 \le x \le 0.75$ and $0 \le y, z \le 1/64$, with cells of $1/512$ and boundaries periodic parallel to the discontinuity after [Gardiner and Stone](#gardiner-stone-2008) (2008), the shock tube of [Brio and Wu](#brio-wu-1988) (1988) with $\gamma = 2$ at $t = 0.4$ and the Riemann problem of [Balsara](#balsara-2001) (2001), which is not planar, at $t = 0.55$ match the one-dimensional solutions without spurious normal fields (Figures 8, 9). The latter has three waves on each side of a contact and fine structure from the small relative speeds of its waves.

The cylindrical blast wave of [Komissarov](#komissarov-1999) (1999) originally holds gas 3.33 $\times 10^4$ times over-pressured and $10^2$ times over-dense in a strongly magnetised medium, which needed artificial viscosity and resistivity for $B^x$ from 0.01 to 1 (§4.6). [Leismann et al.](#leismann-et-al-2005) (2005), and [Del Zanna et al.](#del-zanna-et-al-2007) (2007) after them, reduced the over-pressure to $2 \times 10^3$ and ran $B^x = 0.1$, and Athena runs both versions at moderate field without artificial viscosity or resistivity. In the version of [Leismann et al.](#leismann-et-al-2005) (2005), on $200 \times 200$ cells over $[-6, 6]^2$ at a Courant number of 0.1, as by [Noble et al.](#noble-et-al-2006) (2006), with $\gamma = 4/3$, an ambient medium with $\rho = 10^{-4}$, $P_g = 5 \times 10^{-4}$ and $B^x = 0.1$, so that $\beta = 0.1$, $v_A = 0.91$ and $\Gamma_A = 2.4$, surrounds a cylinder of radius 0.8 with $\rho = 10^{-2}$ and $P_g = 1$. At $t = 4$ the blast is symmetric, with slices as accurate as the fifth-order results that [Del Zanna et al.](#del-zanna-et-al-2007) (2007) obtained; a field at $45^\circ$ to the grid changes the largest Lorentz factor along the field from 4.0 to 5.0, from the differing diffusion of the solver (Figures 10, 11). Stronger fields are hard. [Del Zanna et al.](#del-zanna-et-al-2007) (2007) blame errors of reconstruction and imbalances of energy near the speed of light, and [Komissarov](#komissarov-1999) (1999) and [Mignone and Bodo](#mignone-bodo-2006) (2006) break the conservation of energy, the latter also limiting shocks. A new variant with $P_g = 5 \times 10^{-3}$ outside and 1 inside, and otherwise the parameters of [Komissarov](#komissarov-1999) (1999), reaches $B^x = 1$, with $\beta = 10^{-2}$, $v_A = 0.98$ and $\Gamma_A = 5.0$. At $t = 4$ it stays symmetric for $B^x = 0.1$, 0.5 and 1, ever more elongated along the field, and oblique fields work for the weaker two but give artefacts for the strongest with HLLD at second order, though not with HLLE (Figures 12 to 14). Beyond $\Gamma_A = 5$ the fast and Alfvén waves become degenerate and destabilise HLLD, while HLLE with reconstruction of third order evolves at least $B^x = 10^3$, with $\beta = 10^{-8}$ and $\Gamma_A \sim 5 \times 10^3$. The trouble thus lies in the blast itself: with $B^x = 1$ the version of [Leismann et al.](#leismann-et-al-2005) (2005) reaches $\Gamma \sim 4.0$ against 1.8 in the variant, at similar relative variations of $|\mathbf{B}|^2$, and shows artefacts at the grid scale that Newtonian physics removes. An unmagnetised relativistic blast fails alike beyond $\Gamma = 8$. The inversion, dominated by roundoff at high Lorentz factors and magnetisations, is therefore the cause, and evolving $E - D$ ([Mignone et al.](#mignone-et-al-2007) 2007) or the two-dimensional inversion of [Noble et al.](#noble-et-al-2006) (2006) and [Del Zanna et al.](#del-zanna-et-al-2007) (2007) might help.

The Kelvin–Helmholtz instability probes the effect of the solver, as [Mignone, Ugliano and Bodo](#mignone-et-al-2009) (2009) found an order of magnitude more transverse power with HLLD than with HLLE (§4.7). Following them and [Zhang, MacFadyen and Wang](#zhang-et-al-2009) (2009), two shear layers at $y = \pm0.5$ of thickness $a = 0.01$ separate $V^x = 0.5$ outside from $V^x = -0.5$ between them, which corresponds to a Lorentz factor of 2.29 between them, with $\rho = 1$ and $10^{-2}$ in the two flows, $P_g = 1$, $\gamma = 4/3$ and $B^x = 10^{-3}$ (eq. 62). A transverse velocity $A_0V_{\mathrm{shear}}\sin(2\pi x)$, with $A_0 = 0.1$ and $V_{\mathrm{shear}} = 0.5$, decaying from the layers as Gaussians of width $\sigma = 0.1$, and noise of 1 per cent seed the instability (eq. 63). The periodic domain $[-0.5, 0.5] \times [-1, 1]$ has $128 \times 256$, $256 \times 512$ or $512 \times 1024$ cells. With HLLC and HLLD the transverse four-velocity $\langle|U^y|^2\rangle$ grows at the same, converged rate until $t = 3$, converged at $256 \times 512$ cells, where 2 cells resolve the layer, while with HLLE the rate rises with resolution towards theirs (Figure 15). The contact, which both include, matters given the jump of density across the layers. At $t = 3$ HLLE lacks, even on $512 \times 1024$ and up to $4096 \times 8192$ cells, a secondary vortex that HLLC and HLLD show on $128 \times 256$ (Figure 16). The spectra of eqs. 64 and 65 of density and Lorentz factor agree for HLLC and HLLD: $k|\rho(k)|^2$ falls as $k^{-1}$ up to $k = 30$ and as $k^{-8/3}$ beyond, and $k|\Gamma(k)|^2$ as $k^{-7/6}$ for $5 < k < 100$, the latter also with HLLE, which has less density power for $10 \le k \le 100$ (Figure 17). The spectrum of magnetic pressure of HLLE matches those of HLLC and HLLD when $k$ is stretched by 1.6 and 1.62. In total power (eq. 66; Figure 18), HLLE at the finest grid equals the other two in density and Lorentz factor but has 14 times less magnetic pressure than HLLD, less even than HLLD at the coarsest grid, while HLLC has at most 1.7 times less than HLLD. **[GK]** Eq. 63 centres the Gaussian at $y = -0.5$ also for $y > 0$, where the perturbation of the upper layer requires $y - 0.5$, as in eq. 62.

[Stone et al.](#stone-et-al-2020) (2020, §4.3) test the relativistic module with mesh refinement in special and general relativity. Thin features of relativistic Riemann problems are hard to resolve on uniform grids, so a strong shock tube after [Mignone et al.](#mignone-et-al-2012a) (2012a, §6.1), with $(\rho, p_g, B^y, B^z) = (1, 1000, 7, 7)$ for $x < 0.5$ and $(1, 0.1, 0.7, 0.7)$ for $x > 0.5$, $B^x = 10$, $\mathbf{v} = 0$ and $\Gamma = 5/3$, runs with AMR (§4.3.1, eq. 43). The root grid of 400 cells in blocks of 16 refines up to six levels where the curvature $\max|q_{i-1} - 2q_i + q_{i+1}|/q_i$ of $q = [(B^y)^2 + (B^z)^2]/(\gamma\rho)$ exceeds $10^{-3}$, and derefines below $10^{-4}$, with VL2, PLM, HLLD and a Courant number of 0.6 (eqs. 44, 45). At $t = 0.4$ refinement follows the thin shell moving right and the steep parts of the rarefactions, comparing well with Figure 23 of [Mignone et al.](#mignone-et-al-2012a) (2012a) (Figure 37). On 4 cores of a Skylake node it takes 22.8 core-seconds against 520 for a uniform grid of 25,600 cells, a speed-up of 23, slightly less than PLUTO reports, partly because Athena++ runs the uniform grid much faster.

The magnetised Kelvin–Helmholtz problem of [Mignone et al.](#mignone-et-al-2012a) (2012a) runs in two dimensions with $\rho = 1$, $p_g = 20$, $v^z = 0$, $B^x = \sqrt{2/5}$, $B^y = 0$, $B^z = 10\sqrt{2/5}$, $v^x = \tfrac{1}{4}\tanh(100y)$ and $v^y = \tfrac{1}{400}\sin(2\pi x)\exp(-100y^2)$, with $\Gamma = 4/3$ instead of the EOS of Taub and Mathews used there, an initial enthalpy $\rho h$ of 81 against about 80.02 (§4.3.2, eq. 46). The domain $0 \le x \le 1$, $-0.25 \le y \le 0.25$ is periodic in $x$ and outflowing in $y$, with a root grid of $64 \times 32$ cells in blocks of $16^2$ and up to five levels, refined where the summed curvatures of the conserved energy along $x$ and $y$ exceed $10^{-2}$ and derefined below $10^{-3}$ (eq. 47). With VL2, PPM, a Courant number of 0.4 and, separately, HLLE and HLLD, it runs to $t = 5$, the refined regions following the small-scale structures (Figure 38). The results differ from [Mignone et al.](#mignone-et-al-2012a) (2012a, Figure 31), partly through the EOS, but the outcome depends strongly on the algorithm: the two solvers shift even the largest rolls, and many short features arise at the boundaries between levels, as a uniform grid of $4096 \times 2048$ cells, twice the finest effective resolution, gives a smooth vortex (Figure 39). The run starts with 176 blocks after initial refinement, refines 1174 and creates 177 by derefinement with HLLD, and takes 32.2 core-hours on two KNL nodes against 251 for a uniform grid of $2048 \times 1024$ cells, a speed-up of 7.8.

A strongly magnetised blast in two dimensions, with the field oblique to a Cartesian grid over $[-6, 6]^2$, has $\mathbf{v} = 0$, $B^x = B^y = 1/\sqrt{20}$, $B^z = 0$ and $\Gamma = 4/3$, with $\rho = 10^{-2}$ within $r = 0.8$, $10^{-4}$ beyond $r = 1$ and linear in between, and $p_g$ from 1 to $5 \times 10^{-3}$ (§4.3.3). With PLM, HLLD and a Courant number of 0.25 it runs to $t = 4$ on a uniform grid of $1536^2$ cells and on a root grid of $48^2$ with up to five levels, both in blocks of $16^2$, refined by the curvature of eq. 47 in $D$ with thresholds 0.025 and 0.005. Refinement follows the shocks guided by the field, the conserved energy agrees with the uniform run to better than 1 per cent over most of the volume, and AMR takes 6.18 core-hours against 24.7, a speed-up of 4.0 (Figure 40). The spherical version in three dimensions, with the parameters of [Mignone et al.](#mignone-et-al-2012a) (2012a), $B^x = B^y = 1/\sqrt{200}$ over $[-6, 6]^3$, runs on $768^3$ cells or a root grid of $48^3$ with up to four levels, thresholds 0.15 and 0.03, and agrees to better than 1 per cent nearly everywhere, its errors lying mostly in the evacuated interior near the floors; AMR takes 316 core-hours against 2720 on 16 KNL nodes, a speed-up of 8.6 (Figure 41).

In the code, `gr_shock_tube` sets left and right states of density, pressure, three-velocity and field across `xshock` along the direction `shock_dir`, in special relativity or in flat spacetime in coordinates of general relativity, and a passive scalar of 0 and 1 on the two sides (`src/pgen/gr_shock_tube.cpp`; 2.12). Its input files run the four hydrodynamic problems of [Mignone and Bodo](#mignone-bodo-2005) (2005), `inputs/hydro_sr/athinput.mb_1` to `inputs/hydro_sr/athinput.mb_4`, and the four MHD problems of [Mignone, Ugliano and Bodo](#mignone-et-al-2009) (2009), `inputs/mhd_sr/athinput.mub_1` to `inputs/mhd_sr/athinput.mub_4`, over $[-0.5, 0.5]$ with the interface at 0 (`inputs/mhd_sr/athinput.mub_1`). The hydrodynamic ones run to $t = 0.4$ on 400 cells: a flow at 0.9 against gas at rest with ten times its pressure for $\Gamma = 4/3$, flows moving apart at $-0.6$ and 0.5, a blast with a pressure ratio of $2 \times 10^7$ and one of $10^5$, the last three for $\Gamma = 5/3$. The MHD ones are a relativistic version of the problem of [Brio and Wu](#brio-wu-1988) with $\Gamma = 2$ to $t = 0.4$ on 400 cells, a problem with velocities and fields along all three axes to $t = 0.55$ on 800 cells, the collision of flows at $\pm0.999$ with $B^x = 10$ to $t = 0.4$ on 400 cells, and a problem with transverse velocities on one side and strong transverse fields on both to $t = 0.5$ on 800 cells. The regression tests `hydro_shocks_hllc`, `hydro_shocks_hlle` and `hydro_shocks_llf` run the four hydrodynamic problems and require relative $L_1$ differences of density, energy and momentum from reference data of HLLC of at most 0.01 to 0.02, and 0.5 for the density of the fourth (`tst/regression/scripts/tests/sr/`). `mhd_shocks_hlld`, `mhd_shocks_hlle` and `mhd_shocks_llf` run the MHD problems 1, 2 and 4 against reference data of HLLD, with tolerances for the eight variables of up to 0.04 with HLLD and 0.08 with the others, and zero for the components that must not change.

`gr_linear_wave` sets one of the seven linear eigenmodes of relativistic MHD, or five of hydrodynamics, chosen by `wave_flag`, with amplitude `amp` on a uniform state, travelling along $x$ with one wavelength over the domain, and with `compute_error` writes the $L_1$ errors at the end to a file (`src/pgen/gr_linear_wave.cpp`). `hydro_convergence` and `mhd_convergence` run every mode with $A = 10^{-6}$, $\Gamma = 4/3$, $\rho = 4$, $p_g = 1$, $\mathbf{v} = (0.1, 0.3, -0.05)$ and, in MHD, $\mathbf{B} = (2.5, 1.8, -1.2)$, for one crossing of the domain on 64 and 512 cells with HLLC or HLLD, and require convergence at order 1.8 or better. `sr_mhd_linwave` runs the seven MHD modes in three dimensions on $16 \times 8 \times 8$ and $32 \times 16 \times 16$ cells with $\rho = 1$, $p_g = 0.5$, $\mathbf{v} = (0.1, 0.15, 0.05)$, $\mathbf{B} = (1, 2/3, 1/3)$, $\Gamma = 4/3$ and a Courant number of 0.3. It requires errors on the finer grid below $2 \times 10^{-8}$ to $4 \times 10^{-8}$, depending on the mode, and a ratio of the errors of the two grids below 0.4. `gr_blast` sets a sphere of radius `radius` with density `rho_inner` and pressure `pgas_inner`, optionally repeated on a lattice of centres, in gas of `rho_outer` and `pgas_outer` at rest with a uniform field, in flat spacetime in any coordinates (`src/pgen/gr_blast.cpp`). Its input file runs an unmagnetised blast of radius 0.1, with pressures 2.5 and 0.1 at unit density, on $120 \times 180$ periodic cells over $[-0.3, 0.3] \times [-0.45, 0.45]$ to $t = 1$, in Minkowski coordinates of general relativity (`inputs/mhd_gr/athinput.blast`). It changes density and pressure abruptly at `radius`. The problem generator `blast` of 2.16, valid in special relativity as well, can set the blast of [Stone et al.](#stone-et-al-2020) (2020, §4.3.3) with a ramp of width `ramp` inside `radius`, but there density and pressure vary exponentially with radius, their logarithms interpolated linearly, where the paper describes a linear variation (`src/pgen/blast.cpp`). `kh` with `iprob` set to 3 sets the shear layers of [Beckwith and Stone](#beckwith-stone-2011) (2011), centred at $|y| = 0.5$, as conserved variables of Newtonian form (`src/pgen/kh.cpp`; 2.16).

#### 2.14.7 Tests of general relativistic MHD

As special and general relativity share most of the machinery, several tests of [White, Stone and Gammie](#white-et-al-2016) (2016, §4) are special relativistic, some run in both settings, and the rest need general relativity. Linear waves are the eigenvectors $\delta\mathbf{W}$ of the matrix $A$ of $\partial_t\mathbf{W} + A\partial_x\mathbf{W} = 0$ for a constant background (§4.1, eq. 35). A wave evolves for exactly one period, and the error is the RMS over the primitive variables of the $L_1$ norms of the differences between initial and final states (eq. 36). For hydrodynamics, with the eigenvectors of [Falle and Komissarov](#falle-komissarov-1996) (1996, App. A), the background $\rho = 4$, $p_{\mathrm{gas}} = 1$, $\mathbf{v} = (0.1, 0.3, -0.05)$ and $\Gamma = 4/3$ gives wave speeds of about $-0.30$, 0.1 and 0.47. The entropy wave and the rightgoing sound wave converge at second order with HLLE and HLLC, as reconstruction and integrator are of second order, and HLLE errs 1.7 times more for the entropy wave, as within the fan the sound speeds alone average over too wide a domain of dependence (Figure 3). For MHD the field $\mathbf{B} = (2.5, 1.8, -1.2)$ separates all wave speeds, about $-0.71$, $-0.55$ and $-0.21$ for the leftgoing waves, 0.1 for the entropy wave, and 0.35, 0.54 and finally 0.81, with the eigenvectors of [Antón et al.](#anton-et-al-2010) (2010, eqs. 46, 65, 71). The four rightmost waves converge at second order, HLLD erring less than HLLE by factors of 3.1 for the entropy wave, 1.7 for the slow wave and 1.4 for the Alfvén wave (Figure 4); LLF matches HLLE in both cases.

The same tests exercise general relativity in coordinates tilted against those of Minkowski, $t' = (t + ax)/\sqrt{1 + a^2}$, $x' = (x - at)/\sqrt{1 + a^2}$, $y' = y$ and $z' = z$ with $|a| < 1$, whose time axis is that of an observer moving at $a$ and whose surfaces of constant time are those of an observer moving at $-a$ (eqs. 37). Their connection and source terms vanish, but the metric couples $t'$ and $x'$, so that the transformation matrices of 2.14.4 have off-diagonal terms and the interface moves. The perturbation starts over one wavelength on $t' = 0$ and runs to $t' = (1 + a\lambda)/|\lambda - a|$, one crossing at the Minkowski speed $\lambda$, with $a = 0.1$, or 0.05 for entropy waves, which HLLC and HLLD would capture exactly for $a = \lambda$. HLLC beats HLLE by 2.8 for the entropy wave of hydrodynamics, and HLLD beats HLLE by 4.8 for the entropy wave, 1.8 for the slow wave and 1.4 for the Alfvén wave, larger margins than in special relativity (Figures 5, 6).

Seven shock tubes of [Komissarov](#komissarov-1999) (1999), compared by [Gammie, McKinney and Tóth](#gammie-et-al-2003) (2003, Figures 4, 5), test nonlinear behaviour and discontinuities (§4.2). With HLLD on 220 cells over $[-1.1, 1.1]$, the resolution of [Gammie, McKinney and Tóth](#gammie-et-al-2003) (2003), and a Courant number of 0.8, or 0.2 for the fast shock and 0.5 for the collision, they agree with reference solutions on ten times as many cells (Figure 7). Some artefacts that Komissarov saw recur: ringing ahead of the slow shock, a false bump at the thin end of the switch-off rarefaction and an overshoot at the thin end of the rarefaction of shock tube 2. In shock tube 1 the overshoot of density at the foot of the rarefaction is absent and that at the top of the jump of velocity much smaller at the same resolution, consistent with the good capture of shocks by HLLD that [Mignone, Ugliano and Bodo](#mignone-et-al-2009) (2009) note.

A spherical blast wave tests the formulation in two dimensions: uniform density $\rho = 1$, $p_{\mathrm{gas}} = 2.5$ within a radius of 0.5 and 0.1 outside, on a periodic grid 4 wide and 6 high (§4.3). By $t = 15$ the blast has crossed itself several times, and its shocks are slow enough for Richtmyer–Meshkov fingers to grow. To test nontrivial spatial geometry it also runs in snake coordinates, $x^2 = y + a\sin kx$ with the other coordinates those of Minkowski (eqs. 38). With $\delta = ak\cos kx^1$ the metric has $g_{11} = 1 + \delta^2$, $g_{12} = -\delta$ and $g_{22} = 1$, the transformations differ from the identity through $\delta$, and a source term arises (eqs. 39, 40). On $200 \times 300$ cells at a Courant number of 0.4, with $a = 0.3$ and $k = \pi/2$, HLLE and HLLC give solutions in both coordinates that agree qualitatively, although fluxes, sources and conserved variables differ (Figure 8). Differences above 1 per cent are confined to cell-level shifts of the shocks and to the fingers, which grow from grid noise; HLLC gives sharper fingers than HLLE. With a uniform field $\mathbf{B} = (1, 1, 0)$ and HLLD the field guides the blast and suppresses the instability, again identically in both coordinates, down to a slight curvature of the boundary of the rarefaction from the wave interacting with itself (Figure 9).

Accretion after Bondi onto a black hole of Schwarzschild tests a nontrivial spacetime (§4.4). After [Hawley, Smarr and Wilson](#hawley-et-al-1984) (1984), with mass $M$, adiabatic index $\Gamma$, adiabat $K$, critical radius $r_c$ and polytropic index $n = 1/(\Gamma - 1)$, the critical velocity is $u^1_c = -[M/(2r_c)]^{1/2}$ and the critical temperature $T_c = [n/(n + 1)](u^1_c)^2/[1 - (n + 3)(u^1_c)^2]$ (eqs. 41, 42). With $C_1 = T_c^nu^1_cr_c^2$ and $C_2 = [1 + (n + 1)T_c]^2[1 - 2M/r_c + (u^1_c)^2]$, the temperature solves $[1 + (n + 1)T]^2[1 - 2M/r + C_1^2/(r^4T^{2n})] = C_2$, the smaller root inside $r_c$ and the larger outside, and then $u^1 = C_1/(r^2T^n)$, $\rho = (T/K)^n$ and $p_{\mathrm{gas}} = T\rho$ in Schwarzschild coordinates (eqs. 43 to 48). With $M = 1$, $\Gamma = 4/3$, $K = 1$ and $r_c = 8$ over $3 < r < 10$ and $\pi/4 < \theta < 3\pi/4$, optionally with a radial monopole field $B^r \propto r^{-2}$ normalised to $b_\lambda b^\lambda/\rho = 10$ at the inner radius, $\beta = 0.246$ at the critical point, the steady solution runs on $N \times N$ cells to $t = 10$ (Figure 10). The $L_1$ change of $p_{\mathrm{gas}}$ over the inner three quarters of the grid along both directions, normalised by the $L_1$ norm of the initial pressure, converges at second order with HLLC for hydrodynamics and HLLD for MHD (Figure 11). The stationary problem depends on the order of reconstruction, not of the integrator, so full spatial second order holds in curved spacetime.

The plunging region of a magnetised thin disc around a spinning black hole, after [Gammie](#gammie-1999) (1999), uses the parameters of [Gammie, McKinney and Tóth](#gammie-et-al-2003) (2003): $M = 1$, $a/M = 0.5$, $F_M = -1$ and $F_{\theta\phi} = 0.5$, with the flow matched at the innermost stable circular orbit (§4.5). It covers $2 < r < 4$, with the horizon at $r = 1.86603$ and the orbit at $r = 4.23300$, in the Kerr–Schild coordinates of [Gammie, McKinney and Tóth](#gammie-et-al-2003) (2003), whose $r$ and $\theta$ are those of Boyer and Lindquist, with $\Sigma = r^2 + a^2\cos^2\theta$ in the line element (eq. 49). Initialised to the steady solution with $p_{\mathrm{gas}} = 10^{-10}\rho$ and run to $t = 15$, the $L_1$ errors of four quantities converge at second order (Figure 12). The test uses the frame-transforming HLLE, which thus works even where the frames are strongly shifted.

In the code, `gr_bondi` sets the Bondi solution in Schwarzschild or Kerr–Schild coordinates, with the adiabat `k_adi` of the block `<hydro>`, the critical radius `r_crit` and, in MHD, `bsq_over_rho` for the monopole field, and keeps it fixed in the ghost cells at both radial boundaries (`src/pgen/gr_bondi.cpp`). It computes $C_2$ as $[1 + (n + 1)T_c]^2[1 - 3M/(2r_c)]$, the value of eq. 44 for $(u^1_c)^2 = M/(2r_c)$. Its input file runs the hydrodynamic case in Kerr–Schild coordinates with $a = 0$ on 100 cells over $3 \le r \le 20$ and one cell in $\theta$ about the equator, to $t = 100$ (`inputs/hydro_gr/athinput.bondi`). `gr_mhd_inflow` reads the steady inflow from a data file of `num_data_lines` lines, sets the pressure from `temperature` and fixes the outer boundary (`src/pgen/gr_mhd_inflow.cpp`). Its input file takes $a = 0.5$, 128 cells over $2 \le r \le 4$, a thin wedge about the equator, $p_{\mathrm{gas}} = 10^{-10}\rho$ and $t = 15$, with the data file `magnetized_inflow.dat`, which the copy lacks (`inputs/mhd_gr/athinput.magnetized_inflow`). `gr_geodesic_infall` releases gas at rest in the normal frame, with density and pressure set to the floor profiles $\rho_{\min}r^{\rho_{\mathrm{pow}}}$ and $p_{\min}r^{p_{\mathrm{pow}}}$, onto a black hole in Schwarzschild or Kerr–Schild coordinates, without a field (`src/pgen/gr_geodesic_infall.cpp`). Its input file runs $100 \times 100$ cells over $1.96 \le r \le 20$ and $\pi/6 \le \theta \le 5\pi/6$ to $t = 1$ with $\rho_{\min} = 10^{-4}$, $\rho_{\mathrm{pow}} = -1.5$, $p_{\min} = 3.3 \times 10^{-5}$ and $p_{\mathrm{pow}} = -2.5$ (`inputs/hydro_gr/athinput.geodesic`).

The tilted and snake coordinates are not in the copy, whose problem generators of special relativity transform from Minkowski coordinates only (2.14.6). The regression tests of `tst/regression/scripts/tests/gr/` run the shock tubes of 2.14.6 in general relativity with Minkowski coordinates, against the same reference data and tolerances: `hydro_shocks_hllc`, `hydro_shocks_hlle` and `hydro_shocks_llf` with transformations of frame, `hydro_shocks_hlle_no_transform` and `hydro_shocks_llf_no_transform` without, and `mhd_shocks_hlld`, `mhd_shocks_hlle` and `mhd_shocks_llf`. `compile_minkowski`, `compile_schwarzschild` and `compile_kerr-schild` only check that the code compiles for each metric.

## 3. Software architecture

Nine subjects group the software architecture. `plan.md` lists them, with the steps that write them.

[Stone et al.](#stone-et-al-2020) (2020, §1) explain three decisions that shape the framework of Athena++. First, the mesh is abstract and independent of the physics. The discretisations of the variables, such as volume averages at cell centres, area averages on faces and point values at cell centres or vertices, are built and stored on it, and AMR, boundary conditions and parallelisation by decomposing the domain are implemented for these variables without reference to any physics. New coordinates and new physics therefore work with AMR in any geometry at once, and mesh and physics can be developed apart, as in a version of the framework on Kokkos ([Edwards, Trott and Sunderland](#edwards-et-al-2014) 2014) for heterogeneous machines with GPUs. Second, the mesh is refined by blocks ([Stout et al.](#stout-et-al-1997) 1997) rather than by overlapping patches after [Berger and Oliger](#berger-oliger-1984) (1984), as in Athena, PLUTO and Enzo. With patches, a refined region is covered by several levels, on which quantities computed from the conserved variables, the temperature for instance, may differ, and source terms that depend on them, such as cooling or reaction networks, then drive the levels apart. The overlap between the domains of the processes of the Message Passing Interface (MPI) on different levels also grows complex. In Athena++ each point of the domain belongs to exactly one level, and the levels communicate only through their boundaries, which simplifies the code and improves its performance and scaling. FLASH ([Fryxell et al.](#fryxell-et-al-2000) 2000) also refines by blocks, with the library PARAMESH ([MacNeice et al.](#macneice-et-al-2000) 2000), but Athena++ uses a framework of its own, to support variables on faces, as its MHD requires, to depend on fewer libraries and to gain performance at the cost of generality. Third, the steps of an algorithm, including the sends and receives of MPI, are not executed in a fixed order but assembled into lists of tasks, each of which runs once the tasks it depends on are complete. Computation can then overlap communication, which improves scaling on very many processors, and a calculation chooses its physics by the composition of its task lists. Separate regions can even run separate lists, for example a particle-in-cell code for the collisionless corona above an accretion disc in MHD ([Miller and Stone](#miller-stone-2000) 2000). Libraries such as Legion and Charm++ offer such dynamic execution among much else, but Athena++ implements only the scheduling it needs, in a few thousand lines of its own.

### 3.2 Arrays, the mesh and its blocks

All data of the grid lie in arrays of the template class `AthenaArray`, of one to six dimensions (`src/athena_arrays.hpp`, `AthenaArray`). The data occupy one contiguous block of memory, and the operator of function call addresses them with the last index fastest, so that `A(n,k,j,i)` is the element `i + N1*(j + N2*(k + N3*n))` of an array of size $N_4 \times N_3 \times N_2 \times N_1$. Loops with `i` innermost thus run through memory in order. An array records whether it is empty, allocated, or a shallow slice of another array. Allocated memory is set to zero. Copying an array copies its data, assigning one requires the target to be allocated with the same size already, and moving one hands over its data. Arrays may be allocated after their construction (`NewAthenaArray`) and released (`DeleteAthenaArray`). `SwapAthenaArray` exchanges the data of two arrays of equal size without copying them, and `InitWithShallowSlice` and `ShallowSlice3DToPencil` make views into part of another array, such as a range of its variables or one line of cells along $x_1$, again without copying. The type `Real` of the data is `double` by default and `float` when the code is configured for single precision, and `SIMD_WIDTH` sets the width of the vector lanes from the instruction set that the compiler targets (`src/athena.hpp`). The same header declares the structures for the size of a region and the logical location of a block in the tree of refinement, the containers `FaceField` and `EdgeField` for fields on faces and edges, the enumerations of indices and directions, and the types of the functions that users may enrol (3.6).
